For the first time all week, nobody was yelling, nobody was trying to force Katie to dance or shove us into a room with foam parrot mascots, and as the warm ocean breeze blew past the stern rail, the three of us talked for two straight hours about the programs we were going to write when we got home to Virginia Beach.

### Part 4: Back on Dry Land & The Twelve-Hour Drive Up I-95

**Date:** 2001-08-25 at 08:30 America/New_York

![Buckled safely in their three-point seatbelts side by side on the gray cloth rear bench seat of their family sedan along Interstate 95 North in August 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie hold hands with their father's mylar-wrapped Quantum Basic library textbook open across Nathaniel's lap and a white paper drive-thru lunch bag on Katie's lap]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/aug_2001_i95_drive_home_backseat_quantum_basic.jpg)

#### Saturday, August 25, 2001, 8:30 AM — Back on Dry Land

"Nathaniel. Katie. I'm sorry that your first cruise was wrecked, and somehow I want to make it up to both of you," Dad said earnestly as two crew members helped us transfer out of the ship's loaner wheelchairs onto the gray cloth back bench seat of our Holt sedan at the Port Canaveral pickup curb. Resting a steady hand on each of our shoulders through the open door, Dad continued, "Here is what we will do: neither one of you wanted to go on that cruise, but your mother insisted. I want to finish school at CCHR so that I can do a better job at being a dad without having to worry about money as much."

Before Katie or I could answer him, an angry screech tore across the concrete baggage drop from behind us.

"You! You told the ship to stop my drinks!"

Looking through the open rear doorway, I saw our mother being escorted out of the customs terminal doors by two *Sunward Cruise Lines* security officers after being released from the ship's brig, clutching a folded white sheet of permanent lifetime ban paperwork in her fist. Her face was flushed dark red in the morning Florida heat as she stormed up to the rear fender of our car.

Every muscle in Dad's jaw tightened as he straightened up beside our open door and looked at her.

"If we were anywhere near Hampton Roads right now, Brenda, I would hand you twenty dollars for a bus fare and leave you standing on this curb," Dad told her, his voice as cold and flat as steel. "The only reason I am unlocking this front door is because we are seven hundred miles from Virginia. Get in the front passenger seat, and don't say a single word to the kids for the next twelve hours. The second this car pulls into our driveway in Virginia Beach, you are packing your bags and getting out of our house."

Without waiting for her to reply, Dad closed our rear door securely, walked around to the driver's side, and pulled out of Port Canaveral toward Interstate 95 North.

***

#### 12:15 PM — Exit 94 Near Savannah, Georgia: The Gas Pump, The Restrooms, & Brenda's First Angle

For the first three and a half hours heading north out of Florida across the Georgia state line, the inside of the car was dead silent except for the hum of the tires and the air conditioner. In the back seat, Katie and I sat pressed shoulder-to-shoulder with our inner hands laced together and Dad's mylar-wrapped *Quantum Basic 5.0* library textbook resting across our knees.

Just past noon, the low-fuel chime dinged on the dashboard, and Dad signaled right at Exit 94 near Savannah, pulling under the canopy of a highway travel plaza so we could get gas and use the restrooms.

That stop was when the physical reality of leaving the ship hit Katie and me all over again.

On Thursday and Friday aboard the *Sunward Tropics*, we had rolled everywhere in the Medical Center's two folding loaner wheelchairs. Those wheelchairs belonged to the ship, which meant they were still back at Port Canaveral. When Dad opened our rear door beside Pump 6 and I swung my sneakers out onto the ninety-degree Georgia concrete, my knees and calves trembled so violently that I had to grab the roof frame with both hands just to keep from sliding onto the pavement. Beside me, when Katie tried to push herself up off the bench cushion, the strength in her right forearm gave out halfway up, and she gasped softly as she sank back against the seat.

Up front, Mom didn't even glance back at our door. She slammed the front passenger door shut and marched briskly across the concrete apron toward the air-conditioned travel store restrooms on her own.

Dad saw us struggling immediately. Leaving the gas nozzle clicking in the tank, he stepped right between Katie and me, braced his left arm firmly around Katie's waist and his right hand under my elbow, and took half of our weight onto his own shoulders.

"Take your time, kids—I've got both of you," Dad murmured close to our ears as he walked us slowly, step by shuffling step, across the hot pavement and up the curb ramp to the restroom hallway. He stood waiting right between the men's and women's doors until both of us came back out, then supported us all the way back to the back seat of the Holt before topping off the tank and paying at the window.

When Mom finally strolled back out of the convenience store carrying a bottle of water and buckled herself into the front passenger seat, her furious scowl from Port Canaveral was suddenly replaced by self-pity. As Dad checked in the rearview mirror to make sure Katie and I were buckled securely into our back-seat shoulder belts, shifted into drive, and merged back onto I-95 North, Mom launched into a bitter, non-stop tirade about how "unfairly" she had been treated down in the ship's brig.

"David, honey, look—let's be reasonable now that we're off that awful boat," Mom complained, turning slightly toward him in the front seat. "Do you have any idea how terrible life in a cruise ship brig actually is, or what I just went through for sixty-two straight hours? It was completely unfair! That purser had it out for me from the moment we boarded, and those security guards treated me like a common criminal over a couple of drinks!"

In the back seat, Katie and I glanced at each other and squeezed hands as Mom kept going, oblivious to the way Dad's jaw was clenching harder with every word out of her mouth.

"They put wrist restraints on me and marched me down a steel freight elevator to the Deck 2 crew corridor below the waterline, right on top of the diesel engine room!" Mom ranted, her voice rising with indignation. "They confiscated my cabin card, my wristwatch, my belt, and even my shoes! For two and a half days I was locked inside a windowless steel box that vibrated twenty-four hours a day from the ship's engines. No porthole, no clock, no television, no pillow—just a hard vinyl mat bolted to the steel floor with a stiff Velcro blanket, an institutional stainless-steel toilet welded to the sink in the corner, and a blinding fluorescent ceiling light with a security camera staring at me that never turned off once!"

Beside me, Katie leaned her head close to my shoulder. Both of us could see the muscles in Dad's neck and shoulders stiffening against the driver's backrest. Mom thought she was winning sympathy, when in reality every single complaint she made was hardening Dad's resolve like poured concrete.

"And the food!" Mom went on, gesturing with her water bottle. "While the three of you were upstairs doing whatever you wanted, they wouldn't even let me have a soda! Every four hours the ship's doctor or some nurse slid open the metal slot in the door to take my blood pressure and hand me paper cones of lukewarm tap water and salt packets while my head was splitting open! Three times a day a guard shoved a paper plate of bland crew-galley mush through the slot with a bendable plastic spoon, and every fifteen minutes day and night a guard slid the peephole open to write on a clipboard! Then yesterday afternoon they had the nerve to tell me that stewards had packed my clothes out of Cabin 4118 with red security tape and handed me a lifetime ban! I've been punished enough, David—there's no reason to talk crazy about me packing bags when we get home."

Katie looked at me and gave a tiny, unmistakable shake of her head. *Every argument she makes,* Katie's eyes said to mine, *just weakens her case even more.*

I thought the exact same thing. For sixty-two hours down on Deck 2, Mom hadn't spent a single minute worrying about her sixteen-year-old twins or her husband—she had only cared about her confiscated watch, her crew-galley trays, and her cut-off beer tab.

From the back bench seat, I watched Dad's knuckles turn white against the top of the steering wheel. In the rearview mirror, his eyes met mine and Katie's for a brief, steady second before he answered without even looking at Mom.

"You swung a fist at a ship security officer because they wouldn't serve you beer at four o'clock in the afternoon, Brenda," Dad said in a low, gravelly voice that carried clearly to the back seat. "That headache you had down on Deck 2 was sixty-two hours of forced sobriety. And while you were passed out drunk on the kids' sofa bed before security locked you up, I had to hold our sixteen-year-old son and daughter up by their elbows because their legs and arms were giving out on them. Just ten minutes ago at that gas pump, neither one of them could stand up out of the back seat without leaning on me, and you walked right past them to the store without looking back once. That brig wasn't unfair. It was the consequence of who you've chosen to be."

***

#### 4:45 PM — Rocky Mount, North Carolina: Drive-Thru Dinner & Cementing the Lawyer Decision

By quarter to five that afternoon, as the green highway signs ticked past Fayetteville and into Rocky Mount, North Carolina, the three of us were starving and needed another restroom stop.

After Dad helped Katie and me carefully in and out of the roadside rest-area bathrooms just off the exit—where my wrists shook so badly I could barely push the heavy metal restroom door open by myself—Dad didn't make us walk inside a crowded highway sit-down restaurant and try to carry plastic food trays on trembling arms. Instead, he pulled the Holt sedan straight into the drive-thru lane of a *Penny's* hamburger restaurant next to the interstate ramp, ordered four cheeseburger meals, and handed a warm white paper bag of cheeseburgers and fries back to Katie and me before merging back onto the highway.

![Buckled safely in their three-point seatbelts side by side on the gray cloth rear bench seat of their family sedan along Interstate 95 North in August 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie hold hands with their father's mylar-wrapped Quantum Basic library textbook open across Nathaniel's lap and a white paper drive-thru lunch bag on Katie's lap]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/aug_2001_i95_drive_home_backseat_quantum_basic.jpg)

Buckled safely into our three-point shoulder seatbelts side by side on the gray cloth rear bench seat as the pine trees and green *I-95 NORTH* signs rolled past our rear window, Katie held the white paper *Penny's* bag on her lap while Dad's mylar-wrapped *Quantum Basic 5.0* library textbook rested open across mine, with our inner hands clasped tightly together on the cushion between us.

Up in the front passenger seat, as Mom finished her sandwich, I heard her abandon the brig complaints and try a second angle—cold financial bargaining.

"Think about this logically for two seconds, David," Mom said, her voice sharp and calculating in the quiet car. "You're taking classes at CCHR. How exactly do you think you're going to pay the house note in Virginia Beach, buy groceries, finish your degree, and take care of two sixteen-year-olds all by yourself without my income? Look—I'll cut back to drinking only on Friday and Saturday nights. We can put this behind us and go back to normal on Monday."

Beside me in the back seat, Katie squeezed my left hand with her right. Neither of us was even afraid that Dad would give in anymore—watching the back of Dad's shoulders, both of us could tell that Mom was digging her own grave deeper with every sentence.

"She doesn't get it at all," Katie whispered so softly that only I could hear her over the hum of the highway tires.

"Not even a little," I whispered back, looking down at our joined hands between my open *Quantum Basic* book and her lunch bag. Since the day we were born, Katie and I had been each other's anchor, best friend, and safe harbor—and in the lifelong bond the two of us shared, there was simply no room left for someone as bitter, selfish, and mean as our mother. All we needed was each other and our dad.

Up front, I watched Dad slowly set his cup into the center console holder, glance briefly toward the passenger seat, and speak in a calm, ironclad voice I had never heard from him in my life.

"Thank you for saying that out loud, Brenda—because if I had one percent of doubt left about seeking a lawyer's advice when we get home, every argument you've made since Florida just erased it," Dad told her evenly. "You spent three hours complaining about a steel bunk in the brig, and now you're bargaining to drink on weekends—and you still haven't asked Nathaniel or Katie a single time in eight hours why their legs and arms are failing them. I don't care if I have to work double shifts around my CCHR classes to pay the bills. First thing Monday morning at eight-thirty, I am calling a divorce lawyer to file for divorce and full custody of the twins, and my very next call is going to be to Dr. Chen's office to get both of them examined. We are never going back to 'normal.'"

Mom opened her mouth, saw the look on Dad's face, and snapped her jaw shut without another word for the rest of North Carolina and Virginia.

***

#### 9:15 PM — Home in Virginia Beach: Packing the Bags

At nine-fifteen that night, after more than twelve hours on the road, the headlights of our Holt sedan finally swept across our driveway in Virginia Beach.

Before touching a single suitcase in the trunk, Dad walked around to our rear door, helped Katie and me out of the back seat, and supported both of us up the front steps and straight inside to the living room sofa, propping two throw pillows behind our backs and setting his *Quantum Basic 5.0* library book on the coffee table within easy reach.

Then, true to his word from Port Canaveral, Dad walked back out into the front foyer, folded his arms across his chest, and stood watch at the bottom of the stairs for forty-five straight minutes while Brenda packed her suitcases, dragged them out the front door to her car, and backed out of the driveway.

When Dad clicked the front deadbolt shut and walked into the living room to check on us, our house was finally, mercifully peaceful.

### Part 5: Monday Morning in Virginia Beach — The Divorce Lawyer, The PCP, & Borrowed Time

**Date:** 2001-08-27 at 10:15 America/New_York

![Inside a Virginia Beach primary care physician's exam room in late August 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side on the exam bench holding hands while their father David Prairie stands beside them and the family doctor tests Nathaniel's arm and grip strength]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/aug_2001_back_on_dry_land_pcp_exam_borrowed_time.jpg)

#### Monday, August 27, 2001, 10:15 AM — The Divorce Lawyer, The PCP, & Borrowed Time

"Squeeze my fingers as hard as you can, Nathaniel, and hold it for five seconds," Dr. Evelyn Chen instructed gently inside Exam Room 2 of her Virginia Beach family practice clinic on Monday morning.

Sitting side by side on the crinkly white paper of the padded exam table in our t-shirts and jeans, Katie and I kept our inner hands laced tightly together between our knees while I reached my left hand out to squeeze Dr. Chen's fingers.

Earlier that morning at eight-thirty, while Katie and I sat at the kitchen table eating cereal with the sealed *Sunward Cruise Lines* manila envelope resting beside the napkin holder, we had listened as Dad stood by the kitchen wall phone and kept the first half of his highway vow—calling a family law and divorce attorney's office off Independence Boulevard.

When Dad told the attorney on the line that he had a certified Shipboard Security Incident Report and a duplicate VHS security camera tape from Deck 4 showing Brenda's corridor outburst and her attempt to swing at me on the bench over the character meet-and-greet, we watched a wave of relief wash across Dad's face. Hanging up the receiver, Dad had turned to Katie and me and explained what the lawyer had just told him: under Virginia law, the one-year separation waiting period only applied to no-fault divorces. Because the ship's security camera had caught her attempt to strike a minor child and her subsequent detention in the brig, Dad qualified to file immediately on fault grounds of cruelty and reasonable apprehension of bodily hurt—allowing the lawyer to petition the Virginia Beach court right away for sole custody of the two of us, exclusive possession of our house, and a protective order without waiting a single year.

The moment he finished writing down our afternoon appointment time with the lawyer, Dad dialed Dr. Chen, our Primary Care Physician, and explained how badly our arms and legs had been giving out on the cruise and at the Interstate 95 rest stops. Dr. Chen's nurse had told him to bring both of us straight in at ten o'clock.

![Inside their Virginia Beach primary care physician's exam room, sixteen-year-old twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side on the exam table holding hands while their father David watches and Dr. Chen tests Nathaniel's forearm and hand grip strength]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/aug_2001_back_on_dry_land_pcp_exam_borrowed_time.jpg)

For the first two seconds, my grip held around Dr. Chen's fingers—and then, no matter how hard I concentrated, the strength in my forearm and wrist drained away like water through a sieve. When Dr. Chen tested Katie's grip, her shoulder resistance, and the dorsiflexion in both of our ankles, the exact same drop-off happened.

Dr. Chen stepped back, slipped her reflex hammer into the pocket of her white coat, and looked gravely at Dad, who was standing protectively right beside us.

"David," Dr. Chen said quietly, "bringing them in immediately after you got back from Florida was the exact right call."

"They've had mild cerebral palsy since they were little," Dad said, his hand resting warmly on Katie's shoulder. "Brenda always ignored it and acted like they were two completely typical kids, so at first I assumed the ship's movement was just aggravating their balance. Why are both their arms and their legs getting worse at sixteen?"

"Because cerebral palsy does not degenerate over time," Dr. Chen answered clearly, looking from Dad to Katie and me with deep compassion. "Cerebral palsy is a static, non-progressive condition. When sixteen-year-old twins begin losing measurable motor strength in both their lower extremities and their upper extremities over the course of a single summer, something else is happening."

Beside me on the exam table, Katie squeezed my fingers with every ounce of strength left in her hand, and I squeezed right back.

"I'm ordering a full neuromuscular blood panel, nerve conduction studies, and a genetic workup this morning," Dr. Chen continued gently, picking up her chart clipboard. "Until those test results come back and tell us exactly what condition we are dealing with, I need all three of you to prepare yourselves for the reality of what I'm seeing in their muscle tone today. When it comes to walking, Nathaniel and Katie are on borrowed time. Eventually, neither of them is going to be able to walk, and because their upper arms and wrists are fatiguing too, they are eventually going to need power wheelchairs."

The quiet exam room seemed to hold its breath around those words—*on borrowed time*.

"How soon will the test results be back, Evelyn?" Dad asked quietly, his hand resting steady and warm on Katie's shoulder.

"The genetic and nerve-conduction panel takes about two and a half weeks," Dr. Chen answered, writing an appointment card at her desk and handing it to Dad. "Let's schedule their next checkup for Friday afternoon, September fourteenth, at three o'clock—right after their two o'clock dismissal at Northwood High School. You can pick them up when the final bell rings at two, bring them straight here for their three o'clock appointment, and we'll check how their arms and legs are holding up and go over every test result together."

Yet as I looked sideways at my twin sister, and felt Dad's steady, unwavering arm wrap around both of our shoulders at once, the fear didn't break us. Whatever the test results showed when we came back for our three o'clock checkup after school on September fourteenth, Mom's screaming was gone, Daddy was standing right beside us, and Katie and I were going to face every single mile of it together.

## Junior Year at Northwood High & The Prognosis

### Part 1: The Fault-Grounds Filing, The Protective Order, & Junior Year at Northwood High School

**Date:** 2001-09-04 at 11:40 America/New_York

![Inside the sunlit cafeteria of Northwood High School in Virginia Beach in September 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side at a quiet corner table holding hands during their shared Junior-year lunch period with their lunch bags and coding notebook open before them]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/sep_2001_northwood_high_junior_year_shared_lunch.jpg)

#### Monday, August 27, 2001, 2:00 PM — The Lawyer's Office off Independence Boulevard

"Timestamp fifteen-thirty-two and eighteen seconds," family law attorney Arthur Vance read aloud in his quiet, wood-paneled conference room off Independence Boulevard, pressing the pause button on his office VCR remote so the grainy black-and-white frame from the *M/S Sunward Tropics* Deck 4 stairwell camera froze on the television screen.

Sitting side by side on the leather sofa across from Mr. Vance's desk with our inner hands clasped tightly together, Katie and I looked at the frozen image on the screen. Right there beneath the ceiling camera's timestamp—*AUG 22 2001 15:32:18 CAM-04B*—was our mother lunging toward our alcove bench with her arm raised, and Dad standing like a shield squarely between her and the two of us.

"Mr. Prairie, in twenty-four years of practicing family law in Virginia Beach, I have rarely seen a cleaner, more airtight evidentiary packet," Mr. Vance said, setting the remote down beside the Staff Captain's notarized *Shipboard Security Incident Report* and permanent lifetime exclusion notice. "Normally in Virginia, when minor children are involved, a couple has to live separate and apart for one full year before filing for a no-fault divorce. You do not have to wait a single day."

Across from us, Dad leaned forward in his chair, his hands folded on the edge of the mahogany table.

"How fast can we get the court orders in place so Brenda can't come back to the house or show up at their high school when classes start next week?" Dad asked.

"This afternoon," Mr. Vance answered firmly. "Because the ship's security camera captured her attempting to strike sixteen-year-old Nathaniel while intoxicated—and because the ship's brig and medical logs corroborate every word—we are filing a fault-based Complaint for Divorce on grounds of cruelty and reasonable apprehension of bodily hurt under Virginia Code Section 20-91(A)(6) before the Circuit Court clerk's office closes at four o'clock. Alongside it, I am walking an emergency *pendente lite* petition and family protective order straight in to the duty judge."

Mr. Vance counted the points off on his fingers so Katie and I could follow every step.

"First, you get immediate temporary sole legal and physical custody of Nathaniel and Katie," Mr. Vance said. "Second, you get exclusive use and possession of the marital home in Virginia Beach—meaning you are legally authorized to change every lock on the doors tonight, and Brenda cannot set foot on the property. Third, the protective order bars her from coming within five hundred feet of the twins or setting foot on the campus of Northwood High School. And fourth, because fault grounds of cruelty bar an offending spouse from receiving alimony under Virginia law, she won't get a dime of spousal support."

Beside me on the leather sofa, Katie let out a long, quiet breath and leaned her shoulder against mine. That evening before dinner, Dad bought three brass deadbolt kits at the hardware store, changed every lock on our front, back, and garage doors, and handed the shiny new keys to Katie and me.

***

#### Tuesday, September 4, 2001, 11:40 AM — Junior Year at Northwood High School

"We actually pulled it off, Nate—fourth-block lunch and study hall together every single day," Katie said with a relieved smile the Tuesday after Labor Day as the two of us eased down side by side onto the bench of a quiet, sunlit corner table near the tall windows of the Northwood High School cafeteria.

![Inside the sunlit cafeteria of Northwood High School in Virginia Beach in September 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side at a quiet corner table holding hands during their shared Junior-year lunch period with their lunch bags and coding notebook open before them]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/sep_2001_northwood_high_junior_year_shared_lunch.jpg)

Back in July, when we had given up half of our mornings to sit through high-school summer school and knock out a required academic credit early, this exact forty-five-minute sanctuary in the middle of our Junior year schedule was what we had worked for. Earlier that morning at seven-forty-five, Dad had walked into the Northwood High main office with us to hand-deliver a certified copy of the Virginia Beach judge's custody and protective orders to the principal's secretary, removing Brenda's name from our emergency contact cards for good.

Yet as grateful as Katie and I were to be sitting together with our brown paper lunch bags, juice boxes, and our spiral *Quantum Basic* notebook open on the tabletop between us, getting through the first three class periods of eleventh grade had been a sobering wake-up call for our bodies.

Even without the roll of a cruise ship beneath our feet, the long linoleum hallways and concrete stairwells of Northwood High School had pushed our legs and arms to the brink before noon. Halfway up the B-Wing stairs to second-period Chemistry, my left calf had cramped and given out so suddenly that I would have gone down on the steps if Katie hadn't grabbed my belt loop—and when she tried to pull my weight upright against the handrail, her own right wrist and forearm had trembled so violently that she dropped her binder across the landing. By the time we reached the cafeteria doors at eleven-forty, my thighs felt like lead weights and Katie's fingers were shaking so hard that I had to help her pinch open the top of her straw wrapper.

Reaching across the table between our lunch bags, I laced my right fingers through Katie's left hand and rested our joined hands flat on the cool laminate tabletop so neither of our wrists had to work to hold them up.

"How bad are your legs shaking right now?" I asked her quietly beneath the chatter of the cafeteria.

"From my knees down to my ankles," Katie admitted softly, her thumb brushing against the back of my hand as she glanced down at the event-loop diagram in my notebook. "And my right shoulder still hurts from carrying my backpack down the upstairs hall. Our next scheduled checkup with Dr. Chen is next Friday afternoon after school, right?"

"Friday at three o'clock," I nodded, squeezing her hand gently. "Dad's picking us up right at the two o'clock dismissal bell so Dr. Chen can see how our arms and legs are doing. Whatever happens, we've got Dad on our side now, and we've got this lunch table every day. We'll get through eleventh grade together."

### Part 2: When Borrowed Time Runs Out — The Collapse at School, The Emergency Room Escalation, & Time for Powerchairs

**Date:** 2001-09-14 at 13:15 America/New_York

![Inside Dr. Evelyn Chen's consultation office in Virginia Beach in September 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in wheelchairs holding hands beside their father David Prairie while Dr. Chen reviews their neuromuscular test results and prescribes their power wheelchairs]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/sep_2001_dr_chen_prognosis_arms_legs_consultation.jpg)

#### Friday, September 14, 2001, 1:15 PM — When Our Legs Finally Gave Out at School

"Just forty-five more minutes until the two o'clock dismissal bell, Katie, and Dad'll be waiting for us right out front in the pickup loop," I whispered at a quarter past one on Friday afternoon as the two of us walked slowly down the quiet first-floor B-Wing hallway of Northwood High School on our way back from the guidance office.

All week long, all I knew was that Friday afternoon at three o'clock was our next scheduled checkup with Dr. Evelyn Chen. Dad had arranged his Friday schedule at the Community College of Hampton Roads so he could pick Katie and me up right when school let out at two o'clock and drive us straight over to Dr. Chen's office for our three o'clock appointment to see how our arms and legs were doing and go over whatever test results had come back from the lab.

All we had to do was make it thirty more feet down the waxed linoleum corridor to our sixth-block classroom door.

We were holding hands between us for balance, walking close to the lockers so we could brush our free fingertips against the metal doors if our ankles wobbled, when the sensation below my knees suddenly vanished.

It wasn't a slow cramp like on the stairwell the week before, and it wasn't just our calves getting tired. Halfway past Room 112, it felt as though someone had reached inside our spines and pulled the electrical plug on both of our lower bodies at the exact same second.

My right knee buckled inward without an ounce of resistance. Beside me, Katie let out a sharp gasp as both of her ankles folded underneath her at the exact same instant, and before either one of us could catch the locker handles, the two of us collapsed straight down onto the hard linoleum floor.

"Nathaniel! Katie!" Mr. Henderson, our eleventh-grade U.S. History teacher, called out in alarm from his open classroom doorway ten feet ahead of us, dropping his dry-erase marker onto his podium and hurrying out into the hallway. "Are you two all right?!"

My heart was hammering against my ribs from the shock of going down so fast, and my instinct—the stubborn sixteen-year-old instinct that had kept both of us forcing our legs down hallways all week—was to get right back up before the rest of the class saw us on the floor.

"We're okay, Mr. Henderson—just let us stand back up," I gasped, planting my left palm flat on the cold floor while Katie braced her hands against the tiles beside me.

Straining with every muscle left in our shoulders, wrists, and thighs, Katie and I tried to push ourselves back up onto our feet.

We didn't even make it halfway to our knees.

The moment we put weight on our legs and trembling forearms, both of our legs and arms crumpled completely out from under us a second time—sending both of us pitching forward and sideways with nothing left to break our fall.

*Crack. Thud.*

The right side of my forehead slammed hard against the unforgiving linoleum-over-concrete floor, sending a bright, stinging flash across my vision, and six inches away I heard the sickening thud of the left side of Katie's head striking the tiles right beside the locker base.

Before the ringing in my ears even settled, my very first instinct—and Katie's—was the exact same one we had had since the day we were born. Lying flat on the cold linoleum floor with a hot, throbbing knot rising on my forehead, I reached my right hand blindly across the six inches of tile between us, and Katie's left hand met mine in midair, our fingers locking together in a vise grip so neither of us was alone on that floor.

"Stay down! Do not try to get up again!" Mr. Henderson shouted, his voice dropping an octave in urgent alarm as he saw both of our heads strike the floor and our hands clasp together between us.

He sprinted three steps to the classroom wall intercom just inside his doorway and pressed the red emergency call button. "Main Office, get Nurse Callahan to the first-floor B-Wing hallway outside Room 112 immediately! I need a Clinic Emergency escalation right now—Nathaniel and Katie Prairie both collapsed in the hallway, their legs gave out a second time when they tried to get up, and both of them hit their heads on the floor!"

***

#### Friday, September 14, 2001, 1:18 PM — Manual C-Spine Stabilization & The Emergency Room Escalation

Within forty-five seconds, Nurse Callahan, the school's registered nurse, arrived at a dead run with her orange trauma bag, followed closely by Assistant Principal Brooks and the clinic aide.

One look at the two of us lying flat on the hard tile floor holding hands with red, swelling contusions rising on our foreheads—and Mr. Henderson's report that our arms had given out on the second collapse so nothing had cushioned our heads—was all Nurse Callahan needed to escalate the protocol straight to the Emergency Room.

"Nobody lift them and nobody let them sit up!" Nurse Callahan ordered immediately, dropping to her knees behind my head while her trained clinic aide knelt behind Katie's head. Gently but firmly, Nurse Callahan placed both of her palms along the sides of my head to hold my neck in manual inline cervical stabilization, while her aide stabilized Katie's head the exact same way so neither of us could turn or nod our necks.

"Mr. Brooks," Nurse Callahan commanded, "we have an unbroken dual head strike against concrete-backed tile plus acute bilateral lower-extremity paralysis. Under school division head-and-spine protocol, they cannot be lifted into clinic wheelchairs and they cannot go home in a passenger car until an Emergency Room CT scan clears their heads and cervical spines. Call 911 right now for Virginia Beach EMS transport to Bayside General Hospital Emergency Room, put B-Wing on a temporary Hold in Place so the hallway stays clear when the bell rings, and call their father to meet the ambulance at the ER!"

"Calling 911 and Mr. Prairie right now," Assistant Principal Brooks said, unclipping his radio and telephone handset as Mr. Henderson closed the classroom doors along the corridor.

Down on the floor, even with Nurse Callahan's hands cradling the sides of my head so I couldn't turn my neck, I kept my right hand laced tightly through Katie's left hand on the tiles between us.

"Nathaniel, Katie—keep looking straight up at the ceiling tiles so your necks stay completely still, and keep holding each other's hands," Nurse Callahan said calmly as she checked my pupils and then Katie's with her penlight. "Can both of you hear me and tell me your full names?"

"Nathaniel Prairie," I answered clearly, squeezing Katie's fingers twice so she knew I was okay.

"Katie Prairie," Katie answered right after me, squeezing my hand back just as tightly.

"Did either of you black out when your heads hit the floor? Do you know where you are, what day it is, and what happened right before you fell?" Nurse Callahan asked.

"No, ma'am, neither of us blacked out," I said, my voice steady despite the throbbing ache over my right eyebrow. "It's Friday, September fourteenth, and we're in the B-Wing hallway at Northwood High School. Our dad was supposed to pick us up at the two o'clock dismissal bell to take us to our three o'clock appointment with Dr. Evelyn Chen to see how our arms and legs were doing—and while we were walking down the hall, our legs just gave out all at once."

"And when we tried to push ourselves back up," Katie added clearly from beside me, "our legs and arms crumpled again and we hit our heads."

"Can either of you wiggle your toes or push your feet forward against Mr. Henderson's palms?" Nurse Callahan asked.

I concentrated with everything I had, ordering my ankles and calves to move. Nothing happened. Neither of my feet moved a fraction of an inch, and neither did Katie's.

"All right, don't strain," Nurse Callahan told us gently as the distant wail of sirens approached down the boulevard outside. "Your dad is already on his way to the Bayside General Emergency Room to meet you, and I had the front office call Dr. Evelyn Chen's clinic too—she has hospital privileges at Bayside General and she's bringing your test results straight over to the Emergency Room to meet your dad there."

Four minutes later, four Virginia Beach EMS paramedics wheeled two ambulance stretchers through the B-Wing double doors, where Nurse Callahan handed the lead paramedic two printed clinic sheets from her clipboard.

"Both patients are sixteen-year-old fraternal twins on the autism spectrum, Glasgow Coma Scale fifteen, with an unbroken dual head strike and acute bilateral lower-extremity motor loss," Nurse Callahan briefed the lead paramedic right beside us so I could hear every word. "Look at their hands right now—they ground themselves and prevent sensory panic by holding hands and hugging. Obviously they can't sit up or hug while they're in C-collars on backboards, but please keep their stretchers close enough together in the rig and at triage that they don't have to let go of each other's hands, and brief the ER Charge Nurse before you roll in."

"Copy that, Nurse—we'll keep them side by side in Squad 14 and call it in to Bayside General Triage on the radio," the lead paramedic nodded.

Working with practiced precision so our spines never bent or twisted, the paramedics fitted soft foam cervical collars around our necks, unclipped two-piece aluminum scoop stretchers that slid underneath our backs and hips from both sides on the floor, and lifted Katie and me smoothly onto the two padded gurneys—locking the two stretchers side by side in the back of the dual-patient squad rig so my right hand and Katie's left hand stayed clasped between the rails the entire ride to the hospital.

***

#### Friday, September 14, 2001, 2:10 PM — Hospital Rules, The CT Doorway, & Dr. Chen's Verdict in the ER

Because the Community College of Hampton Roads campus was only ten minutes from Bayside General Hospital, Dad actually beat our ambulance to the Emergency Room entrance by two minutes.

When the rear doors of Squad 14 swung open at the ER bay, Dad was already standing right beside the ER Charge Nurse, having briefed her himself that we were sixteen-year-old twins on the autism spectrum who grounded ourselves through physical contact—and that Dr. Evelyn Chen had already called down from her clinic across the medical plaza to let the ER team know she was bringing our neuromuscular lab packet over in person.

Even with Nurse Callahan's handoff and Dad's briefing, however, there were still real hospital rules for sixteen-year-old minors that the Emergency Room couldn't bend.

First, because Katie and I were opposite-sex sixteen-year-old minors, hospital policy required the triage team to wheel us into two separate adjacent trauma bays—Bay 3 and Bay 4—divided by a heavy fabric privacy curtain while the ER nurses checked our spines and stuck cardiac monitor leads under our shirts. While we lay flat in our rigid foam cervical collars unable to sit up or hug, the Charge Nurse compromised by leaving a six-inch gap at the bottom of the curtain between our two bays so I could reach my right hand under the curtain hem and hold onto Katie's left hand while the monitors beeped overhead.

Second—and hardest of all—was the hospital's radiation safety rule at the CT scanner.

"Mr. Prairie, I know they ground themselves by holding hands, but federal radiation rules only allow one patient inside the CT scanner room at a time," the radiology tech explained gently when the orderlies arrived at two-twenty to wheel us down the hall for our head and cervical spine scans. "We'll scan Katie first while Nathaniel's gurney waits right outside the lead glass door with you, and then we'll swap them. They'll only be apart for five minutes each."

Letting go of Katie's fingers outside the heavy lead-lined doors of the CT suite while my own neck was strapped flat in a foam collar was the longest five minutes of my life. As the sliding door closed behind Katie's gurney, my heart rate monitor began to beep faster—until Dad stepped right up beside my stretcher, took my right hand in both of his warm palms, and stood where he could see through the control-room glass.

"I'm looking right at her through the window, Nate—she's lying totally still inside the scanner ring and giving us a thumbs-up with her left hand," Dad narrated calmly, squeezing my fingers until my breathing steadied. "Ninety more seconds and she's coming right back out, and then I'll watch you through the glass while Katie waits right here."

True to his word, ten minutes later both of our CT scans were finished. By three-ten that afternoon, when the radiologist called down to confirm that neither Katie nor I had a skull fracture, concussion, or cervical spine injury, the ER trauma doctor unstrapped our foam cervical collars, the Charge Nurse pulled the dividing curtain between Bay 3 and Bay 4 all the way open, and the orderlies pushed our two hospital beds right up against each other with the inner side rails lowered.

The instant the beds locked together and the doctor raised the heads of our mattresses to forty-five degrees, Katie and I didn't just hold hands—we leaned across the narrow seam between the two hospital mattresses and wrapped both of our arms tightly around each other's shoulders in the long, deep grounding hug we had needed ever since we hit the hallway floor at school.

![Inside Dr. Evelyn Chen's consultation office in Virginia Beach in September 2001, sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in wheelchairs holding hands beside their father David Prairie while Dr. Chen reviews their neuromuscular test results and prescribes their power wheelchairs]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/sep_2001_dr_chen_prognosis_arms_legs_consultation.jpg)

Seconds later, walking into the combined ER bay alongside the emergency physician was Dr. Evelyn Chen, wearing her white coat and carrying the thick manila lab folder from her clinic across the medical plaza.

"First things first—the head and cervical spine CT scans for both Nathaniel and Katie are completely clean," Dr. Chen announced with a warm, relieved smile as Katie and I eased back against our pillows with our inner hands locked together between our beds and fresh ice packs resting against the matching goose eggs on our foreheads. "Nurse Callahan followed the exact right protocol calling EMS after a dual head strike on a concrete school floor, and thank goodness neither of you suffered a fracture or intracranial bleed."

Then Dr. Chen stepped to the foot of our two ER beds and tested the reflexes and motor response in our knees, calves, and ankles.

Just like on the hallway floor outside Room 112 at Northwood High School, when she tapped our patellar tendons and asked us to lift our shins against her fingers, our lower legs didn't budge.

Dr. Chen pulled a rolling stool up beside our two beds, opened the thick manila lab folder containing the genetic sequencing and nerve-conduction studies that had arrived from Norfolk that morning, and looked at Dad, Katie, and me with deep, solemn compassion.

"David, Nathaniel, Katie," Dr. Chen said quietly, "two and a half weeks ago, when you came to see me the Monday morning after your cruise, I told you that Nathaniel and Katie's legs were walking on borrowed time—and that they would eventually need power wheelchairs. We had scheduled your three o'clock checkup today to review these test results and talk about transitioning to powerchairs."

She looked straight into my eyes, and then into Katie's.

"What happened in the B-Wing hallway at Northwood High School at one-fifteen this afternoon wasn't a temporary spell, and it wasn't just end-of-the-week fatigue," Dr. Chen said gently. "Your borrowed time is up. Neither of your legs can support your body weight anymore, and from this afternoon forward, neither of you can ever try to stand or walk again—because trying to push yourselves up will only cause another collapse like the one that sent you to the Emergency Room today. It is officially time for powerchairs."

Even though Katie and I had known in our hearts from the second we reached for each other's hands on that linoleum floor that our walking days were over, hearing Dr. Chen say the words out loud made everything real. Beside me, Katie's fingers tightened around mine between our two hospital beds, and I squeezed her hand right back with everything I had.

"Tell us what the test results showed, Evelyn," Dad said steadily, standing right between our beds with one hand resting on my shoulder and the other on Katie's. "Why did their legs give out completely today, and what does it mean for their arms?"

"When you two were toddlers," Dr. Chen explained clearly, "your uneven balance was diagnosed as mild spastic diplegic cerebral palsy. Because mild CP is static and non-progressive—and because your mother always brushed off any new symptoms—nobody realized that your early balance issues were masking a second, genetic condition that both of you inherited as fraternal twins: a juvenile-onset progressive axonal motor neuropathy."

Dr. Chen pointed to the nerve-conduction graphs inside the folder.

"In this condition, the longest motor nerves in the body—the ones running from the lower spine down to the feet, ankles, calves, and quadriceps—lose their electrical signal amplitude first, and the sixteen-year-old adolescent growth spurt pushes those nerves past their threshold," Dr. Chen said. "Today at school, those lower-extremity motor nerves finally dropped below the threshold required to keep your knees locked upright. And as you both felt when you tried to push yourselves up off the hallway floor and crumpled the second time, the neuropathy is also causing progressive weakness in your shoulders, biceps, and forearms—which is why standard manual wheelchairs that you have to push with your hands are out of the question."

"What about our fingers, Dr. Chen?" Katie asked, her voice small but brave as she held onto my hand. "Are we still going to be able to type on our computers and write *Quantum Basic* code?"

Dr. Chen's face warmed with an immediate, reassuring smile.

"Yes, Katie—and that is the best news in your entire lab packet," Dr. Chen answered. "As long as we stop you from straining your shoulders and wrists trying to lift your body weight or push manual wheelchair rims, your fine finger dexterity at a keyboard will remain intact for years to come. You will both be able to type, write computer code, use a mouse, and hold hands—and with a fingertip joystick on your power wheelchair armrests, you'll be able to glide through Northwood High School all day without tiring your arms at all."

Dr. Chen pulled three prescription and accommodation forms from her folder and signed them right on the bedside tray table.

"First," Dr. Chen said, "I have signed an urgent hospital prescription for two SwiftChair compact, portable take-apart power wheelchairs—small-footprint motorized chairs with ten-inch drive wheels, right-hand armrest joysticks, and removable battery boxes and seats that you can break down to fit inside the trunk of your sedan, David, along with a smooth composite transfer sliding board so Nathaniel and Katie can slide horizontally from their wheelchair seats onto the back seat of the car or onto their beds without ever putting weight on their legs. Second, Hospital Physical Therapy is bringing up two loaner wheelchairs and a transfer board right now so Dad can take you home safely tonight until the custom powerchairs are delivered in two weeks. And third, here is your updated Section 504 Plan for Northwood High School: elevator keys, duplicate classroom textbooks so you never lift a heavy bag, and full-time wheelchair access starting Monday morning."

Dad picked up the signed forms, then leaned down between our two hospital beds and wrapped one arm around Katie and one arm around me, kissing the top of Katie's head and then mine just above our bruised foreheads.

"I was never so scared in my life as when Mr. Brooks called me and said you two were headed to the Emergency Room," Dad whispered, his voice thick with emotion as he held us both close. "Wait until you see how fast I learn to break those two powerchairs down for the trunk of the car. Your legs may have run out of borrowed time today, but as long as the three of us have each other, nothing is going to stop you."

### Part 3: Two Compact Power Wheelchairs in the Trunk, Downsizing to Military Highway, & CVTA Route 15

**Date:** 2001-10-06 at 13:10 America/New_York

![At the CVTA Route 15 bus stop along the wide eight-lane corridor of US-13 Military Highway near Indian River Road in October 2001, sixteen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in their compact power wheelchairs holding hands beside their father David Prairie as high-floor Beacon V transit bus #914—wearing legacy Tidewater Regional Transit horizontal blue-and-green stripes with a CVTA logo sticker beside the front door—lowers its Elevate-U front-stairwell hydraulic wheelchair lift to the sidewalk]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/oct_2001_military_hwy_indian_river_cvta_route_15_wheelchairs.jpg)

#### Saturday, October 6, 2001, 1:10 PM — Two Compact Power Wheelchairs in the Trunk & Our New House off Military Highway

"Unclip the two under-seat battery boxes first, pull the quick-release pins to lift both padded seats off their posts, fold the backrests and footrests flat, and set the two compact motorized drive bases side by side in the trunk with two inches to spare under the hinges," Dad said with a proud grin on the first Saturday of October, wiping his brow as he demonstrated how both of our brand-new SwiftChair compact portable power wheelchairs broke down to fit inside the open trunk of our silver-blue Holt sedan.

Sitting side by side on the back bench seat of our Holt sedan with the rear passenger doors swung wide open to the concrete driveway of our new rental house and our inner hands clasped together, Katie and I watched Dad practice the disassembly routine he had timed with his wristwatch all morning.

Four days earlier, the seating clinic had delivered the two compact, take-apart power wheelchairs Dr. Chen had prescribed for us after our legs gave out for good in the hallway at Northwood High School—nimble, small-footprint power bases with ten-inch solid drive wheels, under-seat battery packs, and feather-light right-hand armrest joysticks that let us glide down the long corridors of school with a gentle nudge of one finger without ever straining our wrists or shoulders on manual hand rims. Because Dr. Chen had documented our progressive axonal motor neuropathy down to the millimeter, Dad's health insurance had covered ninety percent of the chairs.

What health insurance didn't cover, however, was a vehicle to carry them assembled.

A full-size conversion van equipped with a hydraulic wheelchair lift or fold-out side ramp cost upwards of thirty-five thousand dollars—an astronomical sum that might as well have been thirty-five million on Dad's tight budget. Between trying to finish his computer science degree at the Community College of Hampton Roads, losing Brenda's paycheck—meager as her income had been—and getting stuck with the crushing credit card debt from the seven-day Caribbean cruise that Brenda had booked behind his back, Dad was scraping by to make every dollar count.

Thankfully, because the seating clinic had ordered portable take-apart power chairs whose battery packs, seats, and drive bases separated into manageable thirty-five-pound components—and had supplied us with a smooth composite transfer sliding board that bridged the gap between the wheelchair seat and the back seat of our family Holt sedan—we could still get around by car without a lift van. Whenever we drove somewhere, Katie and I would roll our compact power chairs right up to the open rear passenger door, switch off our joysticks, flip up the inner armrest, and slide horizontally across the transfer board onto the back bench seat with Dad's steadying hands before buckling our three-point seatbelts while Dad broke both chairs down like a pit crew and loaded the bases, battery boxes, and folded seats into the trunk.

Even so, Dad had been completely honest with Katie and me around the kitchen table two weeks earlier when he opened his budget ledger.

Without a second income—and with the cruise credit card bill and legal fees hanging over our heads—we couldn't afford the mortgage on our old two-story house anymore. More importantly, even if we could have paid for it, our old house had fourteen steep wooden stairs leading up to the bedrooms and the only full bathroom—stairs that neither Katie nor I could ever climb again now that the borrowed time in our legs had run out.

So Dad had put the two-story house up for sale—using the modest equity to wipe out Brenda's cruise debt completely—and spent every evening for two weeks poring over rental listings, Virginia Beach school-zone boundary maps, and printed bus schedules from the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority.

What Dad found was a stroke of pure logistical genius: a small, single-story brick ranch house for rent on a quiet side street just off Military Highway near the Indian River Road intersection, right where Virginia Beach met Norfolk and Chesapeake.

First, the rent was nearly four hundred dollars a month less than our old mortgage, bringing our monthly bills comfortably inside Dad's budget while he finished school at CCHR. Second, every single room in the little house—the living room, the eat-in kitchen, the hallway bathroom with its roll-up sink and shower transfer bench, and both bedrooms—sat on one flat ground floor, with a gentle aluminum ramp Dad had bolted over the two brick front porch steps. Third, because the house sat just inside the western edge of Northwood High School's zoned attendance boundary, Katie and I didn't have to change high schools in the middle of our Junior year, and a lift-equipped Virginia Beach school bus could pick us up right at the end of our driveway every weekday morning.

And fourth—the detail Dad was proudest of as he reassembled our two compact power wheelchairs beside the open rear doors of the car, locked the seats onto their posts, and dropped the battery boxes back into place—was what ran right along the massive eight-lane corridor of US-13 (Military Highway) at the end of our block.

"Slide back into your chairs and let's head out to the corner on Military Highway before the one-twenty-five bus gets here," Dad said, closing the trunk lid of the Holt sedan, helping Katie and me slide across the transfer board from the back seat into our waiting power chairs, and holding up a yellow-highlighted paper pamphlet folded in his hand. "Since Route 15 stays strictly out on Military Highway and doesn't turn into the neighborhood streets, I want to show you two how fast we can get out to the main road without having to break your power chairs down at all."

Clicking our lap belts shut and flipping our armrests back down, Katie and I pressed the green power buttons on our armrest joysticks and hummed side by side down the smooth concrete sidewalk behind Dad. In less than three minutes, we turned the corner from our quiet residential block onto the wide sidewalk bordering Military Highway and Indian River Road, where four lanes of northbound traffic and four lanes of southbound traffic roared past a raised center median.

***

#### Saturday, October 6, 2001, 1:25 PM — Catching CVTA Route 15 on Eight-Lane Military Highway

"Look at the timetable right here under Military Hwy. & Indian River Rd.," Dad said over the steady hum of eight-lane highway traffic as the three of us stopped beside the metal bus stop post in the crisp October sunshine, unfolding the printed **CVTA Route 15 — *Naval Base Norfolk / Robert Hall Blvd.*** schedule so both Katie and I could read it.

Holding Katie's left hand with my right hand between our two compact power wheelchairs, I leaned in with my sister to follow the yellow highlighter line Dad had drawn across the grid of numbers.

"Wait a second," Katie said, her eyes widening as she traced the columns from left to right. "It runs every thirty minutes all day Monday through Saturday?"

"Like clockwork," Dad smiled, tapping each column with his index finger. "Going northbound up Military Highway, Route 15 stops right across the highway at Military Highway and Indian River Road at :01 and :31 past every hour from six-thirty-one in the morning until six-thirty-one at night, and then once an hour until ten-thirty-one. In exactly fourteen minutes—at :15 and :45 past the hour—it drops you right at the doors of Glenrock Mall, where you can transfer to Routes 20, 23, 25, or 27, or stay right on Route 15 past Glenrock Shopping Center, Little Creek Road, and Hampton Boulevard all the way to Naval Base Norfolk."

"And what about going southbound on our side of the road?" I asked, looking at the lower table where Dad had highlighted two alternating rows in yellow and green.

"That's the best part," Dad said, pointing to the bold route note printed across the center of the timetable: *On arrival at Old Emerald Creek Rd. and Military Hwy., every other trip will alternate between Robert Hall and Emerald Creek Mall Monday through Saturday.* "Southbound buses leave Glenrock Mall at :15 and :45 past the hour and get right here to Military Highway and Indian River Road ten minutes later, at :25 and :55 past every hour. Every bus that stops here at :25 past the hour—like the 1:25 PM bus coming down the right-hand lane right now—turns onto the Emerald Creek Mall branch and puts us at the mall entrance twenty minutes later at 1:45 PM. And every bus that stops here at :55 past the hour goes straight down to Robert Hall Boulevard and Tidewater Square by :15 past the next hour."

Right on cue, signaling across the four southbound lanes of US-13 (Military Highway) toward our curb was one of the classic forty-foot high-floor workhorses of the fleet: a 900-series Beacon V transit bus (Fleet #914) that operated alongside the agency's high-floor MetroCoach buses. Because Tidewater Regional Transit (TRT) on the Southside and Peninsula Transit (PenTran) across the harbor had only merged two years earlier in October 1999 to form the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority (CVTA), Bus #914 was still wearing its original 1990s TRT paint scheme—a crisp white body banded with horizontal navy-blue and green stripes down its flanks—with a rectangular CVTA logo vinyl decal sticker applied right next to the front boarding doors below the *15 EMERALD CREEK MALL* destination sign.

![At the CVTA Route 15 bus stop along the wide eight-lane corridor of US-13 Military Highway near Indian River Road in October 2001, sixteen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in their compact power wheelchairs holding hands beside their father David Prairie as high-floor Beacon V transit bus #914—wearing legacy Tidewater Regional Transit horizontal blue-and-green stripes with a CVTA logo sticker beside the front door—lowers its Elevate-U front-stairwell hydraulic wheelchair lift to the sidewalk]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/oct_2001_military_hwy_indian_river_cvta_route_15_wheelchairs.jpg)

As Bus #914 hissed to a stop parallel to the concrete curb, the bi-fold front doors swung open. Because the 900-series Beacon V was a true high-floor bus whose main passenger deck sat three full feet above the street at the top of a three-step stairwell, there was no sloped ramp. Instead, standing high up on the elevated driver's platform, the uniformed operator pulled the parking brake knob, flipped up the two forward priority benches behind the driver's area, and switched on the Elevate-U electro-hydraulic stairwell wheelchair lift.

"Afternoon, folks! Welcome aboard The 15," the operator called down warmly from the elevated doorway as the three deep front steps flattened into a level horizontal steel elevator platform, telescoped outward on twin vertical hydraulic lift arms with yellow-and-black tubular side handrails, and lowered straight down to rest flat on the concrete sidewalk before dropping the yellow outer safety barrier flap flush against the curb. "Come on onto the elevator platform one at a time—I've got both wheelchair bays folded up and ready for you!"

Katie and I glanced at each other with identical grins. Nobody had to struggle with a car transfer, and Dad didn't have to unclip battery boxes or lift thirty-five-pound power-chair bases into a trunk. Giving Katie's hand a quick squeeze before letting go for thirty seconds, I nudged my right-hand joystick forward and rolled my compact power chair onto the flat horizontal steel platform between the two side handrails first; the outer yellow safety barrier flipped up behind my rear wheels to lock me in, the twin vertical hydraulic arms raised my chair straight up three feet in the air like an elevator to the high interior floor of Bus #914, and I backed neatly into the right-hand securement bay. Seconds later, the elevator platform lowered straight back down to the sidewalk for Katie and lifted her power chair right up to the high deck across the aisle from me before folding seamlessly back into three staircase steps so Dad could climb up into the bus and drop our fares into the farebox.

Once the operator had clicked the four-point floor straps onto both of our power-chair frames, the front doors hissed shut and Bus #914 merged back into the four southbound lanes of Military Highway.

Across the center aisle of the high-floor bus, Katie reached her left hand out over her armrest, and I reached my right hand out to meet hers, our fingers lacing together in the warm afternoon sunlight streaming through the tall transit windows.

"We don't need a thirty-five-thousand-dollar van to go wherever we want, Nate," Katie said softly over the deep diesel rumble of the Beacon V engine as we cruised south toward Emerald Creek Mall. "Between the school bus on weekdays, breaking our chairs down for the trunk of Dad's car, and Route 15 out on Military Highway at the end of our block, we've got the whole city back."

"And a house with zero stairs," I agreed, squeezing my sister's hand as Dad smiled at the two of us from the seat beside her. "We're going to be just fine."

## Sixteen on Four Wheels & Finding Dates

### Part 1: Navigating Northwood High on Four Wheels, Two First Dates, & Why We Ride on Saturday

**Date:** 2001-10-15 at 07:10 America/New_York

![Inside the sunlit cafeteria of Northwood High School in Virginia Beach, sixteen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side holding hands during their shared Junior-year lunch period]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/sep_2001_northwood_high_junior_year_shared_lunch.jpg)

#### Monday, October 15, 2001, 7:10 AM — Rolling the "Blue Mile" & The Brass Elevator Key

"Keep your speed dial on two until we clear the Commons tiles, Nate, or the vibration is going to rattle our teeth right out of our heads," Katie said with a wry smile on Monday morning as the two of us rolled our compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs through the pinned-open double fire doors of Northwood High School.

Beneath our ten-inch solid drive wheels, the textured quarry tiles of the Northwood High Commons buzzed like a washboard before giving way to the smooth, polished linoleum of the main academic corridor everyone called the "Blue Mile."

It was the start of our second full week navigating eleventh grade on four wheels instead of two feet, and physically, the difference was like night and day.

Back in September, walking from the morning bus loop to first period had left our calves cramping, our knees trembling, and our shoulders aching from the weight of our backpacks before the first bell even rang. Now, thanks to the Section 504 Medical Mobility Plan that Dr. Chen had signed and Dad had walked into the principal's office, neither Katie nor I carried a single textbook between classes. A duplicate set of our Junior-year textbooks sat on a designated shelf in each of our classrooms, and clipped to our lanyard ID badges alongside our house keys were two shiny brass Vanguard Elevator keys issued by the school custodian.

Socially, however, showing up to your Junior year of high school in matching power wheelchairs was like dropping a boulder into the middle of a swimming pool.

As Katie and I glided side by side down the right-hand lane of the Blue Mile with our right index fingers resting lightly on our armrest joysticks, the noisy wall of lockers ahead of us reacted the exact same way it had all last week. Conversations dropped to sudden, awkward whispers as we rolled past. Kids who had known us since sixth grade either stared straight down at their sneakers as if looking at a power wheelchair might be rude, or leaped three feet out of our path against the lockers as though we were driving runaway forklifts.

When we reached the B-Wing stairwell at the end of the hall—where two hundred students were jostling up the concrete steps toward the second floor—I pulled my brass key on its retractable lanyard reel, inserted it into the keyhole beside the heavy gray door of the school's service elevator, and turned it clockwise.

With a slow mechanical clunk, the sliding metal door rattled open, releasing the familiar scent of lemon floor wax and damp mop buckets. Katie and I rolled our compact power chairs straight inside side by side, and as the door slid shut, sealing out the roar of the stairwell, Katie reached her left hand across the two inches between our armrests and laced her fingers through mine.

"Did you see Todd Jenkins jump into the water fountain alcove when we turned the corner by the trophy case?" I asked as the old hydraulic elevator groaned upward toward the second floor at roughly half a mile an hour.

"He nearly knocked his own varsity jacket off his shoulder," Katie laughed, squeezing my hand as the floor indicator light blinked from 1 to 2. "Remind me again why we used to be afraid of using this elevator?"

"Because we didn't know how nice it was to get to second period without our legs shaking," I said honestly as the elevator chimed and the door rattled open onto the upstairs landing. "See you at eleven-forty at our table?"

"Count on it," Katie smiled, giving my hand one last squeeze before we released our fingers and steered in opposite directions down the hall—Katie toward first-period U.S. History, and me toward first-period English.

***

#### Monday, October 15, 2001, 11:45 AM — Head Over Heels for Heather & Katie's Guarded Heart

"You were smiling all the way from the milk cooler to the table, Nate," Katie teased gently four hours later as the two of us parked our compact power wheelchairs side by side at the open end of our sunlit corner cafeteria table and clicked our joystick motors off. "Let me guess: Heather Collins sat next to you in second-period Computer Lab again?"

I felt my ears turn warm all the way to the tips as I set my brown paper lunch bag beside our spiral *Quantum Basic* notebook.

"She moved her chair right next to my power chair for the whole ninety-minute block," I admitted, unable to keep the grin off my face as I poked Katie's straw into her apple juice box for her and handed it across our armrests. "When everybody else in the row stepped around my wheelchair like it was radioactive, Heather didn't even blink. She showed me wallet photos of her family, helped me pick a color palette for our *Quantum Basic* menu screen, and asked if I wanted to sit with her at the computer table every day this semester."

The truth was, I wasn't just crushing on Heather Collins—at sixteen years old, I was completely, head-over-heels in love with her. From the first week of Junior year in Room 204, Heather had treated me with a warm, effortless kindness that made my heart race every time she walked through the classroom door.

Beside me, Katie took a sip of her juice box, her expression softening with genuine happiness for me before a quieter, more thoughtful look crossed her face.

"What about Ryan Miller in third-period Chemistry?" I asked, reaching my right hand across the gap between our chairs to take Katie's left hand on the laminate tabletop. "You said on Friday that he kept leaning across the lab bench to talk to you."

"He did again today," Katie said, though her voice was noticeably more reserved than mine. Ever since seventh grade—when Brandon had dumped her in the middle of the hallway and left her sobbing beneath the blue butterfly in her bedroom—Katie had kept a careful, protective wall around her heart when it came to boys. "Actually, while we were cleaning up our beakers at the accessible lab sink, Ryan asked if I wanted to go out with him to Emerald Creek Mall this weekend. And he asked if you had a girlfriend you wanted to bring along so we could make it a double date."

My breath caught in my throat. Neither Katie nor I had ever been on a real high-school date before. Before our legs gave out in September and we got our compact power wheelchairs, Katie and I had gone to both Emerald Creek Mall and Chesapeake Bay Mall all the time—with Chesapeake Bay Mall out on Lynnhaven Parkway always being our favorite "big trip" mall (which we still hadn't visited yet since getting our powerchairs), and Emerald Creek Mall becoming our favorite regular teenage hangout because we only needed to ride a single bus from Military Highway to get there.

"Wait—what did you tell him?" I asked.

"I told him I'd talk to you at lunch first," Katie said carefully, her fingers tightening around mine. "Honestly, Nate, I don't know about Ryan. He's nice enough when we're lab partners, but I can't tell if he actually likes *me* or if he just likes having someone who balances his chemical equations for him. Still... I see the way your whole face lights up whenever you talk about Heather. And if we go together as a double date on Saturday afternoon, neither one of us has to go through our first high-school date alone."

"Why Saturday specifically?" I asked, glancing at the calendar inside my binder.

"Because I checked Dad's printed CVTA Route 15 schedule on the refrigerator this morning," Katie explained, tapping the timetable notes she had copied into the margin of our notebook. "Remember the alternating branch rule at Old Emerald Creek Road and Military Highway? Route 15 only serves Emerald Creek Mall Monday through Saturday. On Sundays, every single trip runs straight down to Robert Hall Boulevard and skips Emerald Creek Mall completely. So if we want to take the bus to the mall ourselves, it has to be on Saturday."

"Then let's do Saturday," I said earnestly, squeezing her hand so she knew my promise from seventh grade hadn't changed one bit. "I'll ask Heather in Computer Lab on Wednesday morning, and you tell Ryan we'll meet them at the Emerald Creek Mall upstairs food court at twelve-forty-five on Saturday. And Katie—if Ryan acts weird for even one second, you and I have our bus fare in our pockets and each other right beside us."

***

#### Wednesday, October 17, 2001, 10:05 AM — Two Very Different Reactions

"A Saturday afternoon double date at Emerald Creek Mall with you and Katie?" Heather Collins repeated on Wednesday morning in second-period Computer Lab, her warm hazel eyes lighting up with an immediate, genuine smile that made my chest feel weightless. "I would love to, Nate! How are you and Katie getting over to Emerald Creek?"

"We live right near Military Highway and Indian River Road now, so we're taking CVTA Route 15 at twelve-twenty-five," I explained honestly, bracing myself just in case a high-school girl thought riding the city bus on a first date was uncool. "The high-floor bus has a hydraulic wheelchair lift in the front stairwell, so Katie and I can roll our power chairs right aboard together without Dad having to break them down for the trunk of his car."

Heather didn't flinch or look embarrassed for a fraction of a second. Instead, her smile grew even warmer.

"That is so smart—my dad can drop me off at the main mall entrance at twelve-forty, and I'll ride the back glass elevator right up to the second-floor food court to meet you and Katie," Heather said brightly, reaching over to touch the sleeve of my flannel shirt. "I can't wait for Saturday, Nate."

Two hours later at Fourth-Block lunch, however, when Katie rolled up to the corner table and parked beside me, I could see immediately from the tight set of her jaw that her conversation with Ryan Miller in third-period Chemistry had gone very differently.

"Ryan agreed to meet us at twelve-forty-five on Saturday," Katie said quietly, unwrapping her sandwich and taking my right hand across the table, "but we didn't even make it out of the Chemistry lab before he got weird about two things."

"What did he say?" I asked, frowning.

"First, when I told him we were meeting at Emerald Creek Mall, Ryan puffed his chest out and said, *'Oh, I'll drive over to your house in my coupe and pick you up—just fold your wheelchair up and throw it in my trunk,'*" Katie recounted, shaking her head in disbelief. "I tried to explain to him twice that a motorized power wheelchair with a battery box and solid drive wheels doesn't just fold flat like an umbrella stroller, and that even if Dad disassembled it into four pieces, it wouldn't fit inside a two-door sports coupe."

"And what was the second thing?" I asked.

"When I told him that you and I were riding CVTA Route 15 to the mall together because we always travel as a pair," Katie said, her blue eyes steady and guarded as she looked at me, "Ryan frowned and said, *'Wait—you're bringing your brother on the bus instead of riding with me? Don't you ever go anywhere without Nate?'* I looked him straight in the eye and told him, *'No, Ryan. Nate is my twin brother, and he comes first.'* He backed down and said he'd meet us at the food court at twelve-forty-five, but... I have a feeling Saturday is going to be a very short test for Ryan Miller."

### Part 2: Cousin Wesley, Ryan's Escalator Exit, & Two Dates at Emerald Creek Mall

**Date:** 2001-10-20 at 12:45 America/New_York

![Inside the bustling second-floor food court of Emerald Creek Mall beneath its sunlit white tented canopy ceiling in October 2001, sixteen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in their compact power wheelchairs holding hands across from Heather Collins and Ryan Miller]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/oct_2001_emerald_creek_mall_food_court_double_date.jpg)

#### Saturday, October 20, 2001, 12:45 PM — The Second-Floor Food Court & Cousin Wesley

"Nate! Katie! You guys made it right on time!" Heather Collins called out with a radiant smile at twelve-forty-five on Saturday afternoon, waiting for us on the terracotta-tile landing right outside the brushed-brass sliding doors of the freestanding early-1980s glass elevator on the main retail concourse—its warm cream-colored corner columns framing multi-pane glass and topped with a small glass pyramid cap beneath the white steel roof trusses—right where the elevator connected the first floor of the main concourse up to the second-floor food court wing of Emerald Creek Mall.

![Sixteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie rolling side by side in their compact power wheelchairs holding inner hands out of the open brushed-brass doors of the freestanding early-1980s glass elevator onto the second-floor terracotta-tile landing of Emerald Creek Mall in October 2001, greeted warmly by Heather Collins with the two-story entrance to Wellington's Department Store visible across the main concourse behind the elevator]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/oct_2001_emerald_creek_mall_elevator_heather_greeting.jpg)

Twenty minutes earlier, Dad had walked with us out to the corner of Military Highway and Indian River Road, pressed a twenty-dollar bill into each of our hands, and waved from the sidewalk as Katie and I rode the Elevate-U hydraulic stairwell wheelchair lift aboard the 12:25 PM Saturday southbound CVTA Route 15 (Bus #914). Right on schedule at 12:45 PM, the high-floor Beacon V bus had dropped us off at the bus stop on the mall's outer ring road, straight across the parking lot from the first-floor exterior entrance of Wellington's, the two-story anchor department store at the end of the wing. Rolling side by side from the ring road through the ground-floor cosmetics and apparel aisles of Wellington's into the first-floor main concourse, Katie and I had stepped right into the glass-backed scenic elevator directly in front of the department store's mall entrance and ridden it up to the second floor.

Before I could even click my joystick speed dial down to one on the upper landing, Heather stepped straight up to my compact SwiftChair power wheelchair, leaned down without a trace of hesitation, and wrapped her arms around my shoulders in a warm hello hug that sent my heart soaring into the skylights.

"Hi, Nate," Heather said softly against my cheek before straightening up and smiling just as warmly at my twin sister. "And hi, Katie! My dad dropped me off at the lower Wellington's entrance and I rode the glass elevator right up here from the first-floor concourse so we could roll into the food court wing together—and whenever we go downstairs to browse the lower level, I'm riding the elevator right alongside you two."

"Thanks for waiting for us by the elevator, Heather," Katie said, her guarded posture relaxing noticeably at how naturally Heather included both of us and had zero problem being seen riding the mall elevator with two kids in wheelchairs.

Turning 180 degrees away from the glass elevator and the two-story interior entrance of Wellington's behind us on the main retail concourse, the three of us rolled straight into the bustling second-floor food court wing—where the high white tented fabric canopy ceiling and skylights echoed with weekend chatter, the far exterior glass exit doors at the back of the wing opened straight out onto the graded upper section of the mall parking lot that gently sloped up to the second-floor level, and Bobby Vanguard's 1982 synth-pop classic *"Moving Up the Escalator"* drifted down from the PA speakers above the food court escalators—just as Ryan Miller strolled over from the brass-and-glass mezzanine railing in his letterman jacket.

Unlike Heather, Ryan didn't look thrilled to see our two compact power wheelchairs rolling side by side.
