"Hey," Ryan said, glancing down at Katie's motorized drive wheels as we picked a four-top table positioned right on the tile concourse between the two central escalators—with the up escalator rising from the first floor on Katie's and my side, and the down escalator descending to the first floor on Heather's and Ryan's side—and pulled two plastic chairs away so Katie and I could park our power chairs side by side facing Ryan and Heather. "Katie, I still don't get why you made me meet you here instead of letting me pick you up in my car. You seriously couldn't just fold that chair up and stick it in my trunk?"

![Inside the bustling second-floor food court of Emerald Creek Mall beneath its sunlit white tented canopy ceiling in October 2001, sixteen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in their compact power wheelchairs holding hands across from Heather Collins and Ryan Miller]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/oct_2001_emerald_creek_mall_food_court_double_date.jpg)

"Ryan, I told you on Wednesday—it's a power wheelchair," Katie replied evenly, tapping the black battery box beneath her seat. "It has electric motors and heavy batteries. It doesn't fold up like a beach chair, and Nate and I ride Route 15 together."

Ryan let out a loud, skeptical sigh, slumping into his seat across from Katie as Heather and I brought back our lunch trays from Crown Burger and Annie's Pretzels.

Halfway from the counter to the table, the progressive neuropathy weakness in my left forearm flared under the weight of the plastic food tray. My left wrist trembled, tipping my large cola cup toward the edge of the tray. Instantly—without me having to say a single word—Katie glided her power chair half a foot to her right, slid her left hand under the corner of my tray to steady it onto the table, pinched open the paper wrapper on my straw, and laced her left fingers through my right hand between our armrests while my wrist tremor settled.

Across the table, Ryan stared at our joined hands as if we had just committed a felony.

"Are you kidding me right now, Katie?" Ryan snapped, setting his burger down on its wrapper and leaning forward across the table. "First you refuse to ride in my car unless your brother comes along, and now you're sitting here holding his hand and opening his straw while you're supposed to be on a date with *me*? Who's number one here—me or your brother?"

The noisy food court seemed to fade into background static around our table. Beside me, Katie didn't flinch, and she didn't let go of my hand.

"Nate is number one in my life, Ryan," Katie said clearly, looking him straight in the eyes. "He is my twin brother. We've been through everything together, and he is always going to come first."

Ryan scoffed, his face flushing red as he turned toward Heather next to him, clearly expecting her to back him up. "Heather, are you seeing this? Doesn't it bother you that your date is sitting here holding his sister's hand and acting like they're glued together?"

Heather looked at Ryan with calm, unmistakable steel in her hazel eyes—and then reached across the tabletop to lay her right hand warmly over my left hand.

"No, Ryan, it doesn't bother me one bit," Heather said firmly. "In fact, I love how close Nate and Katie are."

Ryan blinked at her. "What? Why?"

"Because my fourteen-year-old cousin Wesley—my mom's sister's son—is severely developmentally disabled," Heather explained, her voice steady and proud as she looked from Ryan to Katie and me. "Wesley and I grew up together, and whenever the two of us go anywhere, I hold Wesley's hand, I hug him, and we look out for each other the exact same way Nate and Katie do. If I ever screamed at Nate or got jealous of him for holding his twin sister's hand and hugging her, I would be disrespecting the exact relationship I have with Wesley."

Heather turned her gaze back to me, her eyes shining with so much warmth and affection that my throat went tight.

"I knew before I ever said yes on Wednesday that Nate and Katie travel as a pair," Heather continued softly. "And watching the way they take care of each other only makes me love Nate more."

***

#### Saturday, October 20, 2001, 1:20 PM — Ryan's Escalator Exit & "Did You Bring Two Dates?"

Ryan stared from Heather to Katie and me, his ego visibly bruised beyond repair as he realized neither girl at the table was going to bend to him.

"Whatever," Ryan muttered bitterly, shoving his plastic food-court chair back across the quarry tile and standing up. "If I wanted to play second fiddle to your twin brother and a pair of wheelchairs all afternoon, I would've stayed home. Have fun riding the city bus, Katie."

Heather, Katie, and I watched in silence as Ryan snatched his jacket off the back of his chair.

Beside me, Katie kept her chin up, but I felt the slight tremor in her left hand where her fingers were still laced with mine, and I saw the hurt glistening at the corners of her blue eyes. Even though Katie had stayed guarded with Ryan all week—and even though part of her had figured from the start that this wouldn't work—having a boy walk out on her in the middle of a crowded food court still stung.

And then Ryan did something that turned my blood to ice.

Because our table sat right between the two central escalators—with the up escalator on Katie's and my side, the down escalator right on Heather's and Ryan's side, and the glass elevator back behind us—the entrance to the down escalator was to Ryan's immediate right as he stood up from his chair. Instead of walking back toward the elevator or the upper-level parking-lot doors, Ryan turned to look directly at Katie sitting in her compact power wheelchair, took two steps to his immediate right, and stepped deliberately onto the moving metal steps of the down escalator—riding it down to the first floor in a massive, calculated show of disrespect because he knew Katie's disability made it physically impossible for her to follow him down those stairs.

As the top of Ryan's head sank below the second-floor floorplate on the escalator, a long, quiet breath left Katie's lips.

"I kind of figured before we even got off the bus today that it wouldn't work with him," Katie admitted quietly, blinking back the moisture in her eyes as she looked down at her lap belt. "Still... taking the escalator just to rub it in that I can't follow him was pretty low."

"It wasn't just low, Katie—it was disgusting, and he didn't deserve five minutes of your time," Heather said immediately, stepping around the corner of the table to rest a gentle, supportive hand on Katie's shoulder while I squeezed Katie's left hand with everything I had. "And we are *not* letting him ruin our Saturday. Come on—finish your pretzels, and let's the three of us go walk the second floor of the mall together!"

Katie looked up at Heather in surprise, then glanced at me.

"You're sure you don't mind me tagging along on your date, Heather?" Katie asked softly.

"Are you kidding? You're not tagging along—we're a team today," Heather smiled warmly.

Ten minutes later, after we cleared our trays, the three of us rolled out of the food court and into the wide, sunlit second-floor concourse of Emerald Creek Mall. I rolled down the center of the polished terrazzo walkway in my compact power chair, with Katie rolling right beside my left armrest holding my left hand, and Heather walking right beside my right armrest holding my right hand.

Halfway past the Oak & Iron Outfitters and The Literary Alcove upper-level windows, three guys from our Junior-year History and English classes at Northwood High School—Marcus, Devon, and Chris—came walking out of the music store carrying CD bags.

None of the three had ever had a class with Katie and me at the same time, so they had no idea Katie was my twin sister—and as their eyes landed on me rolling down the mall in my power wheelchair flanked by two pretty sixteen-year-old girls holding both of my hands, all three of them stopped dead in their tracks.

Marcus grinned from ear to ear and gave me an impressed salute.

"Whoa, Prairie—save some for the rest of us, man!" Marcus laughed, nudging Devon's elbow as they walked past. "Did you seriously bring *two* dates to Emerald Creek Mall today?!"

For one second, Heather, Katie, and I blinked at each other—and then all three of us burst out laughing so hard that every bit of the sadness Ryan had left behind vanished into the vaulted ceiling of the mall.

"Tell them yes, Nate!" Heather giggled, squeezing my right hand as Katie laughed beside us. "Let the whole eleventh grade think you're the smoothest guy at Northwood High!"

"With the two prettiest girls in Virginia Beach on either side of my chair," I grinned, looking from Katie's bright, restored smile on my left to Heather's glowing face on my right, "I'm definitely not correcting them."

***

#### Saturday, October 20, 2001, 2:40 PM — A Hug for Me, A Handshake for Katie, & Route 15 Home

We spent the next hour browsing the second-floor music and book aisles at The Literary Alcove together before riding the freestanding glass-enclosed elevator down to the first-floor main concourse at two-forty and rolling out through the first-floor entrance of Wellington's toward the ring road so Katie and I could catch the 2:48 PM northbound CVTA Route 15 bus back up Military Highway.

Just outside the automatic sliding glass doors in the crisp October sunshine, Heather's father pulled up to the pickup curb in his burgundy sedan and gave a friendly wave through the windshield. Fifty feet down the wide concrete sidewalk, the blue-and-yellow CVTA bus stop sign stood waiting for the 2:48 PM bus.

Stopping beside us on the sidewalk, Heather turned to me first. Bending down in front of my power chair, she wrapped both arms warmly around my neck and hugged me close, her cheek pressing against mine for a long, unhurried moment.

"Today was the best first date I've ever had, Nate," Heather whispered in my ear before pulling back with a bright smile. "Same computer table on Monday morning?"

"Same computer table on Monday morning," I promised, my face glowing.

Then Heather turned to Katie's power wheelchair right beside mine. Instead of giving a polite wave from a distance, Heather stepped squarely in front of my twin sister, met Katie's blue eyes with genuine warmth and respect, and extended her right hand.

"It was really great hanging out with you today, Katie," Heather said earnestly. "Next Saturday, maybe the three of us can bring my cousin Wesley along and get pretzels together."

Katie looked at Heather's outstretched hand, and a real, unguarded smile broke across my sister's face as she reached out with her right hand and shook Heather's hand warmly.

"I'd really like that, Heather," Katie said. "See you at school on Monday."

We watched from the sidewalk as Heather climbed into the front passenger seat of her father's car and waved out the window until the sedan merged onto the ring road. As soon as her dad's car turned the corner, Katie and I steered our compact power wheelchairs side by side down the sidewalk to the CVTA Route 15 bus stop and laced our inner hands together while we waited for the 2:48 PM high-floor Beacon V bus to round the loop.

"Well," I said quietly, looking over at my twin sister as the familiar white bus with its horizontal blue-and-green TRT stripes and CVTA door sticker signaled toward our curb, "one out of two first dates wasn't bad."

"Are you kidding, Nate?" Katie smiled, squeezing my hand as the bus pulled up and the operator switched on the Elevate-U hydraulic stairwell wheelchair lift. "Finding out in twenty minutes that Ryan was a jerk—and finding out that Heather loves you *and* gets us completely because of her cousin Wesley? I'd call that a hundred percent success."

# Seventeen, Senior Year, & Greenway Court

## Drunken New Year

### Part 1: The Gas-Station Run, A Box of Dry Cereal, & Why I Never Had a Twenty-First Birthday Drink

**Date:** 2002-12-31 at 21:30 America/New_York

![Seventeen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in the quiet sanctuary of their bedroom holding hands beneath the framed blue butterfly print while watching the New Year's Eve countdown together]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Tuesday, December 31, 2002, 9:30 PM — The Broken Ground Rules & The Gas-Station Run

"Brittany, Dad and Aunt Liz laid down the ground rules before they walked out that front door—nobody is allowed over here tonight, and there is zero alcohol allowed in this house!" I said sharply at nine-thirty on New Year's Eve, sitting in my compact SwiftChair power wheelchair in the front hallway beside Katie's chair as our seventeen-year-old cousin Brittany pulled the front door open to the cold December night.

Tonight was supposed to be simple, quiet, and safe.

It was Tuesday, December 31, 2002—the middle of our Senior year at Northwood High School, just over three months before Katie and I would turn eighteen on April 8. Ever since the Friday afternoon in September of our Junior year when the borrowed time in our legs had finally run out in the hallway at school, both Katie and I had used our compact power wheelchairs full-time from the moment we woke up until we went to bed at night.

Because Grandma had come down with a bad winter bronchitis over Christmas and couldn't travel on the ARTS train, Dad, our Aunt Elizabeth, and our older cousin Chelsea had driven across the Hampton Roads Bridge-Tunnel to Newport News at seven o'clock that evening to check on Grandma, drop off her prescriptions, and have a quiet New Year's Eve dinner with her before driving back after midnight. Meanwhile, our seventeen-year-old cousin Brittany—who was staying over at our house for the second half of winter break—had stayed behind in Virginia Beach with Katie and me. Before Dad and Aunt Liz left with Chelsea, they had stood right in the center of our living room and laid down the law to the three of us: the front door was to stay deadbolted, nobody was allowed inside the house, and Katie, Brittany, and I were supposed to spend New Year's Eve eating pizza, drinking sparkling apple cider, and watching the Times Square ball drop together on television.

Instead, the second Dad's Holt sedan had turned onto Military Highway, Brittany had picked up the cordless kitchen phone.

Now, standing on our aluminum front porch ramp in the porch light were five teenagers Brittany had met earlier that week—along with a tall, scruffy twenty-one-year-old guy named Derek who had just used his vertical driver's license to do a run to the corner gas station out on Military Highway, carrying two heavy plastic grocery bags clinking with glass bottles and cardboard twelve-packs.

"Oh, chill out, Nate—don't be such a pair of babies," Brittany scoffed, waving Derek and the other four kids past our wheelchairs into our small living room. "Uncle David and Aunt Liz won't be back from Grandma's in Newport News until one in the morning. We'll throw the empties in the outside trash bin before midnight and nobody will ever know unless you two snitch."

"This isn't your house, Brittany, it's ours!" Katie said firmly, her left hand reaching across our armrests to grip my right hand as Derek dumped the plastic bags onto our kitchen counter with a loud crash of bottles. "Our dad works his tail off to pay the rent on this place, and we don't want strangers drinking in our living room!"

"Well, they're already here, and Derek already spent forty bucks at the gas station," Brittany shot back, grabbing two plastic cups from the dish rack as one of her friends cranked the volume knob on our living-room stereo up until the bass rattled the window blinds. "If you two don't want to party, go play on your computers in your room and leave us alone."

***

#### Tuesday, December 31, 2002, 11:15 PM — Hiding Behind a Locked Bedroom Door

By eleven-fifteen that night, our living room had devolved into a loud, sour-smelling war zone.

Katie and I had stayed near the hallway threshold as long as we could to make sure nobody broke Dad's things, but as the gas-station bottles emptied over the next ninety minutes, Brittany's guests turned into every kind of drunk at once.

Over on the living-room sofa, one boy in a hooded sweatshirt was already passed out cold with his mouth open and an empty can rolling across the carpet beneath his sneakers, while a girl beside him was slumped sideways over the armrest, half-conscious and groaning that the room was spinning. Near the kitchen doorway, Derek and another guy in a baseball cap had turned mean and belligerent—shouting profanities inches from each other's faces and shoving kitchen chairs out of the way in an angry argument over who owed whom ten dollars for the gas-station run. And right in the middle of the rug, Brittany and her friend Jenna were completely loopy—stumbling into the coffee table, slurring every third word, and cackling at the top of their lungs as they spilled sticky soda and liquor across the floor.

For Katie and me, the smell of stale alcohol drifting down the hallway and the sound of angry, slurred shouting inside our own home hit every raw nerve left behind by our mother on the cruise ship sixteen months earlier.

When Derek slammed his palm against the kitchen wall so hard that the framed family photos in the hallway crooked on their nails, Katie looked at me with wide eyes and gripped my wrist.

"Nate, I don't want to be out here near them anymore," Katie whispered over the roar of the stereo and the shouting. "If Derek or that other guy trips over our wheelchairs while they're fighting, neither of us can stand up to get out of the way."

"Grab that unopened box of dry cereal off the lower pantry shelf so we have food for the rest of the night and don't have to come back out here," I told her immediately, snagging two cans of ginger ale on my lap tray while Katie tucked the family-size box of toasted oat cereal onto her lap. "We're locking ourselves in your room until Dad gets back."

As we backed our two compact power wheelchairs down the short hallway toward Katie's bedroom at the back corner of the house, one of Brittany's guests—a loudmouth senior from Northwood High named Tyler who had been chugging out of a plastic cup by the stereo—spotted us retreating and stumbled down the hall after us.

"Hey! Where do you two think you're rolling off to?" Tyler slurred, thrusting a red plastic cup toward my face as I steered Katie inside the bedroom ahead of me. "Come on, Prairie, stop acting like you're too good for the rave and take a drink!"

"We don't drink, Tyler—stay out of our hallway," I told him flatly.

Before Tyler could take another step, I backed my power chair across the threshold into Katie's bedroom, swung the solid wooden bedroom door shut right in his flushed, angry face, twisted the brass privacy lock until it clicked, and wedged the heavy rubber doorstop beneath the bottom gap.

On the other side of the oak panel, Tyler banged his fist twice against the door and yelled spitefully over the bass of the stereo, *"Fine, lock yourselves in a bedroom all night, you stuck-up losers! See if anybody cares!"*

For good measure, once Katie and I used our armrests to transfer onto the edge of her bed side by side beneath the framed blue butterfly print from our old house, I used my joystick to inch my fifty-pound power wheelchair right up against the inside of the doorknob like a steel roadblock.

Even through the closed door, we could still hear the muffled thudding of boots, the angry shouting from the kitchen, and the loopy, off-key shrieking from the living room.

Pulling Katie's green-and-gold quilt around both of our shoulders, I opened the top flaps of the cardboard cereal box between us so we had handfuls of dry cereal to munch on for dinner, switched the small thirteen-inch television on her dresser to the GBS New Year's Eve broadcast from Times Square with the volume turned low, and wrapped my right arm around my twin sister's shoulders while my left hand held hers.

***

#### Wednesday, January 1, 2003, 12:00 AM — Why I Never Had a Twenty-First Birthday Drink

"Ten minutes to midnight," Katie murmured, resting her blonde head against my shoulder as the crowd in Times Square cheered on the small screen and another loud crash of glass echoed from the kitchen on the other side of our locked bedroom door. "Can you believe we have to hide in a locked bedroom in our own house on New Year's Eve because Brittany couldn't follow one simple rule?"

"Dad is going to hit the roof when he pulls into the driveway," I said quietly, glancing at the two unopened cans of ginger ale we had brought into the bedroom with us on our lap trays before the party got out of hand.

For a long moment, I sat there listening to the contrast between the two sides of that bedroom door: out in the living room, kids our age were passed out on the floor, screaming at each other in drunken rage, or stumbling around out of their minds—while in here, Katie and I were safe, clear-headed, and holding onto each other.

Up until that exact night at seventeen years old—four months shy of my eighteenth birthday in April—I had always assumed that when it came to alcohol, I was just waiting until my twenty-first birthday like everybody else to have my first legal drink. I was a rule-follower by nature; I didn't break laws, so I had figured that once I turned twenty-one, I'd probably try a glass of beer or wine just to see what the big deal was.

Sitting on the edge of that bed with Katie's hand in mine, listening to Derek's slurred shouting and Brittany's loopy giggling through the drywall—and remembering the look on our mother's face on Deck 4 of the *Sunward Tropics* right before she landed in the ship's brig—something settled permanently inside my chest.

"Katie?" I said softly as the digital clock on the television screen ticked to 11:59 PM and the glittering crystal ball began its sixty-second descent down the flagpole in Times Square.

"Yeah, Nate?" Katie asked, looking up at me.

"You know how I always said I was waiting until my twenty-first birthday to have my first legal drink?" I said, popping the tabs on our two cold cans of ginger ale and handing one to my sister. "Listening to what's going on in our living room tonight—some of them passed out cold, some of them furious and trying to fight each other, some of them acting like they lost half their brains—I don't ever want to end up like that. Not even once. When we turn twenty-one, I'm not having a twenty-first birthday drink. I'm never touching the stuff for the rest of my life."

Katie looked at the can of ginger ale in her hand, then looked up into my eyes with a fierce, unmistakable pride.

"Me neither, Nate," Katie said, clinking her ginger ale can gently against mine just as *12:00 AM — HAPPY NEW YEAR 2003* flashed across the small screen and confetti snowed over Times Square. "Why would either one of us ever put something in our bodies that turns people into *that*—when we can stay sharp, write our code, and take care of each other?"

"Happy New Year, Katie," I whispered, setting my ginger ale on the nightstand and pulling my twin sister into a tight, two-armed hug under the quilt.

"Happy New Year, Twin Brother," Katie whispered back, hugging me tight while the noises in the living room finally began to stumble toward the front door—just twenty minutes before Dad's headlights swept up the driveway and Brittany's New Year's Eve privileges came to a permanent, unceremonious end.

### Part 2: The Drunken New Year Lie, Heather's Bitter Goodbye, & Twin Sisters Always Stay

**Date:** 2003-01-06 at 08:05 America/New_York

![Seventeen-year-old blonde fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sit side by side in the quiet sanctuary of Katie's bedroom beneath the framed blue butterfly print as Katie comforts her heartbroken brother]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Monday, January 6, 2003, 8:05 AM — Blindsided Outside Room 204

"Save it, Nathaniel—don't you dare roll up to my locker with that innocent smile on your face and pretend you didn't do anything wrong!" Heather Collins snapped at five minutes past eight on Monday morning, slamming her locker door shut so hard that the metal vents rattled down the second-floor hallway of Northwood High School.

My right thumb froze on the joystick of my compact SwiftChair power wheelchair, bringing my chair to an abrupt halt three feet from her locker outside Room 204.

It was Monday, January 6, 2003—the very first morning back at school after winter break. Because Heather's parents had taken her and her cousin Wesley up to the Shenandoah Valley to visit her grandparents between Christmas and New Year's Day, I hadn't seen her in person for over a week. All morning long on the CVTA Route 15 bus ride and up the school elevator with Katie, I had been grinning from ear to ear, carrying a small folded note in my jacket pocket asking Heather if she wanted to meet Katie and me at Emerald Creek Mall that coming Saturday afternoon.

Instead, as Katie rolled her power chair up beside mine in the busy hallway before first period, I found myself staring into a face I barely recognized.

The warm, gentle girl who had held my hand on the second-floor bridge of the mall, who had defended my bond with my twin sister because of how fiercely she loved her cousin Wesley, was staring down at me with hard, glassy eyes and a jaw clenched so tight her chin was trembling.

"Heather, what's wrong?" I asked, completely blindsided as my stomach dropped into my shoes. "Did something happen over break? I don't even know what you're talking about!"

"Oh, you don't know what I'm talking about?" Heather laughed—a short, jagged, bitter sound that made several students walking past us turn their heads. "Really, Nate? Look down the hall by the stairwell!"

I glanced over my shoulder toward the east stairwell. Leaning against the cinderblock wall in his varsity jacket with a smug, spiteful smirk plastered across his face was Tyler—the loudmouth senior who had stumbled down our hallway with a red plastic cup on New Year's Eve and banged his fist on Katie's bedroom door when we refused to join Brittany's drunken rave.

"Tyler was in my homeroom ten minutes ago bragging to half the senior class about the wild New Year's Eve rave at your house off Military Highway," Heather said, her voice shaking with a cold, venomous fury that cut straight through the hallway chatter. "He told everybody how you got yourself and some girl wicked drunk on gas-station liquor, locked the two of you inside a back bedroom with a chair wedged against the doorknob, and spent the whole night hooking up behind a locked door while the party went on outside!"

For two full seconds, my brain couldn't even process the sheer absurdity of what I was hearing.

"What?!" I gasped, staring at her in disbelief. "Heather, that's a complete lie! Neither one of us drank a single drop of alcohol!"

"And you know what the worst part is, Nate?" Heather cut me off, her voice cracking with bitter humiliation as angry tears welled in the corners of her eyes. "When Tyler first started laughing in homeroom about how you locked yourself in a back bedroom all night with a girl, I actually defended you! I immediately assumed you were in that room with *Katie*—because I know the two of you always stick together and look out for each other! I looked right at Tyler in front of everybody and said, *'That was his twin sister Katie, you idiot!'* And Tyler looked me dead in the eye, laughed in my face, and told the whole room it *wasn't* Katie—that it was some blonde girl from the party who got wasted with you before you locked the door in his face!"

Beside me, Katie's blue eyes went wide with outrage.

"Heather, Tyler *only* saw the back of my blonde head from the hallway before Nate shut the door in his drunken face!" Katie spoke up immediately, rolling her power wheelchair six inches forward to put herself right alongside me. "It *was* me in that bedroom! Our cousin Brittany broke our dad's ground rules and invited strangers with alcohol into our house while our dad, our aunt, and our cousin Chelsea were in Newport News! Nate and I grabbed an unopened box of dry cereal out of the pantry so we'd have food to eat and locked ourselves in my bedroom all night just to stay safe and away from them! Tyler was furious that Nate wouldn't drink with him or join the rave—he's lying to get back at us!"

I reached into my jacket pocket with a shaking hand, desperate to make Heather hear the truth.

"Heather, please, listen to me—Katie's telling the absolute truth!" I pleaded, my chest aching as if someone had dropped a cinderblock onto my sternum. "We ate dry toasted oat cereal out of a cardboard box and drank two cans of ginger ale while watching the ball drop on a thirteen-inch TV! I literally promised Katie at midnight that I'm never even having a twenty-first birthday drink because watching Tyler and those kids act like drunken idiots disgusted me so much! You know me, Heather—how could you ever believe I would get drunk or cheat on you?"

Slowly, painfully, I realized from the cold, shuttered look on Heather's face that the damage was already done.

Sitting in front of twenty kids in homeroom while Tyler laughed in her face and told everyone her boyfriend was locked in a bedroom all night with another girl had humiliated her to the core—and once that bitter poison had taken root in her chest, no amount of truth about a cardboard box of dry cereal was going to undo it.

"Save it for somebody gullible enough to buy it, Nathaniel," Heather said coldly, stepping back from our two wheelchairs as the five-minute warning bell rang overhead. "I stuck up for you in front of everybody because I thought you were different from every other guy at this school, and you made me look like a complete fool. Don't call my house, don't wait for me at the bus loop, and don't ever talk to me again. We're done."

Before I could get another word out of my throat, Heather spun on her heel, walked briskly down the hallway past Tyler—who flashed me one last triumphant, nasty grin before disappearing into his classroom—and vanished around the corner toward the science wing.

***

#### Monday, January 6, 2003, 3:45 PM — Roles Reversed Under the Blue Butterfly

All through first period, lunch, and afternoon classes, I felt like I was moving underwater.

What hurt more than anything wasn't just that my very first relationship had ended in less than sixty seconds on a Monday morning—it was the crushing, helpless injustice of being condemned for the exact opposite of who I was. I had followed every rule my father had laid down; I had protected my twin sister from a living room full of reckless drunks; I had sworn off alcohol for the rest of my life; and yet because one angry liar wanted revenge for having a bedroom door closed in his face, the girl I had been head over heels in love with for a year and a half now hated my guts without ever knowing I was innocent.

By the time the CVTA Route 15 Beacon V bus lowered us down the Elevate-U hydraulic stairwell lift onto the sidewalk along Military Highway at three-thirty that afternoon and we rolled up the aluminum ramp into our house, the knot in my throat was so tight I could barely breathe.

Without saying a word, Katie steered her power chair ahead of me down the short hallway into her bedroom—the exact same room where we had hidden six nights earlier—and patted the edge of her mattress beneath the framed blue butterfly print on the wall.

Just like we had done on that afternoon in seventh grade when Chad had dumped Katie in the cafeteria of Bayside Middle School—except with our roles completely reversed—we locked our wheelchair brakes side by side and transferred onto the edge of her bed.

As soon as I sat down, I buried my face in my hands, my shoulders shaking as the bitter sting of Heather's final words replayed in my ears.

Beside me, Katie didn't lecture me or tell me to tough it out. Instead, she wrapped both of her arms tightly around my shoulders, pulled my head down against her shoulder, and rested her cheek against my blonde hair while her hand rubbed slow, steady circles across my back.

"It isn't fair, Katie," I choked out against the sleeve of her sweater, my voice breaking in the quiet bedroom. "I didn't even know what she was talking about when I rolled up to her locker. We were sitting on this exact bed eating dry cereal out of a box to stay away from Tyler's booze, and she thinks I betrayed her."

"I know, Nate. I was right there with you every single second," Katie whispered fiercely, holding me with every ounce of strength in her seventeen-year-old arms. "Tyler is a vindictive jerk who couldn't stand that you wouldn't act like an idiot with him, and if Heather threw away everything she knew about your character over one drunk liar's story without even listening to the two of us, then she didn't deserve my brother's heart in the first place."

I lifted my head, wiping my eyes with the back of my wrist as I looked at my twin sister.

In seventh grade, when Chad had walked out on Katie and left her crying in the middle school hallway, I had held her right under that same framed blue butterfly print and promised her that I would never leave her behind. Now, five years later at seventeen—with both of our power wheelchairs parked side by side at the foot of the bed—Katie reached out, laced the fingers of her left hand tightly through my right hand, and gave me the exact same unbreakable anchor.

"Remember what you told me in seventh grade when Chad broke my heart, Nate?" Katie said softly, her blue eyes locked onto mine. "Boyfriends and girlfriends can walk away over a rumor in a sixty-second hallway conversation. Twin Sisters never walk away. You and I know the truth about who you are, and we've got each other for life."

Taking a long, steadying breath, I squeezed Katie's hand back and leaned my head against her shoulder until the shaking in my chest finally subsided—knowing beyond a shadow of a doubt that as we headed toward high school graduation and our adult lives together, the only person in the world I would ever completely trust was sitting right beside me.

## Greenway Court, Apartment A6 & The Square Closet Sanctuary

### Part 1: Apartment A6 on Greenway Court, Dad's Microwave Cart, & Across-the-Hall Neighbors

**Date:** 2003-07-16 at 07:40 America/New_York

![Eighteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie in their compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs inside their first studio apartment on Greenway Court in West Ghent, Norfolk]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Wednesday, July 16, 2003, 7:40 AM — Dad's Rolling Cart & Our Own Set of Keys in Apartment A6

"Careful with the power cord on the toaster, Nate—tuck it through the back wire loop so it can't snag on your armrest when you turn around by the stove," our father said cheerfully at twenty minutes to eight on Wednesday morning, tightening the last chrome corner bolt on the two-shelf rolling utility cart he had just assembled beside our tiny kitchenette counter.

Sitting side by side in our compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs inside Apartment A6, Katie and I watched with huge grins as Dad lifted our new countertop microwave onto the top shelf of the cart, set our two-slice chrome toaster right next to it, and plugged both cords into the wall outlet above the speckled laminate counter.

"Look at the clearance on that," Katie said happily, rolling her wheelchair forward to test the reach. Without leaning an inch off her seat cushion, she popped open the microwave door with her left hand and tapped the toaster lever with her right. "Now we still have the whole counter square above the dorm fridge free for making sandwiches!"

"That's the whole idea," Dad smiled, wiping his forehead with the back of his sleeve as the through-wall box air conditioner mounted into the red brick directly below our wide, three-pane white-framed rear window hummed steadily in the July heat. "When a studio kitchen only gives you a four-burner stove, a single sink, and an under-the-counter dorm refrigerator, you two need every square inch of prep space at seated height."

We had turned eighteen on April eighth and rolled across the graduation stage of Northwood High School a month ago in June. Because our estranged mother Brenda had spent the spring calling our dad's house in Virginia Beach and threatening to track us down the second we turned eighteen and were no longer covered by the family-court custody order, Dad had quietly gone apartment hunting across the city line in the tree-lined West Ghent neighborhood of Norfolk.

At the beginning of July, Dad had introduced us to Dave and Deb—the husband-and-wife landlords who owned this quiet, two-story flat-roofed red-brick building on Greenway Court, where Apartments A1 through A10 sat downstairs on the first floor and Apartments B1 through B10 sat upstairs on the second floor. Even better, Dad had signed the lease and paid the rent for Apartment A6 himself so that Katie and I could finally have a safe, independent home to call our own as eighteen-year-old college freshmen.

Every detail of the building and our studio fit our daily lives like a glove. Out front, beneath the black lamppost on the grass walkway, the building's recessed glass-and-aluminum main entrance door stayed locked twenty-four hours a day and required our brass apartment key to open—meaning nobody from the street could wander into the interior hallway uninvited. Just inside the front lobby past the row of locking metal mailboxes, Dave had bolted down a short, nonslip aluminum modular ramp over the three shallow rubber-treaded lobby steps so Katie and I could roll our power wheelchairs straight up into the first-floor hallway without a bump.

Down at the very back of the first-floor hallway—directly opposite Apartment A5—our wooden door opened into Apartment A6. All twenty apartments in the building shared the exact same enlarged bedroom-style rectangular studio layout, with units on opposite sides of the hallway like Apartment A5 and Apartment A6 built as exact mirror images of each other, giving us plenty of open floor space for two wheelchairs to pivot side by side. Against the side wall stood the full-sized bed we shared and our long workstation desk holding our shared desktop computer tower and seventeen-inch CRT monitor. Along the front corner sat our small kitchenette with its stove, sink, under-the-counter dorm fridge, and Dad's brand-new microwave-and-toaster cart. Tucked off to the side were two doors: one leading into our square walk-in closet with its soft carpeted floor, and the other opening into an accessible bathroom equipped with a roll-under sink, a toilet, and a standing shower where Dad had placed a sturdy padded transfer bench and a handheld shower sprayer within easy reach.

"Alright, you two, I've got to get across the river to work before traffic backs up on Interstate 264," Dad said, picking up his tool bag and kissing the top of Katie's head before squeezing my shoulder. "Remember, keep the front lobby door latched behind you whenever you go out to catch the Route 2 bus on Hampton Boulevard, and call me tonight after you test out the microwave."

"Thank you for the cart, Dad—and for giving us our own place," I said, rolling beside Katie to walk him to the door.

"You two earned it," Dad said warmly. "Have a great morning."

***

#### Wednesday, July 16, 2003, 7:48 AM — Familiar Faces Across the Hall in Apartment A5

"Come on, Nate—let's roll out with Dad as far as the front sidewalk while the morning air is still cool," Katie suggested, slipping her brass Greenway Court key lanyard around her wrist. "I want to see how the cell signal is out by the grassy field across the street before we call CCHR about our fall books."

"Good call," I agreed, glancing at the single bar of reception on my flip phone—the thick red-brick walls of the Greenway Court building blocked half the cellular towers in West Ghent whenever we were inside Apartment A6.

I pulled our wooden apartment door open, rolled my SwiftChair out into the quiet first-floor hallway right behind Dad and Katie, and turned to pull our deadbolt shut.

At that exact second, the heavy deadbolt of Apartment A5—directly across the narrow back hallway from our door—let out a loud metallic thunk.

The door to Apartment A5 swung open, and out stepped a slender eighteen-year-old guy with reddish-blond hair, wire-rimmed glasses, and a stiff, slightly uneven gait in his legs, flanked closely by an athletic eighteen-year-old girl with a protective posture and her hand steadying his elbow.

For two full seconds, all four of us froze in the back hallway between Apartment A5 and Apartment A6.

I stared at the guy's wire-rimmed glasses and the unmistakable way he stood shoulder-to-shoulder with the girl beside him. He stared down at our two compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs, then up at Katie's and my matching platinum-blonde hair.

"Wait a second," I blinked, my jaw dropping as nine years of neighborhood and school memories clicked into place at once. "Michael? Michael Ragsdale?!"

Behind his wire-rimmed glasses, Michael's eyes went wide with genuine shock, and the tight, guarded tension in his shoulders vanished on the spot.

"Nathaniel? Katie?!" Michael said, recognizing us immediately from [three doors down on Delaney Street and our childhood two-week summer camp at Virginia Dominion University](https://books.raggiesoft.com/katie/early-childhood/summer-camp-at-virginia-dominion-university/the-parallel-pair-dominion-mall-the-vdu-fieldhouse)—as well as the hallways of Northwood High School, where all of us had just graduated a month earlier. "You two live in A6?!"

"We just moved in at the start of the month!" Katie beamed, rolling her wheelchair half a foot closer to shake hands with Michael and with Ashley Raybourn, whom we recognized from our graduating class at Northwood High. "Our dad rented A6 for the two of us so we'd have our own place near the Route 2 bus line! Don't tell me you two just moved into A5?!"

"We literally got the keys from Deb last night," Ashley laughed, a huge, relieved smile breaking across her face as she looked from Katie and me to our dad. "[We were just heading outside across the street to the grassy field so we could get three bars of cell service to call Michael's mom before the moving truck gets here at nine](https://books.raggiesoft.com/rachel/michaels-senior-year/the-extraction/the-morning-perimeter)."

"That's the exact reason we were rolling outside right now!" I grinned, holding up my flip phone with its lone, stubborn bar of signal. "The brick walls in the back of the building eat every cell signal alive!"

Dad chuckled warmly, shaking Michael's and Ashley's hands in the hallway before we all headed down the corridor together, rolled smoothly down Dave's nonslip aluminum ramp over the three rubber-coated lobby steps, and pushed out through the locked glass-and-aluminum front entrance door into the sunny July morning.

While Dad waved goodbye from his pickup truck in the decal-controlled parking lot, Katie and I rolled across the quiet street with Michael and Ashley to the open grassy field where neighbors were walking their dogs among the slatted wooden benches. Parking our two power wheelchairs right flush against the ends of the wooden bench where Michael and Ashley sat pressed side by side, we spent the next twenty minutes catching up like old teammates—talking about how Michael's sisters Jessica and Rachel were out in Washington State, how Katie and I were starting our freshman classes together at the Community College of Hampton Roads on the bus, and how relieved both pairs of us were to have a locked front building door and a quiet rectangular studio where nobody could bother us.

When we finally waved to Michael and Ashley as they headed off to meet Michael's mom and the moving truck, Katie and I rolled back across Greenway Court, unlocked the glass front door with our brass key, and wheeled back into the cool, air-conditioned sanctuary of Apartment A6.

"Can you believe that, Nate?" Katie smiled as the deadbolt clicked shut behind us and the aroma of fresh toast drifted from our new rolling cart in the kitchenette. "Out of every apartment building in the entire city of Norfolk, our old neighbor from Delaney Street and VDU summer camp is right across the hall from us in A5."

"Best across-the-hall neighbors we could ever ask for," I agreed, parking my wheelchair beside hers at our shared computer workstation desk.

### Part 2: Brenda in the Back Alley, Hiding in the Square Closet, & A Happy Year Together

**Date:** 2003-08-09 at 20:45 America/New_York

![Eighteen-year-old fraternal twins Katie and Nathaniel Prairie sitting side by side in their first studio apartment on Greenway Court in West Ghent, Norfolk]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Saturday, August 9, 2003, 8:45 PM — Footsteps Behind the Exterior Iron Stairs & Hiding in the Square Closet

"Wait—turn the speakers down, Nate," Katie whispered sharply at a quarter to nine on Saturday evening, her hand dropping onto my wrist over the computer mouse. "Listen."

I clicked the mute icon on our seventeen-inch CRT monitor at the shared workstation desk, leaving only the low, steady hum of the through-wall box air conditioner mounted into the brick below our wide three-pane rear window.

Outside in the narrow first-floor back alley—right behind the black iron exterior fire-escape staircase that climbed the rear brick wall of 801 Greenway Court beside the bushes and trash bins—an angry, slurred woman's voice echoed off the brick.

"I know their father rented them an apartment in this building!" our estranged mother Brenda's voice rasped in the humid August dusk, followed by the crunch of her shoes on the gravel behind the iron stairs. "Stupid locked front door won't even let a mother into the lobby to see her own eighteen-year-old kids! Katie! Nathaniel! Which window is yours?!"

My blood turned to ice water in my veins. Beside me at the computer desk, Katie's fingers clamped around my forearm so tightly I could feel her pulse racing against my skin.

Even though we had kept the white horizontal mini-blinds tilted shut over the wide three-pane window above our through-wall air conditioner, Apartment A6 sat on the first floor at the very back of the building. If Brenda stepped two paces past the black iron exterior stairs and pressed her face against the glass beside the bushes, even the faintest glow from our desk lamp or our computer monitor would tell her immediately that Apartment A6 was ours—and she would bang on our back window and scream in the alley all night long.

"Kill the lamp and the monitor," Katie breathed silently in my ear, her voice barely above a whisper. "If the room is pitch dark and dead silent when she looks through the blinds, she'll think the apartment is empty."

In less than three seconds, I reached behind the tower to flip the master rocker switch on our surge protector—plunging the CRT monitor and the desk lamp into instant darkness—while Katie clicked off the small stove hood light over the kitchenette.

Outside in the back alley, Brenda's footsteps crunched closer toward the iron stairs, her knuckles rapping sharply against one of the rear brick window frames down the wall.

"The square closet," Katie whispered, pointing through the shadows toward our open closet door. "Grab the sleeping bag and the pillows off the bed—if we're inside the closet with the door closed, she won't see a single shadow or hear our chairs even if she shines a flashlight through the window slats."

Moving in silent, practiced twin synchronization, I pulled our soft navy-blue sleeping bag and two pillows off the full-sized bed while Katie guided us across the dark studio carpet to the doorway of our square walk-in closet. Quickly spreading the sleeping bag and pillows flat across the square carpeted floor inside the closet, Katie and I parked our two compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs side by side just outside the closet frame, switched both joystick controllers completely off so their green battery-level LEDs wouldn't glow in the dark, and transferred smoothly down onto the closet floor.

Reaching up from inside the shared sleeping bag, I pulled the solid wooden closet door shut until the latch clicked softly into place.

Inside the square closet, the darkness was total and absolute.

Katie immediately turned on her side inside the sleeping bag, wrapped both arms tightly around my shoulders, and pulled my head against her chest while I wrapped my arms around her waist—holding each other with every ounce of steady, deep-pressure warmth we had so neither of us would tremble or make a sound.

Thirty seconds later, through the muffled layers of the closed closet door and the rear brick wall, we heard Brenda's footsteps stop right behind the exterior iron staircase outside our back window, just above our humming through-wall air conditioner.

We heard her fingernails tap twice against the exterior glass of Apartment A6. We heard her mutter a bitter curse under her breath when she saw nothing but dark, empty blinds and heard nothing over the drone of the air-conditioning compressor. Two minutes later, her footsteps retreated down the back alley toward Raleigh Avenue, and the distant slam of a car door told us she had finally given up and driven away.

Even after the alley went quiet, neither Katie nor I moved to open the closet door.

Wrapped together inside our shared sleeping bag on the carpeted floor of that small, enclosed square closet, something remarkable happened to our nineteen-thousand-rpm fight-or-flight nervous systems. Out in the open studio room, the streetlamp shadows on the blinds and the knowledge that someone could walk down the back alley always left a tiny background thread of vigilance running in our autistic brains. Inside the enclosed four walls of the square closet, zipped up together in the velvet dark with Katie's arms locked securely around me, the outside world couldn't touch us at all.

"Are you okay, Nate?" Katie whispered against my hair in the pitch dark, her hand rubbing slow, soothing circles between my shoulder blades.

"More than okay," I whispered back, letting out a long, sleepy breath as every tense muscle in my back and legs finally melted into the carpet. "It's so quiet in here, Katie. I actually feel completely safe."

"Me too," Katie murmured, resting her chin against my forehead as a soft yawn escaped her lips. "We don't have to move back out to the bed tonight if you don't want to. Let's just stay right here in our little fortress."

Resting my head against my twin sister's shoulder inside our shared sleeping bag on the square closet floor, I closed my eyes—and for the first time since moving out on our own, both of us slept nine uninterrupted hours straight through the night without waking up once.

***

#### Wednesday, November 12, 2003, 5:30 PM — Dad's Microwave Cart, Shared Code, & A Happy Year in Our Studio Sanctuary

By late autumn and winter of our eighteenth year, life inside Apartment A6 on Greenway Court had settled into the happiest, most peaceful routine Katie and I had ever known.

Every weekday morning, after waking up well-rested—often still curled up together inside our shared sleeping bag on the carpeted floor of our square closet whenever a thunderstorm or a stressful exam week made us crave that pitch-dark sanctuary—we used the padded transfer bench in our accessible shower, brushed our teeth side by side at the roll-under bathroom sink, and rolled out to our compact kitchenette. While I popped four slices of whole-wheat bread into the toaster on Dad's rolling utility cart and pulled the milk carton and butter out of the under-the-counter dorm fridge, Katie scrambled eggs on the four-burner stove, and we washed our plates and skillet in the sink the minute we finished eating.

Whenever we headed out our door and down Dave's nonslip aluminum ramp through the locked front lobby, we waved to Michael Ragsdale and his one-eyed black Lab Archie across the hall in Apartment A5 before rolling down Greenway Court and Redgate Avenue to Hampton Boulevard to catch the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority Route 2 bus into Downtown Norfolk. Side by side in the front wheelchair bays of the bus, we rode ten minutes downtown to the Martin Building at the Community College of Hampton Roads Norfolk campus for our morning lectures, grabbed hot burgers and fries upstairs at the third-floor food court of The Admiral Center on Fridays, and rode the afternoon Route 2 bus right back home to West Ghent.

In the evenings, while the radiators and the through-wall fan kept our rectangular studio cozy and warm, Katie and I parked our SwiftChairs side by side at our long workstation desk in front of our shared desktop computer. Taking turns at the keyboard while the other sister or brother read aloud from our textbooks or debugged lines of HTML and Quantum Basic code on the seventeen-inch CRT monitor, we had everything we needed under one roof: four locked walls, a full kitchen cart, our own bus passes, and each other.

## The Admiral Center Bookstore & Meeting Tiffany

### Part 1: Death by Cascading Style Sheets & Iced Cocoa at Chapters & Co.

**Date:** 2004-05-14 at 15:15 America/New_York

![Nineteen-year-old fraternal twins Nate and Katie Prairie in their compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs inside the computer books aisle of Chapters & Co. at The Admiral Center in Downtown Norfolk]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Friday, May 14, 2004, 3:15 PM — The Computer Books Aisle at Chapters & Co.

"Two tall iced cocoas with extra whipped cream, coming right up—go pick out the web-design book you want for the summer, Nate, and I'll meet you in the computer aisle!" Katie called out cheerfully over her shoulder, steering her compact SwiftChair power wheelchair up to the lowered marble counter of the café inside Chapters & Co. on the ground floor of The Admiral Center.

"Deal!" I grinned, turning my matching power wheelchair down the wide carpeted bookstore corridor toward the Computer & Internet shelves.

Katie and I had just turned nineteen a month earlier on April eighth, and twenty minutes ago we had walked—or rather, rolled—out of our final spring-semester exam across the street at the Downtown Norfolk campus of the Community College of Hampton Roads. Nearly a full year had passed since Dad had rented Apartment A6 on Greenway Court for the two of us, and life in our West Ghent studio had settled into the happiest, most comfortable rhythm we had ever known. Every weekday morning, Katie and I rode the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority Route 2 bus down Hampton Boulevard to our college classes; every afternoon, we rolled home to our locked red-brick building, waved to Michael in Apartment A5 across the hall whenever we crossed paths by the lobby mailboxes, made honey-ham sandwiches beside Dad's rolling microwave cart, and spent our evenings side by side at our shared desktop computer writing HTML websites.

Braking my wheelchair between the towering oak bookshelves of the programming section, I scanned the rows of thick technical manuals until my eyes landed on a fat, eight-hundred-page paperback near the upper-middle shelf titled *Cascading Style Sheets & Dynamic Web Design*.

It sat just an inch higher than my normal seated reach. Checking to make sure my wheels were locked, I braced my left hand on my armrest pad and stretched my right arm up from my seat cushion, hooking my index finger over the top edge of the spine to slide the heavy book out.

As I tilted the paperback forward, a second copy that had been wedged crookedly beside it slipped loose from the shelf and tipped straight toward my lap.

Before I could pull my hand back to catch both volumes at once, someone stepped smoothly into the aisle beside my right wheel and caught the falling book cleanly in midair with both hands.

"Whoa—careful! Death by cascading style sheets is a terrible way to start the weekend," a warm, playful voice laughed right beside me.

I blinked and looked up. Standing next to my wheelchair was a girl who looked to be about twenty, with a heavier build, shoulder-length dark reddish-brown hair cut with neat bangs across her forehead, and black-rimmed rectangular glasses framing a bright, friendly smile. Tucking the rescued copy back onto the shelf with one hand, she held out the copy I had been reaching for with the other.

"Here you go," she said warmly, handing the heavy paperback right into my hands at chest height without a trace of awkwardness. "I saw that second one teetering on the edge from the end of the aisle."

"Thank you," I said, feeling my ears warm slightly as my social scripts scrambled to catch up. Since Heather Collins had dumped me in the hallway of Northwood High School back in January of our senior year over my cousin Brittany's New Year's Eve party rumor, I hadn't spoken to a girl my age outside of college group projects. "I didn't realize the two copies were stuck together."

"Those thick computer books are practically bricks," an older woman with a kind, maternal smile agreed as she stepped up behind the girl, carrying a bookstore shopping bag over her forearm.

"Oh, I'm Tiffany, by the way—Tiffany Sloan," the girl said, adjusting her black-rimmed glasses with a small, endearing laugh before extending her hand to shake mine. "And this is my mom, Sandra."

"I'm Nathaniel—Nate Prairie," I said, shaking Tiffany's hand and nodding politely to her mother.

Instead of turning away the way most strangers did after a polite aisle interaction, Tiffany glanced down at the cover of *Cascading Style Sheets & Dynamic Web Design* resting across my lap, and her eyes lit up behind her glasses.

"Wait—do you actually know how to build websites?" Tiffany asked, sounding genuinely impressed. "Like, writing the real code from scratch?"

Speaking about HTML and CSS was the one topic on earth that bypassed every ounce of my shyness.

"Yeah, my twin sister and I have been hand-coding HTML since we were in seventh grade at Morrison Middle School," I said, my posture brightening immediately. "We've been using HTML tables for page layouts, and we're switching everything over to CSS positioning this summer now that spring finals at CCHR are done."

"No way!" Tiffany beamed. "I've been trying to put together a simple little webpage on my computer at home for weeks, and every time I try to line up a picture next to a paragraph, the whole page jumps to the bottom of the screen! I came over to this aisle looking for a beginner book, and half of them look like they're written in ancient Greek."

"That's usually just an unclosed table cell tag or an image alignment attribute," I explained eagerly, opening the paperback on my lap to a diagram of floating div containers. "Once you see how container boxes work, it's super logical."

At that exact moment, the quiet hum of electric wheelchair motors rounded the corner of the bookshelf, and Katie rolled into the aisle balancing a cardboard four-cup carrier on her lap with two tall plastic cups of iced cocoa topped with swirled domes of whipped cream.

"Got our celebratory cocoas, Nate—zero coffee, extra chocolate drizzle!" Katie announced happily before spotting Tiffany and Sandra standing beside my chair. "Oh! Hi!"

Out of pure lifelong habit, I braced myself for the micro-expression we saw from ninety percent of the public whenever they realized there were two of us in identical compact power wheelchairs.

Tiffany didn't blink, stare, or take a step back. Instead, her face broke into an even wider, delighted smile.

"Katie, this is Tiffany Sloan and her mom, Sandra," I introduced them proudly. "Tiffany just saved me from getting clobbered by a falling eight-hundred-page CSS manual. Tiffany, Sandra—this is my twin sister, Katie."

"Hi, Katie! It is so nice to meet you!" Tiffany said, shaking Katie's free hand with warm enthusiasm. "Nate was literally just telling me that the two of you have been coding websites together since seventh grade and just finished your freshman finals at CCHR!"

"Last exam turned in twenty minutes ago," Katie grinned, immediately warming to Tiffany's friendly, unhurried energy. "And knowing my brother, he was already explaining unclosed HTML tags before he even said hello."

Tiffany laughed out loud, looking between the two of us with genuine admiration. "Are you kidding? I think it's amazing! Wait—do you two take all your classes together too?"

"Every single one," Katie said proudly, glancing over at me. "And we share a studio apartment over in West Ghent on Greenway Court."

"Are you serious?! That is so cool!" Tiffany said, placing a hand over her heart. "My younger brother and I are super close too. Honestly, I always say family comes first, no matter what. When a brother and sister look out for each other the way you two do, that shows what kind of heart a person has. If a guy isn't close with his sister, that's a giant red flag in my book!"

Beside me, I saw Katie's shoulders relax completely, a bright, unmistakable smile of approval lighting up her blue eyes. To both of us, hearing someone say out loud that a close brother-and-sister bond was something wonderful—rather than something strange—felt like passing the most important test in the world.

***

#### Friday, May 14, 2004, 3:35 PM — Café Chairs, A Sudden May Cloudburst, & The Umbrella over Our Joysticks

"Well, if you two aren't in a rush to catch your bus right this second, why don't we sit down in the café for a few minutes while you drink your iced cocoas?" Sandra suggested warmly, gesturing toward the open tables near the front windows of Chapters & Co. "Tiffany and I were just about to grab a lemonade."

"We'd love that," Katie said at once, looking at me for confirmation.

"Definitely," I nodded.

We rolled back out of the computer aisle to the checkout counter so I could pay for the CSS paperback, and then headed over to a round four-top table in the bookstore café. Before Katie or I even had to ask, Tiffany stepped ahead of our wheelchairs, lifted two of the heavy wooden café chairs away from the table, and tucked them neatly against the wall so Katie and I could roll right up to the table edge side by side with plenty of elbow room.

Katie caught my eye across our armrests and gave me a subtle, approving nod. Almost nobody thought to clear two chairs from a restaurant table without making a big show out of it.

For the next forty minutes, while Tiffany and her mother sipped iced lemonades and Katie and I drank our iced cocoas, the conversation flowed without a single awkward pause. Tiffany asked Katie what her favorite classes at CCHR had been, laughed at our story about how our dad had assembled a rolling microwave-and-toaster cart in our Greenway Court kitchenette because our studio only had an under-the-counter dorm fridge, and listened with rapt attention as Katie and I took turns explaining how we formatted our shared computer desktop.

When I glanced at my watch at four-fifteen, I saw that the afternoon sky outside The Admiral Center's glass rotunda had turned the dark slate-gray of a sudden May coastal thunderstorm.

"We should probably head up to Monticello and Charlotte so we catch the four-thirty Route 2 bus back to West Ghent before the sky opens up," Katie said, tucking her empty cocoa cup into the trash bin beside the café exit.

"Which way is your stop?" Tiffany asked as the four of us stepped out through the mall's sliding glass doors onto the downtown sidewalk.

"Just one block north up Monticello Avenue," I said.

Right as our front caster wheels cleared the mall awning, the first fat drops of warm May rain began pattering against the concrete. We couldn't load our heavy motorized SwiftChairs into a standard family minivan without a hydraulic lift, which meant the Route 2 bus was our only way home—and the one part of a power wheelchair that hated getting rained on was the electronic joystick controller mounted on the right armrest.

"Oh, don't let the rain get on your controls!" Sandra said immediately, popping open a wide, oversized navy-blue golf umbrella from her tote bag while Tiffany pulled a second compact umbrella out of her purse.

Without hesitation, Tiffany stepped right alongside my right armrest and angled her open umbrella directly over my head and my right-hand joystick controller, while Sandra walked beside Katie's wheelchair holding the big golf umbrella over Katie's head and joystick.

Walking shoulder-to-wheel with us down the block to the covered glass bus shelter at Monticello and Charlotte, Tiffany kept her umbrella steady over my armrest until both of our wheelchairs were safely parked underneath the dry acrylic roof of the transit shelter.

"Thank you so much, both of you," Katie said warmly, wiping a single raindrop off her sleeve. "Most people just sprint for their car when it starts raining."

"What are friends for?" Tiffany smiled, shaking out her umbrella and tucking a lock of dark reddish-brown hair behind her ear as she looked down at me through her black-rimmed glasses. Reaching into her purse, she pulled out a ballpoint pen and a cardstock bookmark from Chapters & Co., scribbled her name and phone number across the back in neat handwriting, and handed it to me.

"Here's my number, Nate," Tiffany said softly, her cheeks turning a pretty pink. "If you and Katie aren't busy tomorrow afternoon, maybe my mom could drop me off over at your building in West Ghent? I could bring over a big cheese pizza from Gianni's for the three of us, and you two could show me how to fix my webpage!"

My heart did a sudden, weightless flip in my chest as I looked from the bookmark in my hand to Katie.

"Saturday pizza and HTML debugging sounds awesome to us," Katie beamed, answering for both of us without missing a beat. "Just call Nate's cell when you pull into the Greenway Court lot tomorrow so we can come down to the lobby and unlock the front door for you."

Down the block, the amber destination sign of our CVTA Route 2 bus rounded the corner onto Monticello Avenue, its air brakes hissing as it pulled flush to the curb and kneeled.

"There's our bus," I said, slipping Tiffany's bookmark carefully into my shirt pocket right over my heart before pulling out my flip phone so we could trade numbers.

As the bus driver deployed the front hydraulic wheelchair ramp onto the sidewalk and Katie rolled aboard first to turn into the rear securement bay, Tiffany leaned down beside my wheelchair. Wrapping both arms around my shoulders, she pulled me into a warm, remarkably firm hug—pressing her cheek against mine with a steady, comforting weight that made my whole chest glow.

"I'm really glad that book almost fell on you today, Nate," Tiffany whispered against my ear before straightening up and waving to Katie through the open bus doors. "It was so great meeting both of you! I'll call you tomorrow morning!"

"See you tomorrow, Tiffany! Bye, Sandra!" Katie called out from the wheelchair bay with a huge wave as I rolled up the hydraulic ramp right behind my twin sister.

As the Route 2 bus pulled away from the curb and headed toward West Ghent through the May sun-shower, Katie reached across the aisle between our two secured wheelchairs, squeezed my hand, and grinned from ear to ear.

"Did you see how she cleared those café chairs and held her umbrella right over your joystick, Nate?" Katie said, her eyes shining with genuine happiness for me. "And she wants to bring pizza over for the three of us tomorrow! I think she really, really likes you."

### Part 2: Saturday Pizza in Apartment A6 & Katie's Wholehearted Blessing

**Date:** 2004-05-15 at 13:00 America/New_York

![Inside Apartment A6 on Greenway Court in May 2004, nineteen-year-old twins Nate and Katie Prairie sit in their compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs at their shared workstation desk while Tiffany Sloan sits beside them looking at the computer monitor]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b003/jun_2001_bonding_with_my_sister_katie_bedroom_butterfly.jpg)

#### Saturday, May 15, 2004, 1:00 PM — Pizza, Floppy Disks, & HTML in Apartment A6

"She's pulling into the parking lot right now!" I said at one o'clock on Saturday afternoon, peeking through the white mini-blinds after my flip phone rang with a single bar of signal by our window.

"Come on—let's roll out to the front lobby together to let her in," Katie smiled, smoothing down the hem of her royal-blue sweater as she checked our studio one last time.

We had spent the morning tidying every corner of Apartment A6 until our rectangular studio gleamed. Our full-sized bed was neatly made against the side wall, the speckled laminate counter above our under-the-counter dorm fridge was wiped spotless beside Dad's rolling microwave-and-toaster cart, and our shared desktop computer was booted up at our long workstation desk.

Rolling down the first-floor hallway and down Dave's nonslip aluminum ramp over the three rubber-treaded lobby steps, I unlocked the building's 24/7 keyed glass-and-aluminum front door right as Tiffany stepped up the front walkway past the circular brick lamppost planter, carrying a steaming cardboard pizza box in both hands while her mother Sandra waved warmly to us from the driver's window of their minivan in the parking lot.

"Hey, you two!" Tiffany beamed, stepping into the cool lobby as Sandra pulled out of the lot to run afternoon errands. "One large extra-cheese pizza and a two-liter bottle of ginger ale—Nate told me yesterday at the bookstore that the two of you only drink soda or ginger ale, so I made sure to grab your favorite!"

Katie and I exchanged an instant, delighted look. Remembering a detail like our ginger-ale rule from a single conversation the day before meant everything to us.

"You remembered!" Katie said warmly as we led Tiffany up the short aluminum ramp and down the first-floor corridor to the back of the building. "Right this way—we're in A6, right across the hall from our old neighborhood friend Michael in A5."

When I unlocked our wooden door and held it open for Tiffany to step inside Apartment A6, she paused in the center of the carpet and looked around our studio with an appreciative smile.

"Look how nice and neat you two keep this place!" Tiffany said, setting the warm pizza box down on our kitchenette counter next to Dad's rolling microwave cart. "Seriously, Katie, you can tell a girl who cares about organization lives here—there isn't a single dish in the sink, and look at that microwave cart your dad put together!"

"Wait until you see the walk-in closet and the roll-under bathroom sink," Katie said proudly, grabbing three paper plates and three plastic cups from the cabinet above our stove before pouring cold ginger ale for all three of us.

After we ate two slices of warm cheese pizza apiece, Katie washed our three plastic cups in the kitchenette sink right away—following our house rule of washing dishes as we go—and Tiffany pulled a blue three-and-a-half-inch floppy disk out of her purse.

"Okay, Professor Nate and Professor Katie," Tiffany laughed, holding up the floppy disk as she walked over to our long computer workstation desk. "Don't laugh at my messy code, okay? I brought my HTML file on disk so you two could show me what I did wrong."

Because our studio didn't have extra desk chairs—since Katie and I always sat side by side at the workstation in our compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs—Tiffany didn't ask to sit on our bed across the room. Instead, she kneeled right down on the soft carpet between our two wheelchairs so her black-rimmed glasses were at the exact same eye level as ours while I slid her blue floppy disk into the A: drive of our desktop tower.

Opening her index.html file in a plain-text editor on our seventeen-inch CRT monitor, Katie and I spotted the bug on line forty-two at the exact same second.

"There it is," Katie pointed at the screen with a friendly grin. "You opened a second table row right here without closing the first table cell."

"And look what happens when we replace that nested table with two simple CSS float blocks from the book you caught for me yesterday," I said, typing out a clean ten-line stylesheet block and hitting refresh in the browser window.

On the monitor, Tiffany's jumbled pictures and text snapped into a clean, centered two-column page layout.

"No way! You two fixed that in thirty seconds!" Tiffany gasped, clapping her hands together before turning on the carpet and wrapping one arm around my shoulders and the other arm around Katie's shoulders in a spontaneous three-way group hug. "You guys are absolute geniuses! I wish I had half the brains the two of you have."

For the rest of the afternoon, the three of us stayed right there in Apartment A6 like we had known each other for years. Tiffany listened attentively as I walked her through every CSS property in the new manual, asked Katie about the fall course schedule at the Community College of Hampton Roads, and laughed whenever Katie and I finished each other's sentences. Not once did Tiffany act impatient, not once did she try to pull me away from my twin sister, and when Sandra called at five o'clock from the parking lot out front to pick her up, Tiffany helped Katie fold up the empty pizza box before giving both Katie and me a warm goodbye hug in the hallway.

***

#### Saturday, May 15, 2004, 5:20 PM — "You Deserve Someone Who Makes You Happy, Nate"

When we rolled back up the lobby ramp after watching Sandra's minivan turn off Greenway Court and locked the deadbolt of Apartment A6 behind us, I parked my wheelchair beside our computer desk, still feeling the warm glow of Tiffany's hug in my chest.

Across the room by the kitchenette, Katie pivoted her SwiftChair around, rolled right up beside me, and took my left hand in hers.

"Well?" I asked quietly, looking into my twin sister's blue eyes—because in nineteen years of life, I had never made a single major decision without knowing what Katie thought first. "What did you think of her, Katie?"

Katie's face softened into the warmest, most genuine smile I had ever seen.

"I really like her, Nate," Katie said wholeheartedly, giving my hand a reassuring squeeze. "She brought ginger ale just for us, she sat right down on the carpet between our chairs so we were all together at the computer, and she didn't act weird for a single second about how close you and I are. After how unfair Heather was to you in high school over a rumor that wasn't even true, you deserve someone sweet and patient who makes you happy."

A huge wave of relief and excitement washed through my chest. Having a girl like Tiffany smile at me and hold my hand felt wonderful—but having my twin sister's complete, enthusiastic blessing made the whole world feel safe.

"She asked if we wanted to hang out with her again next weekend," I said, smiling back at Katie.

"Then you should call her tonight and tell her yes," Katie beamed, resting her head briefly against my shoulder the way we always did when everything in our world was right. "Everything is going to be great, Nate."

# West Ocean View, Ideas for CVTA, & From City Council to the Senate of Virginia

## My Brother Only & The Speak Now Vow

### Ocean View Station, The Green Sofa Confrontation, & Speak Now or Forever Hold Your Peace

**Date:** 2004-09-18 at 19:30 America/New_York

![Inside the Sea Breeze West living room on West Ocean View Avenue, nineteen-year-old twins Katie in her royal-blue V-neck sweater and Nate in his brick-red polo sit side by side on the bright green three-cushion sofa beneath the white window blinds with their compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs parked beside the oval coffee table while Tiffany Sloan glares through her black-rimmed glasses in her mint-green graphic shirt by the corner CRT television]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b005/sep_2004_ocean_view_green_sofa_my_brother_only_tiffany.jpg)

#### Saturday, September 18, 2004, 7:30 PM — Ocean View Station & The Green Sofa Confrontation at Sea Breeze West

"This is going to be a great weekend," I said, rolling my compact SwiftChair power wheelchair right up to the curb cut at Ocean View Station as the hydraulic front ramp of the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority Route 1 bus folded down onto the concrete and my nineteen-year-old twin sister Katie wheeled off the bus straight into my arms.

Wearing her favorite royal-blue long-sleeve V-neck sweater with black trim at the collar and cuffs and light-blue jeans, with an overnight backpack clipped to the rear frame of her matching SwiftChair, Katie threw both arms around my shoulders across our armrests and hugged me tight in the warm September evening air blowing off the Chesapeake Bay.

"I've missed you all week, Nate," Katie said happily, resting her cheek against mine for a second before we released our brakes and rolled side by side down the sidewalk along West Ocean View Avenue. "Two whole days of just brother-and-sister time, coding on your computer, and no drama."

"We've got the whole weekend," I smiled, checking my watch on my left wrist as we turned past the low white two-rail fence into the small, tree-shaded courtyard of Sea Breeze West.

Our complex consisted of two low-rise early-nineteen-seventies buildings facing each other across the narrow courtyard and asphalt parking lot—the red-brick building with the script Sea Breeze West sign and the quarter-operated laundry room on one side, and our white-painted two-story courtyard building on the other, where the exterior unit doors and wide double windows were lined up side by side like an old coastal motel. My unit—Apartment 3—sat on the ground floor at the very far end of the white-painted building, furthest from the parking lot.

When I unlocked the wooden front door in the right-hand corner and rolled inside ahead of Katie, the single six-hundred-square-foot apartment opened straight into our combination living room and kitchen. Immediately to the left of the front door, directly beneath the two wide front windows covered by white horizontal mini-blinds looking out onto the courtyard, sat our bright green three-cushion sofa with an oval weathered-wood coffee table in front of it and a black cathode-ray-tube television on a two-shelf black-and-glass stand in the opposite front corner. Just beyond the oval coffee table stood a large dining table that I used as a computer desk for my desktop tower PC running Quantum OS XN Pro, looking straight ahead into the open kitchen with its olive-green base cabinets, speckled laminate prep counters, slate-blue four-burner electric stove, sink, and full-size top-freezer refrigerator.

Standing near the corner television stand in a mint-green short-sleeve graphic T-shirt, charcoal-gray pants, and black-rimmed rectangular glasses, my nineteen-year-old girlfriend Tiffany Sloan froze and stared at Katie's wheelchair rolling through the doorway behind me.
