And looking from the pristine, unwrinkled comforter on Tiffany's caving-in queen mattress to the two well-slept pillows inside the hallway closet, our dad put the whole picture together in three seconds flat.

"Kids," Dad said in a low, gentle voice, crouching down beside the open bi-fold doors and resting a hand on the edge of the sleeping bag, "you two haven't slept on that bed in the bedroom a single night since she left, have you?"

Katie and I looked at each other across our wheelchair armrests, and then Katie looked up at Dad with honest blue eyes.

"We tried to make the bedroom look nice with the new comforter so you wouldn't worry when you came over, Dad," Katie admitted softly. "We even took two of the four pillows off the bed to put in here. Every time we roll into the bedroom at night and look at that mattress caving in down the middle, all either of us can think about is Tiffany. Neither of us wanted to sleep on Tiffany's bed."

"So the two of you have been sleeping in the hallway closet every night?" Dad asked quietly, no anger or judgment in his voice at all—only a deep, aching tenderness behind his glasses.

"Every single night since Sunday the thirteenth," I confessed, looking down at the carpeted closet floor. "When we transfer into the sleeping bag together and pull the two bi-fold doors almost all the way shut, it's completely dark and snug in here, Dad. It blocks out the draft from the windows, it feels safe and tight around our shoulders, and as long as we're holding each other inside the sleeping bag, neither of us has nightmares about Williamsburg or about Tiffany."

Dad stayed crouched beside the closet threshold for a long moment, swallowing hard as he looked at the two pillows pressed side by side in the narrow dark of the closet. I could see how much it broke his heart that his nineteen-year-old son and daughter had been hurt so badly—and left with such a broken, caving-in reminder of Tiffany in their bedroom—that they had retreated into a hallway closet every night just to feel clean and safe in each other's arms.

When Dad stood back up and pulled his yellow tape measure from his belt clip, his voice was warm and steady.

"Well," Dad said, tapping the drywall on the left half of the closet nearest the kitchen, "then we are going to engineer this closet around your sleeping spot so nobody ever takes your sanctuary away from you."

Both Katie and I looked up in surprise. "You don't think it's weird that we're sleeping in the closet, Dad?" I asked.

"Nate, you and your sister just survived fourteen months of hell, and the two of you found a way to keep each other safe and calm through the night," Dad said firmly, resting a hand on my shoulder and then on Katie's. "There is nothing weird about two autistic twins who love each other needing the dark, a snug space, and a hug to sleep in peace. Watch what we're going to do."

Pulling out his carpenter's pencil and level, Dad marked the vertical mounting tracks on the kitchen half of the closet wall starting twenty-eight inches above the carpet—leaving the entire bottom level of the closet completely open from end to end so that even with four tiers of white wire pantry shelving mounted on the half closest to the kitchen, and a sturdy coat rod mounted high on the half closest to the bedroom for our winter jackets, the carpeted floor beneath remained a 100-percent unobstructed, full-length sleeping-bag berth for the two of us.

![Looking straight down the carpeted far-right hallway of Sea Breeze West Apartment 3 toward the doorless back bedroom: on the left wall of the hallway, a standard-width closet doorway fitted with two white bi-folding doors opens outward to reveal white wire pantry shelves on the half closest to the kitchen, hanging winter coats on the half closer to the bedroom, and a carpeted closet floor lined with a shared navy-blue sleeping bag and two white pillows, while at the end of the hall nineteen-year-old twins Katie and Nate sit side by side in their compact SwiftChair power wheelchairs with the queen bed peeking from the far-left corner, the high-mounted through-wall box A/C unit next to the bedroom window, and Katie's wooden dresser with her television and DVD player against the right bedroom wall behind Nate]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/katie/b005/nov_2005_ocean_view_hallway_and_left_closet_sanctuary_baseline.jpg)

Across Black Friday afternoon and Saturday morning, the three of us worked together in the hallway like a well-oiled crew. From our wheelchairs, Katie and I held the spirit level against the drywall, handed Dad the steel drywall anchors, screws, and wire shelf brackets, and tested the height clearances while Dad drilled the vertical standards into the studs, snapped the four white wire pantry shelves into place on the kitchen side, and anchored the coat rod and upper shelf on the bedroom side.

By Saturday afternoon, when we rolled the boxes of toasted oat cereal, the blue boxes of enriched pasta, and the rows of canned soups and vegetables out of the cramped kitchen cabinets and arranged them neatly across the four white wire pantry shelves on the left half of the hallway closet—with our winter coats hanging on the right half and our freshly fluffed sleeping bag and two white pillows laid smoothly across the vacuumed carpet below—the hallway closet looked like it had been custom-designed from the ground up just for us.

"Half pantry closest to the kitchen, half coat closet closest to the bedroom, and our dark cuddle sanctuary right on the carpeted floor," Katie beamed, leaning across her armrest to hug Dad around the waist as he packed his drill back into his red toolbox. "Thank you, Dad—it's even better than I pictured it."

"You're welcome, sweetheart," Dad said, kissing the top of Katie's head and ruffling my hair as he glanced one more time toward the caving-in queen bed in the back bedroom with a quiet, determined look in his eyes. "And I promise you two—you aren't going to be stuck with that caving-in mattress forever. As soon as I get things lined up, I'm going to make sure the two of you have a real, brand-new queen bed of your own to share."

### Part 4: The Seattle Transit Blog Inspiration & Quietly Launching Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit

**Date:** 2005-11-27 at 19:30 America/New_York

#### Sunday, November 27, 2005, 7:30 PM — The Seattle Transit Blog & A Question Across the Desk

"Look at this site I just found on OmniSearch, Katie," I said, angling our seventeen-inch off-white CRT monitor slightly toward my twin sister's wheelchair as the cooling fan inside my 2004 desktop tower hummed steadily on the repurposed dining table along the far-left wall of our front room. "It's called the Seattle Transit Blog."

Katie set her cold can of ginger ale down on a coaster beside her open laptop, released her joystick brake, and rolled her compact SwiftChair right up flush against mine so our armrests touched in front of the desk.

It was Sunday evening of Thanksgiving weekend—the night after Dad had finished anchoring the four white wire pantry shelves and the winter coat rod above our sleeping-bag sanctuary inside the hallway closet—and for the past hour, Katie and I had been spreading paper Coastal Virginia Transit Authority folding pocket timetables across the tabletop to plan our upcoming January spring-semester bus commute down to the Community College of Hampton Roads Virginia Beach Campus. Between my freshly wiped Quantum OS XN Pro desktop—now cleanly configured with separate user accounts for the two of us and zero trace of Tiffany anywhere on the hard drive—and our two laptops flanking the keyboard tray, the left corner of Apartment 3 already felt like a proper twin command center.

"What's the Seattle Transit Blog?" Katie asked, leaning her chin on my shoulder as her blue eyes scanned the web page glowing on the CRT glass.

"It's a website run by everyday transit riders out in Washington State who ride TAPS—the Transit Authority of the Puget Sound," I explained, scrolling down through the front-page articles with the mouse wheel. "Instead of just complaining to themselves at bus stops, a group of regular riders, computer programmers, and wheelchair commuters started a blog where they write out actual proposals for how their regional bus and light-rail grid ought to work."

Katie leaned closer to the monitor, reading silently beside me as I clicked from one post to the next.

On the screen, the writers at the Seattle Transit Blog were dissecting TAPS King County and Pierce County bus corridors with the exact kind of logical, map-by-map precision that made both of our autistic brains light up. One post broke down how unifying five separate county and municipal bus systems under TAPS—complete with a single regional smart card called the Cascade Card and coordinated low-floor CNG bus fleets—eliminated the penalty for riders crossing city and county lines around the water of Puget Sound. Another post mapped out how the planned TAPS Central Corridor light-rail spine from downtown Seattle toward Tukwila and the airport would only succeed if local feeder buses were redesigned into high-frequency, clock-face transfer pulses rather than leaving riders stranded at suburban hubs after six o'clock in the evening.

Better yet, down in the comment threads below every post, dozens of daily Puget Sound riders—alongside local transit operators, mechanics, and even city planners—were trading timetable math, pointing out broken wheelchair lifts or missing sidewalk curb cuts, and debating which corridors needed fifteen-minute headways next.

Katie stayed quiet for a long moment, her gaze moving from the glowing Seattle Transit Blog header on the seventeen-inch monitor down to the crumpled stack of paper CVTA timetables spread beside our keyboard: Route 1 from Granby Street and Ocean View down to CCHR; Route 61 across the Hampton Roads Bridge-Tunnel from Newport News and Hampton; Route 29 up to Chesapeake Bay Mall and Hilltop; Route 15 down Military Highway; and Route 12 past Dad's neighborhood off Indian River Road.

"Look at how they write this, Nate," Katie said softly, tapping her fingertip against the edge of the desk. "They aren't screaming or venting into the void. They're posting the real schedules, showing where the connections fail, and laying out constructive ideas for what their regional transit system could be if the people in charge actually listened to daily riders."

She turned her head to look straight at me, a quiet spark catching in her eyes.

"Why doesn't Hampton Roads have something like the Seattle Transit Blog for CVTA?" Katie asked.

I blinked, looking from the CRT monitor to my sister. "A transit blog for Coastal Virginia?"

"Think about it, Nate," Katie said, gesturing toward the paper timetables on our table. "Nobody knows this bus grid better than the two of us. We don't drive cars. Every single time we go to college at CCHR, or go shopping at Chesapeake Bay Mall or Emerald Creek Mall, or cross the water from the Peninsula, our wheelchairs are rolling up the front ramps of CVTA buses. Two Saturdays ago, when I fled Williamsburg in the pouring rain, it took me five buses across two transit agencies just to get from the College of Charles and Anne to your front door—and if I had missed that 6:45 PM Route 61 bus out of Newport News Transit Center, I would have been stranded on the wrong side of the Hampton Roads Bridge-Tunnel all night long because the cross-harbor bus shuts down before seven o'clock!"

"And ten days ago, when we rode Route 1 down to the CCHR Beach Campus to register for United States History," I added, the pieces clicking together in my head, "it took ninety minutes each way on a single local bus—and the schedule book says the very last Route 1 back leaves campus at 5:45 PM. Basically the whole Virginia Beach bus grid rolls up the sidewalks at dinnertime."

"Exactly!" Katie said, sitting up straighter in her SwiftChair. "We talk about what we wish CVTA would fix every single time we're waiting at a bus shelter together. What if we quietly start our own website and start jotting those ideas down in public—just like the Seattle Transit Blog did for TAPS?"

***

#### Hand-Coding Our Template on BlogSphere: Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit

My fingers hovered over the mechanical keyboard as the technical architecture spun up in my head.

"We can do it tonight," I said, opening a fresh browser window on Quantum OS XN Pro. "We haven't learned server-side database programming yet to build our own dynamic content engine from scratch, and static hand-coded HTML pages alone would be a pain to update every time someone leaves a comment. But Omni Corporation's hosted blogging service—BlogSphere—handles the post database, permalinks, RSS syndication feed, and reader comments on their servers automatically, while letting you switch into raw HTML mode to hand-code your own custom page template."

Katie grinned, pulling her laptop closer across the dining table. "And we've known how to write clean HTML by hand since seventh grade at Morrison Middle School. What should we call it?"

We looked down at the teal-and-navy logo printed across the cover of the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority timetable booklets on our desk.

"What about Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit?" Katie suggested. "Simple, direct, and constructive. Anyone searching OmniSearch for CVTA or Hampton Roads bus routes will know right away that we aren't there to tear public transit down—we're there to share ideas for what our regional bus system could be."

"And for the web address," I said, typing the subdomain check into the BlogSphere registration form on the CRT monitor, "ideasforcvta.blogsphere.com."

A green confirmation line appeared on the screen immediately: the address was available.

Side by side at our repurposed dining-table desk, Katie and I clicked through the registration screen, created our shared administrator account under both of our first names—Katie and Nate, two nineteen-year-old college students and daily wheelchair transit riders living in Norfolk and commuting into Virginia Beach—and immediately opened the raw HTML template editor.

For the next hour, the only sounds in Apartment 3 were the rhythmic click of our keyboards, the gentle hum of the desktop tower's cooling fan, and the occasional fizz of ginger ale as the two of us stripped out BlogSphere's cluttered stock layout and hand-coded our own clean, two-column HTML and style-sheet template from scratch.

Working together the same way we had since we were twelve years old, I coded the structural HTML tables, semantic heading tags, and high-contrast style rules—using a deep coastal-navy banner, crisp white background panels, dark slate body text that was effortless to read without eye strain, and a coastal-teal accent bar—while Katie wrote the sidebar markup on her laptop and transferred the snippets over our local network folder. In the right-hand sidebar, Katie coded an About the Authors box explaining that we were twin brother and sister who used compact power wheelchairs full-time and rode CVTA buses across Norfolk, Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Hampton, and Newport News every week, followed by a clean list of links to official CVTA route schedules, the Colonial Triangle Transit Authority in Williamsburg, and a Blogroll section where the very first link pointed directly to the Seattle Transit Blog that had inspired us.

"Take a look at the preview," I said, hitting Refresh on the seventeen-inch CRT monitor.

Across the top of the screen ran our clean navy-and-teal masthead:

> Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit<br>
> A Rider-Written Blog Exploring What Public Transit Across Hampton Roads Could Be<br>
> Written by Katie & Nate

"It looks clean, fast, and completely readable," Katie said proudly, squeezing my shoulder. "No flashy clutter, no broken tables, and anyone on dial-up or broadband can load it in two seconds. Now let's jot down our very first posts so the site doesn't launch with an empty front page."

***

#### Jotting Down Our First Posts & Planting the Seed for 2008

We didn't issue any grand manifesto or pretend to be transportation lobbyists. We simply opened the New Post editor on BlogSphere and started jotting down the exact, practical realities we lived every time our wheels touched the pavement at a Hampton Roads bus stop—paired with the common-sense improvements we wished CVTA and our local city councils would build.

For our opening post, Katie rolled her chair right up to the center keyboard and typed out the story of what it actually took to ride public transit from the Peninsula to Southside Norfolk on a Saturday afternoon and evening—without naming Brandon or the personal nightmare she had been escaping, focusing entirely on the five-bus regional gauntlet from Williamsburg down through Lackey on Route 116, down Warwick Boulevard on Route 107 to Newport News Transit Center, across the Hampton Roads Bridge-Tunnel on Route 61 to Wards Corner, and north on Route 1 to West Ocean View.

> Sunday, November 27, 2005 — Welcome to Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit: Why Does Our Cross-Harbor Lifeline Shut Down at 6:45 PM?<br><br>
> Hampton Roads is a metropolitan region of more than one and a half million people divided by water, yet if you rely on a wheelchair and public transit to cross between the Peninsula and Southside Norfolk on a Saturday evening, the entire regional grid hangs by a single thread: the last southbound CVTA Route 61 bus leaves Newport News Transit Center at 6:45 PM (and Hampton Transit Center at 6:57 PM), and isn't even a full route into Virginia Beach—it's an Expressways trip that terminates in Downtown Norfolk at 7:34 PM. Miss that 6:45 PM connection because an upstream bus on Warwick Boulevard runs late, and you are stranded on the wrong side of the water until Sunday morning.<br><br>
> Even worse, while waiting thirty-five minutes at Newport News Transit Center for that final 6:45 PM bus—or transferring through Hampton Transit Center—riders are forced to use public transit center restrooms with half-ripped mirrors, bare glue-stained plywood, broken toilet-paper dispensers hanging by a single bolt, and vandalized stalls. Riders who pay their fares every day deserve cross-harbor Route 61 service that runs until at least 10:00 PM or 11:00 PM seven nights a week, and transit center facilities that are kept clean, safe, and fully accessible.

As soon as Katie clicked Publish on our first entry, I opened a second draft tab so the two of us could jot down the twin Virginia Beach problems we had just run headfirst into during our Monday college-registration trip to CCHR and our rides across the Norfolk–Virginia Beach city line: the ninety-minute local bus crawl, the strict two-hour paper transfer time limit, and the glaring service cliff where buses crossed out of Norfolk into Virginia Beach.

"Look right here at the printed Route 1 and Route 25 schedule booklets, Nate," Katie said, smoothing both folded paper timetables flat under the desk lamp while I typed. "You don't even need an inside source at City Hall to see why Virginia Beach's bus service hours are so terrible compared to Norfolk's. Just look at the columns on Route 1 between Downtown Norfolk and Pembroke East. On the Norfolk side of the route—from Monticello and Charlotte up Granby Street through Wards Corner, Ocean View at Duffy's Lane, and out to the Amphibious Base at the city border—the bus runs every fifteen minutes during weekday rush hours, every thirty minutes midday and Saturdays, and keeps running late into the night until 2:15 AM, seven days a week. Then look at what happens the exact second Route 1 crosses the city line from the Amphibious Base into Virginia Beach along Pleasure House Road, Haygood Shopping Center, and Pembroke East: most of the buses stop at the Amphibious Base and turn right back around into Norfolk! Inside Virginia Beach, Route 1 drops to once an hour, only runs during the day with the last bus leaving Pembroke East at 6:22 PM, and on the back of the brochure under Sunday, the Virginia Beach columns literally just say NO SUNDAY SERVICE in capital letters across an empty white box!"

"And look at Route 25 between Glenrock Mall and CCHR Virginia Beach Campus," I said, tracing my finger across the weekday timetable columns. "It leaves Glenrock Mall in Norfolk every thirty minutes, rolls down to Greenwich Road and Newtown Road right at the Virginia Beach border, and every second bus literally stops at Newtown Road and turns right back around to stay in Norfolk—dropping the Virginia Beach half down Princess Anne Road to CCHR to once an hour, ending at 5:53 PM, with zero weekend service at all."

Katie shook her head with a wry half-smile. "It reminds me of that old local political story Dad told us about Virginia Beach's mayor, Myra Overton. Everyone knows Mayor Overton is a genuinely sweet, grandmotherly lady in person who got her political start serving on the city's library board—and she's been a great champion for the Central Library—but she is not so great a politician when it comes to public transit. Like the story that always goes around among bus riders: there's no way to confirm she ever actually said it, of course, but so they say, if Mayor Overton had her way, Route 20 on Virginia Beach Boulevard would depart Downtown Norfolk, take the Boulevard out to Newtown Road, and turn right back around and stay in Norfolk—just to keep the riff-raff out!"

"Whether anyone at City Hall ever said it out loud or not, that exact Newtown Road mentality is printed right here in the CVTA schedule book," I said, typing the Route 1 and Route 25 timetable numbers straight into our second post without naming any unconfirmed rumors, letting the black-and-white schedule columns speak for themselves. "At CCHR Virginia Beach Campus, the last Route 12 toward Military Highway and Indian River Road leaves at 5:48 PM, the last Route 25 leaves at 5:53 PM, and if a citizen takes Route 33 down to the Virginia Beach Municipal Center to watch a 6:00 PM City Council meeting and needs to connect at CCHR to Route 12, Route 25, Route 29, or Route 36 to get home, they have to leave the Municipal Center on the 5:33 PM bus—twenty-seven minutes before the 6:00 PM City Council meeting even gavels in! And when every Virginia Beach route runs once an hour, if you ride a multi-bus loop across Virginia Beach and Norfolk—say from CCHR up Route 29 to Hilltop, across Virginia Beach Boulevard on Route 20 to Glenrock Mall, down Military Highway on Route 15, and onto Route 12 back to CCHR—your two-hour paper transfer expires before you can even board your final connecting bus, forcing you to pay a second full fare just to finish one trip."

Together, we formatted our second entry—laying out our wish list for late-evening service hours until at least ten o'clock at night on core Virginia Beach routes, timed clock-face transfer pulses at CCHR, Glenrock Mall, and Hilltop, limited-stop express trunk buses along Granby Street, Virginia Beach Boulevard, and Military Highway, continuous curb-cut sidewalks leading to concrete wheelchair boarding pads, and a regional smart-card or day-pass system modeled on what the Seattle Transit Blog documented out in the Puget Sound.

When I clicked Publish on the second post and reloaded ideasforcvta.blogsphere.com, both articles sat cleanly on the screen beneath our navy-and-teal header, ready for OmniSearch's web crawlers to index overnight.

Right then, of course, the visitor counter at the bottom of our sidebar read zero. Nobody in Norfolk or Virginia Beach knew our blog existed yet. We were just two nineteen-year-old twins in power wheelchairs sitting side by side in a small West Ocean View apartment at nine o'clock on a Sunday night.

Katie reached across her armrest, laced her fingers through mine, and rested her head gently against my shoulder as we looked at the glowing CRT screen together.

"We don't need a crowd tonight, Nate," Katie said quietly, as if reading my mind. "The Seattle Transit Blog didn't build its readership in a single weekend either. If you and I just keep riding the buses together, studying every route map and timetable change, and jotting down two or three honest, well-researched posts every single week while we finish our degrees—think about where this site will be two or three years from now."

I looked at the calendar pinned beside our desk and did the math in my head. "In 2008, the City of Virginia Beach holds its next Mayor's Race and City Council elections."

"That's three full years of building an archive, showing up in OmniSearch results whenever anyone looks up a CVTA bus route, and building a community of riders across Hampton Roads," Katie said, her voice steady and quietly determined. "If we start putting in the work right now in 2005, then by the time the 2008 Mayor's Race in Virginia Beach comes around, Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit will already have its name out there—and maybe, for once, the people running to lead our city will actually have to pay attention to the people who ride the bus."

## Do Better: The Four-Bus Loop, Kempsville City Council, & The Path to the 85th House District

### Part 1: Does the Bus Run? Calling Out CVTA's Complacency & The 2008 Regional Mayoral Challenge

**Date:** 2008-04-14 at 13:30 America/New_York

#### Thursday, April 14, 2008, 1:30 PM — The Binders at Eighteenth Street & The "Good Enough" Mentality

"Look at the open three-ring binder on the corner of the staff table, Nate," Katie whispered, leaning slightly across her SwiftChair armrest toward mine as we parked our wheelchairs at the end of the front visitor row inside the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority boardroom on Eighteenth Street in Norfolk. "That's our Tuesday post. They printed the whole thing out and highlighted the timetable chart in yellow."

I followed my sister's gaze across the polished laminate table where CVTA's senior operations staff and the appointed city council commissioners from around Hampton Roads were shuffling their agenda packets before the monthly Transportation District Commission meeting gaveled in.

Sure enough, clipped inside the deputy planning director's black binder—and photocopied into three separate packets along the commissioners' dais—were six pages printed directly from Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit, complete with the coastal-navy header and the custom PHP route-comparison tables I had coded the week before.

Two and a half years had passed since that quiet Sunday night in November 2005 when Katie and I had first launched our blog on BlogSphere from our dining-table desk at Sea Breeze West Apartment 3. In that time, our lives had moved forward together step by step. We had ridden CVTA Route 1 and Route 29 side by side through rain, summer heat, and winter wind to finish our associate degrees at the Community College of Hampton Roads. When our West Ocean View lease ended in March 2007 and Dad took his new systems engineering position up in Tysons Corner, the three of us had moved our permanent family home base up to Dad's two-story brick house on Sunset Drive in Alexandria while Katie and I spent twelve intense months living in the accessible dorms at Roosevelt Rehabilitation Center in Fishersville, completing the Web Applications Developer program and mastering PHP and relational database architecture. And just last month, in March 2008, after graduating from Roosevelt Rehabilitation Center at age twenty-two, Katie and I had returned home to Hampton Roads, pooled our earnings, and bought our own accessible single-story home fifty-fifty together in the Kempsville section of Virginia Beach.

And through every single one of those hundred and twenty-four weeks, we had never missed a post on Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit.

What had started as two nineteen-year-old twins quietly jotting down what they wished their regional bus system could be had grown into the first independent transit watchdog Hampton Roads had ever seen. After we rebuilt the site in custom PHP during our final semester at Roosevelt Rehabilitation Center, I had written a server-log analyzer to see where our weekday traffic was coming from. The results had made both of us sit up at our desks: every weekday morning between eight and ten o'clock, dozens of page views were coming straight from static IP blocks registered to CVTA headquarters in Norfolk, alongside municipal government networks inside the City Halls of Virginia Beach, Norfolk, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Hampton, and Newport News.

They were reading every word we wrote. They just didn't know what to do with two twenty-three-year-old twins in power wheelchairs who refused to pat them on the back for the status quo.

When the commission chair called the brief public-comment period at the start of the one-thirty afternoon meeting—scheduled, as always, in the middle of a Thursday workday when almost no working commuter could attend—Katie released her joystick brake and rolled smoothly up to the low microphone table at the front of the room, with me rolling right up beside her holding our printed schedule audit.

"Mr. Chairman, commissioners, and CVTA staff," Katie said clearly, her voice steady and polite as every head along the horseshoe dais turned toward our two wheelchairs. "My brother Nathaniel and I are the authors of Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit. We ride CVTA buses in our power wheelchairs every week across Virginia Beach, Norfolk, Chesapeake, and the Peninsula, and we pay our regular thirty-day pass fares just like thousands of other citizens who depend on this network."

Down at the staff table, the senior service planner and the communications director exchanged an immediate, tight-lipped glance, recognizing our names at once.

"Over the past two years," Katie continued, resting her hand on the timetable charts across my lap, "we have documented why riders across Hampton Roads are being left behind—not because our region lacks the roads or the riders, but because this agency and our six member cities have settled for schedules that lock working people out. Look at your own printed Route 1 schedule between Downtown Norfolk and Pembroke East: on the Norfolk side of the line, from Downtown Norfolk through Wards Corner and Ocean View to the Amphibious Base, Route 1 runs every fifteen to thirty minutes and runs late into the night until 2:15 AM, seven days a week. The exact moment Route 1 crosses the city line into Virginia Beach toward Pleasure House Road, Haygood, and Pembroke East, most of the buses turn around at the border, service drops to once an hour, only runs during the day with the last bus leaving Pembroke East at 6:22 PM, and has no Sunday service at all. On Route 25 from Glenrock Mall to the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus, buses run every thirty minutes to Newtown Road at the city line, where half of them turn right back around into Norfolk, leaving the Virginia Beach side down Princess Anne Road to CCHR with hourly daytime-only service that ends at 5:53 PM. At the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus hub, the last Route 12 bus back toward Military Highway and Indian River Road leaves at 5:48 PM. If a citizen rides Route 33 to the Virginia Beach Municipal Center to attend a 6:00 PM City Council meeting and needs to catch Route 12, Route 25, or Route 29 at CCHR to get home, they have to leave City Hall at 5:33 PM—twenty-seven minutes before the council meeting even starts. Across the water, the last southbound Route 61 across the Hampton Roads Bridge-Tunnel still leaves Newport News Transit Center at 6:45 PM, and riders transferring on multi-bus routes are still getting penalized with a second fare because the two-hour paper transfer expires while they wait for sixty-minute local buses."

"We aren't here to bash public transit," I added from my chair beside Katie. "We love public transit. We need public transit to live our lives. We're here because CVTA can be so much better than this—with timed evening transfer pulses at CCHR, Glenrock Mall, and Hilltop, service running until at least ten or eleven o'clock at night, clean transit center restrooms, and a modern smart-card fare system that doesn't punish two-bus commuters—if the leaders on this commission have the political willpower to fund and demand it."

The commission chair offered a polite, practiced nod as the three-minute timer beeped. "Thank you both for your comments. Staff will take your blog submissions under advisement."

Twenty minutes later, when the board recessed into the hallway outside the Eighteenth Street conference room, CVTA's senior service planner and one of the veteran municipal council commissioners walked past our wheelchairs near the water fountain. Stopping beside us with a folder tucked under his arm, the planner offered the tired, defensive half-smile of a bureaucrat who had spent fifteen years guarding an unchallenged routine.

"Look, Katie, Nate," the planner said, lowering his voice as the commissioner nodded beside him, "we read your blog every week. You two obviously put a lot of math into those PHP charts of yours. But you have to be realistic about how transit works in a region like Hampton Roads."

"Realistic about what?" Katie asked, looking straight up at him from her SwiftChair.

The commissioner shrugged his shoulders, palms turned upward in the exact gesture that summed up thirty years of regional transit policy.

"Does the bus run?" the commissioner asked rhetorically. "Yes, the bus runs. Every morning the garages roll the fleet out across all six cities. Do people get to CCHR and the malls and the shipyards during the day? Yes. Is it good enough for what our city councils budget every spring? I guess so. Nobody at City Hall is clamoring to spend millions more running buses late into the night or overhauling transfer rules when the buses are already out there running their routes."

Katie and I looked at each other as the planner and the commissioner patted the folder and walked back into the boardroom.

There it was, spoken right out loud in the hallway of Eighteenth Street: the entire institutional philosophy of the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority.

*Does the bus run? Yes. Is it good enough? I guess so.*

For decades, CVTA and the politicians who funded it had hidden behind that exact complacency. As long as a diesel bus rolled past a stop sign once an hour during banker's hours and the annual federal grant paperwork got signed, the agency and the six city councils treated the box as checked. Until Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit came along, nobody with the technical skills to dissect their Transportation Service Plans—and the daily lived reality of riding those buses in power wheelchairs—had ever stood up in public and told them to *do better*.

As we rolled our wheelchairs out the automatic glass doors of the Eighteenth Street headquarters toward the Granby Street bus stop to catch our ride home, Katie's jaw was set with quiet fire.

"Did you hear what that commissioner said, Nate?" Katie asked as we waited on the concrete sidewalk pad. "'Nobody at City Hall is clamoring to spend money or overhaul the routes because it's good enough.' He just admitted the truth. The problem isn't that CVTA can't be improved. Every single improvement we've written about on the blog is completely doable. The only thing missing is the political willpower from the mayors and city councils who control CVTA's purse strings and sit on that board."

"And half of those city halls are up for election this year," I said, the realization clicking into place as the low-floor CVTA bus pulled up to the curb and kneeled its front suspension.

"Every single one of them has elections in 2008," Katie said as the hydraulic ramp folded down onto the concrete and we rolled up into the front wheelchair securement bays. "And as soon as we get home to Kempsville, we are going to put their political willpower to the test in front of every voter in Hampton Roads."

***

#### Mapping the 2008 Regional Mayoral Elections & Issuing the Open Challenge

Back at our dual computer desks in the spare bedroom office of our new Kempsville house that evening, Katie and I pulled up the official 2008 Virginia State Board of Elections candidate filings across all six member cities of the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority: Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Norfolk, Hampton, and Newport News.

Sipping cold ginger ale from our glasses while my PHP editor and our browser tabs glowed side by side across our monitors, we mapped out the exact 2008 municipal calendar across the six cities.

"Look at how the 2008 mayoral calendar breaks down across the six CVTA cities," I said, pointing to the state election schedule on my screen. "Out of the six cities—Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Norfolk, Hampton, and Newport News—four of them are holding direct elections for Mayor here in 2008, while the other two are mid-term on their mayoral cycle."

Katie rolled her chair closer and read down the certified candidate lists with me.

"Norfolk and Newport News both elected their mayors to four-year terms back in May 2006, so neither Norfolk nor Newport News has a Mayor's race until May 2010—though both cities have City Council seats on the ballot next month on Tuesday, May 6, 2008," Katie noted. "Then, on that same Tuesday, May 6, 2008 spring municipal ballot, three CVTA cities are electing their Mayors: Chesapeake has an open-seat Mayor's race, Portsmouth has a contested Mayor's race, and Hampton has an open-seat Mayor's race across the water."

"And then our own city—Virginia Beach—moved its municipal elections to the fall general election ballot on Tuesday, November 4, 2008," I said, scrolling down to the Virginia Beach filings. "And look at the Virginia Beach Mayor's race: Mayor Myra Overton is running for another term after twenty years as Mayor, and she's facing three challengers in a four-way race for Mayor of Virginia Beach, right alongside the at-large and district City Council races."

Katie looked at Mayor Myra E. Overton's name on the screen and shook her head thoughtfully.

Every lifelong resident of Virginia Beach knew the paradox of Mayor Myra Overton. On a personal level, she was a genuinely sweet, gracious, grandmotherly lady who had gotten her start in civic and political life serving on the city's public library board and had championed the Central Library on Virginia Beach Boulevard for decades. If you met her at a ribbon-cutting or a neighborhood library reading, she couldn't have been nicer.

As a municipal politician overseeing transportation for the largest city in Virginia, however, her twenty-year tenure had been a disaster for anyone who rode a bus. Among daily CVTA riders, bus operators, and local political watchers, there was a famous story that had circulated for years to explain why Virginia Beach's bus service hours were so abysmal compared to Norfolk's. There was no way to confirm she had ever actually said the words out loud, of course, but as the local legend went, if Mayor Overton had her way, the Route 20 bus on Virginia Beach Boulevard would depart Downtown Norfolk, roll east down the Boulevard to Newtown Road at the city line, and turn right back around and stay in Norfolk—to keep the riff-raff out, of course.

Whether that story was literal truth or courthouse folklore, one glance at the printed Route 1 and Route 25 timetables—where frequent, late-night Norfolk buses hit the Virginia Beach border and suddenly shriveled into once-an-hour daytime-only runs that vanished after dinnertime—made it painfully obvious why everyone in Hampton Roads believed it.

Katie leaned forward, resting her forearms on the desk.

"Four mayoral elections across Hampton Roads in 2008—Chesapeake, Portsmouth, and Hampton in May, and Virginia Beach in November—plus City Council races in all six CVTA cities," Katie said. "Every single one of those candidates puts glossy photos on their campaign flyers claiming they care about regional transportation, traffic relief, working families, and citizens with disabilities. Let's see how many of them are actually willing to step out from behind a podium and ride the bus with the two of us."

Opening the PHP administrative dashboard for Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit, Katie and I drafted our landmark open letter together, carefully checking every word before publishing it to the top of the front page and sending personalized copies directly to the official campaign email addresses of every certified candidate for Mayor in Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, and Hampton—as well as the City Council candidates across all six CVTA cities:

> Thursday, April 14, 2008 — An Open Invitation to the 2008 Mayoral & City Council Candidates of Hampton Roads: Come Ride the Bus With Us<br><br>
> Whenever we speak with staff and commissioners at the Coastal Virginia Transit Authority about late-evening bus curfews, ninety-minute routes, expired two-hour paper transfers, and missing sidewalk curb cuts, we hear the same unspoken excuse: Does the bus run? Yes. Is it good enough? I guess so.<br><br>
> For the thousands of citizens across Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Norfolk, Hampton, and Newport News who rely on CVTA buses every single day to get to college, to work, to medical appointments, and to civic meetings—including wheelchair riders like the two of us—"good enough" is not good enough. CVTA can be improved into a first-class regional transit network, and the only ingredient missing is the political willpower of the elected mayors and city councils who fund and govern it.<br><br>
> This year, voters in four of our six CVTA cities—Chesapeake, Portsmouth, and Hampton on May 6, and Virginia Beach on November 4—will elect their next Mayor, while voters across all six cities will elect members of their City Councils. Therefore, Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit is issuing a simple, non-partisan public invitation to every candidate for Mayor in Virginia Beach, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, and Hampton, and every candidate for City Council across the region:<br><br>
> Meet the two of us at a CVTA transit hub, leave your campaign car in the parking lot, pay the regular bus fare alongside us, and ride a real multi-bus loop on CVTA with two daily wheelchair riders. No staged press vans, no special charter buses, and no shortcuts—just the real schedules, the real transfers, and an honest conversation on camera about how we can give Hampton Roads the transit system our citizens deserve.

When I clicked Publish and dispatched the invitation emails across all four mayoral races and six cities, the comment section on Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit lit up within twenty minutes. Daily bus commuters from Ocean View to Kempsville, CCHR students, shipyard workers in Newport News and Portsmouth, and even readers who had followed us over from the Seattle Transit Blog cheered the challenge on and began forwarding the link to local newspaper reporters and campaign managers.

Then we waited to see which politicians actually had the courage to show up.

***

#### Silence Across the Region — And the One Candidate Who Said Yes

Days turned into weeks as the May 6, 2008 municipal elections in Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Hampton, Norfolk, and Newport News approached and passed.

Every morning, Katie and I sat side by side at our desks in Kempsville with our coffee and ginger ale, checking our campaign inbox and phone messages.

From the mayoral campaigns in Chesapeake: silence.
From the mayoral campaigns in Portsmouth: silence.
From the mayoral campaigns in Hampton: a single three-sentence form email from a campaign aide thanking us for our "interest in regional infrastructure" and stating that the candidate's schedule did not permit a multi-hour bus ride, followed by silence.
From the incumbent City Council members and establishment candidates across Norfolk, Newport News, Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Hampton, and Virginia Beach: not a single acceptance.

Even here in Virginia Beach, as the four-way November 2008 Mayor's race heated up through the summer, the twenty-year incumbent mayor's campaign ignored our invitation completely. The wealthy banking-executive frontrunner backed by the courthouse establishment didn't reply. The veteran budget-hawk former councilman didn't accept either. And every single incumbent and establishment candidate running for the Virginia Beach City Council pretended our email didn't exist.

"They really don't care, do they, Nate?" Katie said quietly one warm afternoon at our desks, staring at the spreadsheet on my monitor where almost every single row across Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Hampton, Norfolk, Newport News, and Virginia Beach still read *No Response*. "They want the title of Mayor or City Council member, and they want to sit on regional boards and cut ribbons on highway interchanges, but not one of them is willing to spend two and a half hours riding the bus system they fund."

Just as I reached for the mouse to update our public tracker post on the blog, my email client chimed with a new incoming message.

I blinked at the sender line on the screen, then clicked it open.

"Katie," I said, sitting forward in my SwiftChair. "Look at the inbox. Someone just replied."

Katie rolled her chair right up against mine and leaned in toward the monitor.

The email wasn't from a campaign press secretary or an automated mailing list. It came directly from the fourth candidate on the ballot for Mayor of the City of Virginia Beach—Scott Tanner, a young, thirty-something political-outsider Navy veteran who wasn't part of the entrenched City Hall establishment and didn't have the backing of the big beachfront real-estate donors.

His message was four sentences long:

> From: Scott Tanner for Mayor Campaign<br>
> To: Katie & Nate — Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit<br>
> Subject: Re: Open Invitation to 2008 Mayoral Candidates — Come Ride the Bus With Us<br><br>
> Katie and Nate,<br>
> I read your blog post and your route analyses, and you're right—nobody asking to lead the largest city in Virginia should be afraid to ride the bus system our citizens depend on every day. I accept your challenge. Tell me which transit hub in Virginia Beach to meet you at, what time to be there, and which buses we're riding, and I'll leave my car in the lot and ride the whole loop with the two of you.<br>
> — Scott

Katie stared at the screen for three seconds, and then a slow, fierce grin broke across her face as she reached over and gripped my hand.

"Out of every mayoral candidate in Chesapeake, Portsmouth, Hampton, and Virginia Beach—and every City Council candidate across all six cities—only one person had the guts to say yes," Katie said, her blue eyes shining with determination as she pulled the folded CVTA Virginia Beach route map across our desk. "And he's running for Mayor right here in Virginia Beach. Come on, Nate—get your video camera batteries on the charger. We're going to meet him at the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus bus loop, and we're going to take him on the exact four-bus circuit across Virginia Beach and Norfolk: Route 29 to Hilltop, Route 20 to Glenrock Mall, Route 15 down Military Highway, and Route 12 right back to CCHR."

### Part 2: The Four-Bus Mayoral Loop, The 5:48 PM Municipal Center Curfew Trap, & Winning the Kempsville City Council Seat

**Date:** 2008-09-16 at 14:00 America/New_York

#### Tuesday, September 16, 2008, 2:00 PM — Four Buses Across Two Cities & The Expired Two-Hour Transfer

"Right on time," twenty-three-year-old Katie said, nodding toward the concrete island at the Community College of Hampton Roads Virginia Beach Campus transit hub as mayoral candidate Scott Tanner locked his sedan in the student visitor lot and walked over to our two compact SwiftChairs with a notebook tucked under his arm.

"Katie, Nate—good to meet you both in person," Scott said, shaking Katie's hand and then mine while I checked the microphone levels on the compact digital camcorder mounted to my wheelchair lap tray. "I brought crisp single-dollar bills and quarters just like your email said. Where does our loop start?"

"Right here on Route 29 northbound to Hilltop," Katie said, pointing up at the approaching forty-foot low-floor bus pulling into the CCHR bus loop. "We're going to ride a single continuous four-bus circuit across Virginia Beach and Norfolk and bring you right back to your car at this exact bus stop: Route 29 from CCHR up to Hilltop, Route 20 westbound down Virginia Beach Boulevard into Norfolk to Glenrock Mall, Route 15 southbound down Military Highway back across the city line into Virginia Beach, and Route 12 back here to CCHR."

When the front ramp of the Route 29 bus folded down onto the concrete pad, Katie and I rolled aboard into the dual front wheelchair securement bays and swiped our thirty-day CVTA magnetic passes through the farebox. Right behind us, Scott stepped up into the vestibule, fed his base fare plus the paper transfer fee into the bill validator, and took the timestamped paper transfer slip the driver tore off the cutter bar.

"Keep an eye on the punched time at the top of that paper transfer," I told Scott as I framed the timestamped slip in my camcorder viewfinder while the bus pulled out of CCHR onto Princess Anne Road. "It's good for exactly two hours."

Because Virginia Beach's local routes ran on forty-five- to sixty-minute headways without synchronized transfer pulses, the math of the two-hour paper transfer trap unfolded right in front of my camera lens in real time.

Our first leg on Route 29 carried us northeast up First Colonial Road to the Hilltop transfer stop, where we waited twenty-five minutes on an unsheltered concrete sidewalk pad in the September sun before boarding a westbound Route 20 bus down Virginia Beach Boulevard.

Halfway down the Boulevard past Little Neck and Newtown Road, an older Hispanic gentleman carrying a canvas tool bag boarded the bus and asked the operator a confused question in Spanish about which stop connected to the Military Highway corridor. Before the operator could shake his head, Scott leaned across the aisle and answered the rider fluently in Spanish—explaining the exact stop at Glenrock Mall where they could both transfer to Route 15.

"You speak Spanish?" Katie asked with genuine respect when the rider thanked Scott warmly and sat down two rows behind our wheelchairs.

"Picked it up during my Navy deployments to South America," Scott said simply. "Half the folks riding Route 20 work in the hospitality kitchens at the Oceanfront or the construction yards along the Boulevard. A mayor ought to be able to talk to the people who keep the city running."

When Route 20 dropped us off at the Glenrock Mall transit hub in Norfolk, we waited another twenty-eight minutes for our third bus—a southbound Route 15 heading down Military Highway toward Virginia Beach.

By the time the Route 15 bus rolled south along Military Highway and climbed the bridge over the Eastern Branch of the Elizabeth River to cross from Norfolk back into Virginia Beach, it was four-fifteen in the afternoon. Holding my camcorder steady on my lap tray with the river glinting through the bus window behind Katie and Scott, I recorded the conversation that became the centerpiece of our video.

"We're crossing back into Virginia Beach right now, Scott," Katie said on camera, holding up the official printed CVTA timetables for Route 33 and Route 12. "Let's talk about what happens if a taxpaying citizen of Virginia Beach who relies on the bus—like Nate and me, or a working parent, or a senior citizen—wants to attend a formal evening meeting of the Virginia Beach City Council down at the Municipal Center in Courthouse."

"Formal City Council meetings gavel in at six o'clock in the evening," Scott said, looking down at the printed schedule columns in Katie's hand.

"Six o'clock in the evening," Katie repeated clearly. "Now look at the Route 33 schedule serving the Virginia Beach Municipal Center. You can ride Route 33 down to City Hall in the afternoon just fine. If you live at the north end of the Oceanfront, the last northbound Route 33 all the way to Sixty-Eighth Street leaves the Municipal Center at 6:01 PM—one minute after the council meeting starts—and the 7:01 PM trip terminates early at Nineteenth Street. And if you live anywhere else in Virginia Beach and need to ride Route 33 from the Municipal Center back to the CCHR hub to catch your connecting bus home—like Route 12, Route 25, or Route 29—look at the departure times."

Katie tapped her finger against the bottom row of the Route 33 and Route 12 timetables so my camera lens could capture the exact numbers.

"The very last Route 33 out of the Municipal Center to CCHR leaves at 6:33 PM, and by the time it reaches CCHR, every single connecting route except Route 36 has already shut down for the night," Katie said. "Because Nate and I live in Kempsville and ride Route 12 home from CCHR toward Military Highway and Indian River Road, look at when the last Route 12 bus of the entire day departs CCHR: 5:48 PM. To catch that 5:48 PM Route 12 home from CCHR, we have to leave City Hall on the 5:33 PM Route 33 bus—twenty-seven minutes before the six o'clock City Council meeting even starts! Our last bus home literally pulls out of CCHR twelve minutes before the Mayor bangs the gavel."

Scott stared at the two printed timetables in disbelief, looking up into my camera lens. "So if you ride the bus to a six o'clock Virginia Beach City Council meeting to speak during public comment, you're stranded at City Hall with no way home."

"Every single Tuesday night," I said from behind the camera. "You cannot tell citizens that local government is open to the public when City Hall's own bus schedule locks bus riders and wheelchair users out thirty minutes before the meeting begins."

Twenty minutes later, when we stepped off Route 15 at Indian River Road and boarded our fourth and final bus—eastbound Route 12 back to CCHR to complete our circle—the final piece of CVTA's broken math played out right on cue.

Katie and I rolled up the ramp and swiped our thirty-day passes. Behind us, Scott handed his paper transfer slip to the Route 12 operator.

The operator glanced at the punched holes along the top edge of the paper slip and shook his head.

"Sorry, sir—this transfer expired nineteen minutes ago," the driver said, tapping the two-hour rule printed on the farebox. "That'll be another full dollar-fifty fare to ride back to the college."

Right on camera, Scott had to reach into his pocket and feed a second full fare into the box just to finish a single afternoon round-trip back to his parked car at CCHR—solely because waiting for hourly buses had eaten up more than two hours.

***

#### November 2010 to January 2011 — Running for the Kempsville Seat, The Ramp to the Dais, & Fixing the 5:48 PM Curfew

That night, I edited the four-bus ride down into a crisp twelve-minute documentary video with full burned-in open captions and published it at the top of Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit.

Within a week, the video had been watched more than forty thousand times across Hampton Roads and picked up by every newspaper and television station in Norfolk and Virginia Beach. In the November 2008 election, twenty-year incumbent Mayor Myra Overton was defeated, and although the wealthy banking-executive candidate edged out Scott Tanner in the four-way race, the newly elected mayor was forced by the public outcry over our video to appoint two transit riders to Virginia Beach's citizen advisory seats.

Yet when it came time for the eleven-member Virginia Beach City Council to vote on the spring 2009 and spring 2010 municipal budgets, the entrenched council majority—led in our own Kempsville District by ten-year incumbent Councilman Harold Dietz—quietly stripped out the funding for late-evening bus service out of the Municipal Center and CCHR, muttering at a Tuesday afternoon work session that "almost nobody rides the bus out of Courthouse after dark anyway."

Sitting side by side at our desks in our Kempsville house on Lord Nelson Drive that evening watching the archived City Hall webcast, Katie reached over and clicked Pause on the video player.

"Of course nobody rides the bus out of Courthouse after dark, Councilman Dietz," Katie said to the monitor, her voice calm and razor-sharp. "There *isn't* a bus out of Courthouse after 6:33 PM! That's like taking the ramp off a building and claiming nobody in a wheelchair ever comes inside."

Katie turned her SwiftChair toward me, her blue eyes steady.

"Harold Dietz's Kempsville District seat on the Virginia Beach City Council is on the ballot this November, Nate," Katie said. "City Council elections in Virginia are non-partisan, and we've owned our home right here in Kempsville for two years. None of those council members were willing to ride the bus with us in 2008, and now they just voted to keep bus riders locked out of six o'clock Council meetings. What do you say we stop waiting for Councilman Dietz to read our blog—and go take his seat?"

"Let's do it," I said immediately, already opening a fresh PHP project directory on my laptop.

Over the next six months of 2010, our twenty-five-year-old campaign for the non-partisan Kempsville District seat on the Virginia Beach City Council didn't look like anything the Courthouse political establishment had ever seen.

We didn't hire a campaign consultant, we didn't take a dime from real-estate developers, and we didn't hold a single cocktail fundraiser. Instead, I coded a custom PHP voter-turnout and sidewalk-canvassing web application on our home server, mapping every precinct in Kempsville, Bellamy Manor, Brandon, and Larkspur. Every weekday morning and afternoon, Katie and I campaigned directly from the dual front wheelchair bays of CVTA Routes 12, 15, 25, and 29, talking with bus commuters, CCHR students, and working parents. Every evening and weekend—with Dad driving down from Alexandria on Saturdays and Sundays to walk up front-porch steps and hand our palm cards to homeowners while Katie and I rolled our SwiftChairs right up the driveway to the bottom step—we talked face-to-face with thousands of Kempsville neighbors about neighborhood drainage, missing sidewalks, covered bus shelters, and opening City Hall to working people.

Councilman Harold Dietz didn't take us seriously until October, when he showed up at a Kempsville Civic League candidates' night without a single note and Katie dismantled his ten-year voting record on infrastructure and transit line by line from memory.

On Tuesday, November 2, 2010, twenty-five-year-old Katie Prairie defeated incumbent Councilman Harold Dietz by eleven percentage points to become the newest member of the Virginia Beach City Council.

Before Katie's swearing-in ceremony in January 2011, City Hall facilities crews had to remove the steps on the right side of the curved mahogany City Council dais at the Virginia Beach Municipal Center and construct a permanent, carpeted wheelchair ramp up to the Kempsville District desk.

When twenty-five-year-old Councilwoman Katie Prairie rolled up that ramp on the first Tuesday evening of January 2011—with me holding our bound leather copy of the Constitution of the Commonwealth of Virginia beside her wheelchair as she placed her left hand on the page and swore her oath of office ending with a clear, ringing *"So help me, God!"*—the very first ordinance and CVTA Transportation Service Plan budget amendment she introduced on the eleven-member Council was the Evening Civic Access Transit Pulse.

Katie didn't just drop the resolution into the clerk's hopper and hope for the best. Over the next eight weeks, she and I sat down one-on-one with every single member of the Virginia Beach City Council, showing them the exact dollar-for-dollar operating math on my laptop and bringing thirty working commuters, CCHR night-class students, and disabled citizens to the afternoon sessions to testify.

In May 2011, by a nine-to-two roll-call vote, Councilwoman Katie Prairie won passage of her landmark amendment—extending CVTA Route 33 out of the Municipal Center until 10:30 PM on Council nights and funding a synchronized 9:00 PM and 10:00 PM evening connecting pulse for Routes 12, 25, 29, and 36 at the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus hub.

For the first time in the history of Virginia Beach, any citizen who rode a public bus or used a wheelchair could attend a six o'clock City Council meeting, speak at the public microphone, and ride a CVTA bus safely home afterward.

And on every single Tuesday night when the Virginia Beach City Council adjourned at nine-thirty, Councilwoman Katie Prairie and I rolled down the dais ramp, rolled out to the Municipal Center bus shelter alongside the citizens who had come to watch the meeting, swiped our thirty-day CVTA passes, and rode the 9:45 PM Route 33 and Route 12 pulse right back home to Kempsville.

### Part 3: Stopped at the Third-Floor Gallery by Capitol Police, Seated on the House Floor Near Delegate Ronald Villa, & The Spark to Run

**Date:** 2012-02-09 at 11:35 America/New_York

#### Thursday, February 9, 2012, 11:35 AM — Stopped Outside the Third-Floor Public Viewing Gallery by Capitol Police

"The public viewing gallery for the House of Delegates should be right around this corner on the left, Nate," twenty-six-year-old Katie said as the brass doors of the small historic passenger elevator slid open on the third floor of the Virginia State Capitol.

Side by side, we guided our compact SwiftChairs out of the narrow elevator cab onto the polished heart-pine floorboards of the third-floor balcony corridor.

It was a cold Thursday morning in February 2012, midway through the winter session of the Virginia General Assembly and thirteen months into Katie's term representing the Kempsville District on the Virginia Beach City Council. Earlier that morning at nine o'clock, after riding up from Hampton Roads to testify both as a Virginia Beach City Councilwoman and as co-founder of Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit, Katie and I had presented our evening ridership numbers and bus-stop accessibility data to a House Transportation subcommittee down in the General Assembly Building—urging the state legislature not to slash the state transit operating funds that matched our new evening bus service in Virginia Beach. Since our afternoon train back home didn't leave until four o'clock, we had rolled through the underground Capitol tunnel hoping to sit in the public viewing gallery and watch the full House of Delegates convene at noon.

Ahead of us, a line of schoolteachers, college students, and day-trippers was filing past the velvet rope into the steeply tiered wooden benches of the third-floor House Public Gallery.

Before our front casters reached the doorway, however, a uniformed Virginia Division of Capitol Police lieutenant standing beside the gallery entrance stepped gently into the center of the corridor and held up a gloved hand.

"Good morning, folks—hold up just a second for me," the Capitol Police lieutenant said, his tone polite but firm as he looked down at our two power wheelchairs.

Katie and I braked our SwiftChairs together. "Good morning, Lieutenant," Katie said calmly. "We already cleared the security screening downstairs at the visitor entrance. We're just heading into the public viewing gallery to watch the noon House session."

"Yes, ma'am, I see your visitor stickers," the lieutenant replied, glancing toward the narrow gallery threshold and then back at our heavy powerchairs with genuine concern on his face. "I'm very sorry, folks, but we can't bring wheelchairs into the third-floor public viewing gallery."

My eyebrows drew together. "Is there not a level space at the top of the gallery aisle?"

"It's not the floor space inside the doorway, sir—it's emergency evacuation safety," the Capitol Police lieutenant explained earnestly, gesturing back toward the small retrofit elevator we had just ridden up and the steep, winding eighteenth-century stairwell at the end of the hall. "Thomas Jefferson designed this building in 1788. If a fire alarm goes off or we have to order an emergency evacuation of the Capitol while the House is in session, that passenger elevator automatically locks out and recalls to the ground floor. From this third-floor gallery, the only way out of the building is down three flights of steep, narrow historic stairs. My officers have no safe way to carry power wheelchairs down three stories of stairs in an emergency, and I can't put the two of you in a position where we couldn't get you out of the building safely."

Katie and I looked at each other across our armrests. Having spent more than ten years navigating every flavor of architectural barrier in Virginia, both of us could tell immediately that the Capitol Police officer wasn't trying to brush us off—he was looking at a genuine life-safety trap in a two-hundred-year-old building and taking his duty seriously.

"So there's nowhere in the State Capitol where a citizen in a wheelchair is allowed to watch their own House of Delegates vote?" Katie asked quietly, not angry at the officer, but with that familiar, steady look in her blue eyes whenever she ran into a systemic wall.

"I didn't say that, ma'am," the Capitol Police lieutenant said with a reassuring half-smile, reaching up to unclip the microphone of his shoulder radio. "Give me two minutes right here by the elevator. I'm going to walk downstairs and speak directly to the Sergeant-at-Arms and House Leadership to explain that we have a member of the public in a wheelchair and her companion upstairs who came to watch the House session, and that Capitol Police have emergency-egress safety concerns with the third-floor gallery. Let me see what Leadership can do."

We parked our SwiftChairs beside the marble bust in the third-floor elevator vestibule and waited while the ten-minute warning bell for the noon floor session chimed overhead.

Four minutes later, the brass indicator needle above the third-floor elevator swung upward. The doors slid open, and the Capitol Police lieutenant stepped out alongside a tall gentleman in a dark three-piece suit wearing the silver badge of the Sergeant-at-Arms of the Virginia House of Delegates.

"Councilwoman Prairie and Mr. Prairie?" the Sergeant-at-Arms asked warmly.

"Yes, sir," I said.

"The Lieutenant just spoke with me and the Speaker's office about the safety concern up here on the third floor," the Sergeant-at-Arms said, holding out two heavy cream-colored cards stamped in gold leaf with the seal of the Commonwealth. "House Leadership does not want any Virginian turned away from watching the people's House in session because of a wheelchair. By order of the Speaker, you and one ADA companion—or in your case, both of you together—have been granted a rare honor today. If you'll ride the elevator down to the first floor with me, we're going to seat you right on the Floor of the Virginia House of Delegates."

Katie's blue eyes widened slightly as she looked across at me, and we rolled our wheelchairs straight back into the elevator cab beside the Sergeant-at-Arms.

***

#### Thursday, February 9, 2012, 11:52 AM — Seated on the House Floor Near Virginia Beach Delegate Ronald Villa & The Spark

When the elevator doors opened on the first floor, the marble rotunda corridor outside the House Chamber was packed three-deep with corporate lobbyists, reporters, and political staffers pressed against the brass velvet rope.

Under the rules of the House of Delegates, members of the general public and registered lobbyists were strictly barred from crossing that threshold onto the Chamber floor during session. Yet as the Sergeant-at-Arms stepped out of the elevator ahead of our two wheelchairs, the doorkeepers immediately unhooked the velvet rope and swung open the tall mahogany double doors.

"Make way, please—honored guests of the House coming through to the Floor," the Sergeant-at-Arms announced.

Thirty-five lobbyists in thousand-dollar suits parted in stunned silence as two twenty-six-year-old twins from Virginia Beach rolled their compact black SwiftChairs straight across the threshold onto the lush green carpet of the Virginia House of Delegates Chamber.

Rolling inside at floor level felt completely different from looking at photographs of the chamber. High above us rose the cream-and-gold coffered ceiling and the glittering crystal chandeliers; directly ahead stood the Speaker's elevated marble rostrum and the twin electronic voting boards listing all one hundred Delegates in alphabetical order. And instead of staring down at the tops of lawmakers' heads from a distant third-floor balcony, we were rolling right past the curved rows of polished mahogany desks where the elected representatives of the Commonwealth sat.

"Right over here on the outer right aisle, folks," the Sergeant-at-Arms whispered kindly, guiding our two wheelchairs to a wide, level carpeted bay near the rear row of desks on the first floor—twenty feet from an emergency ground-level exit door. "I figured two folks from Virginia Beach would feel right at home sitting next to one of your hometown Delegates."

At the mahogany desk immediately beside our wheelchair bay, a dark-haired Delegate in a crisp navy suit—with a brass nameplate on his desk reading *MR. VILLA — VIRGINIA BEACH (21ST DISTRICT)*—turned around in his leather swivel chair to see who was being seated beside him.

The instant his eyes landed on our two wheelchairs, Delegate Ronald Villa broke into a huge, genuine grin and stood right up to shake our hands.

"Katie! Nate!" Delegate Villa said warmly, clasping Katie's right hand and then reaching across to shake mine. "What a surprise! How did the two of you manage to score floor seats in the House of Delegates?"

"We tried to go upstairs to the third-floor public viewing gallery, Delegate Villa," I explained with a grin, "and Capitol Police stopped us because they were worried about how they'd evacuate our wheelchairs down three flights of stairs in an emergency. They talked to House Leadership, and the Sergeant-at-Arms brought us down here to sit next to you."

"Well, I couldn't ask for better company on my aisle!" Delegate Villa laughed, gesturing toward the two neighboring delegates seated to his left. "Hey, guys, let me introduce you to Virginia Beach City Councilwoman Katie Prairie from Kempsville and her twin brother Nathaniel. Long before Katie won her Kempsville Council seat, the two of them started the Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit blog. Back when I was serving At-Large on the Virginia Beach City Council before I was elected to the House, I was actively fighting to get more covered bus shelters built for our bus riders so working folks and seniors wouldn't be standing out in the pouring rain along Kempsville Road, Princess Anne Road, and Holland Road. Every time city staff claimed we didn't know which bus stops needed shelters the most, Katie and Nate had already ridden every route in their wheelchairs and published the exact stop-by-stop boarding numbers and sidewalk photos on their website!"

The two neighboring delegates smiled and shook our hands across our lap trays as the noon bell rang and the Speaker rapped his wooden gavel to call the House of Delegates to order.

For the next hour and fifteen minutes, Katie and I sat side by side in our SwiftChairs right on the green carpet of the House Floor beside Delegate Ronald Villa's desk.

Whenever a bill came up on the electronic calendar board, Delegate Villa leaned over his desk armrest between votes to whisper a quick, friendly explanation of the parliamentary procedure—pointing out how the white request-to-speak buttons worked on the desk consoles, how committee chairmen signaled floor amendments, and how the red and green lights on the big wall boards tallied the yeas and nays in real time. Even though he sat on the Republican side of the aisle and knew our transit blog frequently called out state and local leaders in both parties when public transit got shortchanged, Delegate Villa treated us with the warm, unpretentious respect of a former At-Large City Councilman who genuinely cared about the bus riders in his city.

Then, at twelve-forty-five, the budget amendment Katie and I had testified for at nine o'clock that morning came up on the floor—a measure to restore four million dollars in state operating assistance for local public bus systems across Hampton Roads, Richmond, and Northern Virginia.

Sitting right on the House Floor, Katie and I watched the big electronic voting board on the front wall as the bell chimed and the red and green lights blinked on beside each lawmaker's name.

Right next to us, Delegate Ronald Villa reached forward and pressed his green *YEA* button to support the transit funds for Hampton Roads.

Across the chamber, however, the incumbent Delegate representing our own Eighty-Fifth House District back home in Kempsville—Delegate Richard Tatum, who had ignored three straight emails Katie had sent him asking for a ten-minute meeting to coordinate state matching funds for Virginia Beach's new evening bus routes—didn't even look up from his cell phone as he reached over and slapped his red *NAY* button, helping defeat the transit amendment by six votes.

I turned my head slightly to look at my twin sister.

Katie wasn't looking at the floor carpet anymore. Sitting upright in her compact SwiftChair on the floor of the Virginia House of Delegates, her blue eyes were locked onto the mahogany desks and the big electronic voting board at the front of the room. For the first time in her life, the Virginia General Assembly wasn't a distant, abstract institution behind a television glass screen or three stories below a balcony rail. She was sitting right on the Floor. She could see the buttons on the desks. She could hear how the bills were debated. And she could see firsthand that while she had fought for a full year on the Virginia Beach City Council to get evening buses running out of the Municipal Center, the Eighty-Fifth District's own State Delegate up here in Richmond was voting to starve those very same buses of state matching funds.

When the House recessed at one-fifteen and Delegate Villa shook our hands again before heading off to his afternoon committee meeting—promising to keep working with us on bus shelters and regional transit back home in Virginia Beach—Katie and I rolled out the tall mahogany double doors of the House Chamber into the first-floor marble corridor.

As we rode the elevator down toward the underground Capitol tunnel, Katie reached across our wheelchair armrests and took my right hand in a grip so steady and electric that I felt it all the way to my shoulders.

"Nate," Katie whispered as the elevator doors closed around just the two of us.

"Yeah, Katie?" I asked, looking straight into her eyes.

"Being stopped at the third-floor viewing gallery by Capitol Police and brought down to sit on the House Floor beside Delegate Villa today was the best thing that ever could have happened to us," Katie said, her voice quiet and ringing with unmistakable purpose. "Did you see how ordinary those mahogany desks are when you're sitting right next to them on the green carpet? Just like on City Council back home, all it takes to make room for a power wheelchair at one of those desks is pulling out a single wooden swivel chair."

My breath caught in my chest as I realized what my twin sister was saying.

"Richard Tatum didn't even look up from his phone when he voted against our buses today," Katie continued, squeezing my fingers tightly. "We proved on the Virginia Beach City Council that when you put a bus rider in a wheelchair at the table, you can change the schedules for the entire city. But as long as politicians in Richmond keep cutting state transit funds and blocking disability Medicaid waivers, local City Councils are fighting with one hand tied behind their backs. The next House of Delegates election is in November 2013. Let's go home, talk it over with Dad, and map out how we win the Eighty-Fifth District seat."

### Part 4: Telling Dad at Our Kempsville Kitchen Table, The 125-Signature Ballot Petition at CCHR, & Winning the Eighty-Fifth House District

**Date:** 2012-11-23 at 18:30 America/New_York

#### Friday, November 23, 2012, 6:30 PM — Brainstorming with Dad Around the Kempsville Kitchen Table

"Dad," twenty-seven-year-old Katie said after the three of us had finished our Friday evening Thanksgiving leftovers and I had set three fresh glasses of ice-cold ginger ale down on the oak kitchen table of our house on Lord Nelson Drive in Kempsville, "Nate and I have a big decision we want to talk through with you before the end of the year."

Sitting across the table from our two compact SwiftChairs in his flannel shirt, our fifty-four-year-old dad set his glass down, adjusted his eyeglasses, and looked from Katie's face to mine with a knowing fatherly smile.

"Wait—let me guess," Dad said warmly. "The last time the two of you gave me that exact synchronized twin look across this table was back in 2010 right before Katie told me she was going to run against Harold Dietz for the Kempsville seat on the Virginia Beach City Council. Who rattled your cage this time?"

"Delegate Richard Tatum," Katie answered without missing a beat, sliding my MagnaBook Pro laptop across the table so Dad could see the newly redistricted map of the Eighty-Fifth House District glowing on the screen. "Back in February, when Nate and I rode up to Richmond to testify for state public-transit matching funds, Capitol Police stopped us at the third-floor viewing gallery over emergency-evacuation concerns and House Leadership brought us down to sit right on the Floor of the House of Delegates next to Ron Villa's desk. We sat twenty feet away from Richard Tatum and watched him slap his red Nay button against Hampton Roads bus funding without even looking up from his cell phone."

"And after serving two years on the Virginia Beach City Council," I added, tapping the boundary lines on the laptop screen, "we've seen firsthand that almost everything we're trying to fix locally—dedicated regional funding for CVTA, clearing the state developmental-disability Medicaid waiver waitlist for Virginia families raising adults like our cousin Chelsea up in Newport, Rhode Island, and enforcing real accessibility rules—is controlled up in Richmond by the General Assembly. Look at the new decennial map for the Eighty-Fifth House District, Dad: it covers Kempsville, Bellamy Manor, Brandon, and Larkspur, right around Fairlawn Recreation Center on Marlborough Drive. Eighty-five percent of the precincts in the Eighty-Fifth House District are the exact same Kempsville neighborhoods that elected Katie to City Council in 2010."

Dad leaned over the table, studying the precinct map carefully before looking back up at Katie.

"I know my daughter, and I know that once the two of you set your minds to something, nobody in Virginia is going to stop you," Dad said proudly, though his voice turned serious and practical as he switched into systems-engineer problem-solving mode. "So let's brainstorm this right now from start to finish before the calendar flips to 2013. Walk me through every step between tonight and swearing-in day in January 2014. First question: Virginia Beach City Council is non-partisan under state law. If you run for the Virginia House of Delegates against Richard Tatum next November, you have to declare a party ballot line. How are you filing?"
