"I'm running as a Democrat—Katie Prairie, D-Virginia Beach," Katie said firmly. "Ron Villa has always been fair to us on bus shelters from the Republican side because he came up through City Council, but the House majority caucus in Richmond just spent the whole 2012 session cutting public transit, blocking healthcare expansion, and starving disability services. If I'm going up to Richmond to fight for working families, bus riders, and disabled Virginians, I want to be completely honest with the voters about where I stand."

"Good," Dad nodded, pulling a yellow legal pad and a ballpoint pen toward his elbow. "Second question: what happens to your Kempsville seat on the Virginia Beach City Council during the campaign—and what happens to your software business here at home if you win?"

"Under Virginia law, a sitting City Council member does not have to resign her municipal seat just to run for the General Assembly," I explained, opening the Code of Virginia tab I had bookmarked in our browser. "Katie's four-year City Council term runs through 2014, and the House of Delegates election is in November 2013. That means Katie keeps representing Kempsville on the Virginia Beach City Council through the entire 2013 campaign so our neighbors never lose their vote at City Hall. Once she wins the House seat in November 2013, she'll serve through the final December 2013 Council meeting and then formally resign her City Council seat effective December 31, 2013—right before taking her oath in the House of Delegates on the second Wednesday in January 2014."

"And because the Virginia General Assembly is a part-time citizen legislature," Katie added, "session only runs for sixty days in even-numbered years and forty-five days in odd-numbered years during January and February. Ten months out of every year, Nate and I stay right here in our house on Lord Nelson Drive running our software business and riding CVTA buses around the district. For January and February, we'll rent a one-bedroom accessible apartment right on a JRTC bus route in Richmond."

Dad wrote *Jan–Feb: 60/45-day Winter Session (Richmond Bus Route Apt)* at the top of his legal pad, then tapped his pen below it.

"Alright—now walk me through the official election-law timeline under Title 24.2 of the Code of Virginia," Dad said. "What paperwork has to be filed between January 1, 2013 and the ballot deadline so Tatum's lawyers can't try to knock you off the ballot on a technicality?"

I rotated the second browser tab toward Dad, where Katie and I had already built a step-by-step statutory checklist.

"Step one happens on January 2, 2013," I said. "We file Katie's Statement of Organization with the Virginia State Board of Elections in the COMET campaign finance system, register *Katie Prairie for Delegate*, and open a dedicated campaign checking account at our credit union within ten days. Under state law, every candidate committee has to designate an official Campaign Treasurer who lives in Virginia. Katie and I want me to serve as Campaign Manager and Chief Technology Officer—and we want to know if you'll serve as our official Campaign Treasurer, Dad."

Dad's chest swelled visibly behind his glasses. "Try and stop me. I balanced our household books down to the penny when I was putting myself through CCHR after the divorce—I'll make sure every campaign finance report we file with Richmond reconciles to the cent. What's step two?"

"Step two is ballot qualification between January 2 and the late-March primary filing deadline," Katie said, pointing to the four official State Board of Elections forms we had downloaded:

"To qualify for the June Democratic Primary ballot in a House of Delegates district," Katie explained, "a candidate has to file four things before five o'clock on the March filing deadline: first, the Certificate of Candidate Qualification under Section 24.2-501 certifying that I'm a qualified voter and resident of the Eighty-Fifth House District; second, the Declaration of Candidacy under Section 24.2-520 along with the party primary filing fee paid to the Virginia Beach Treasurer; third, the General Assembly Statement of Economic Interests under Section 30-111 disclosing our fifty-fifty software company and our house here in Kempsville; and fourth—the most important part—the Petition of Qualified Voters under Section 24.2-506."

"How many valid voter signatures does the Code of Virginia require for a House of Delegates candidate?" Dad asked, poised with his pen.

"The statute requires a minimum of one hundred and twenty-five signatures from qualified, registered voters residing inside the Eighty-Fifth House District, witnessed by a circulator who signs the affidavit on the back of each legal-sized petition sheet before a notary public," I said. "Professional campaigns sometimes get sloppy and turn in two hundred signatures, and then get disqualified when fifty of the signers turn out to live across the street in the Eighty-First or Twenty-First District. So we aren't turning in one hundred and twenty-five signatures. We're going to collect four hundred verified Eighty-Fifth District signatures—and before a single voter signs our clipboard, I'm going to look up their street address live on my laptop against the official Eighty-Fifth District registered-voter file so we have a one-hundred-percent validity rate."

"And where are we collecting those four hundred signatures?" Dad asked with a grin, already guessing the answer.

"Right at the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus bus transfer loop, along the Kempsville Road and Princess Anne Road bus stops, and out on the neighborhood sidewalks every Saturday morning in January and February," Katie smiled, reaching across our armrests to take my hand while extending her other hand across the table to Dad. "No paid petition mercenaries from out of town. Just the three of us."

Dad placed his large, warm hand over both of ours in the center of the kitchen table.

"Then on Saturday, January fifth, I'm driving down from Alexandria with three legal-sized clipboards and a box of black pens," Dad said. "Let's put a bus rider in the House of Delegates."

***

#### January to December 2013 — 412 Signatures at CCHR, Canvassing the 85th District, & Passing the Baton on City Council

On the first Saturday morning of January 2013, right after Katie and I filed her Statement of Organization with Dad listed as Treasurer, the three of us set up at the edge of the concrete passenger island at the CCHR Virginia Beach Campus bus loop.

Every time a CVTA Route 12, Route 25, Route 29, or Route 33 bus pulled into the college transfer hub, dozens of Kempsville residents stepped off the doors and recognized Katie immediately as the twenty-seven-year-old City Councilwoman in the black SwiftChair who had fought to keep their evening buses running until ten-thirty at night.

"Councilwoman Prairie! Are those petitions to run for State Delegate against Richard Tatum?" a CCHR nursing instructor asked excitedly on our very first morning, spotting the legal-sized State Board of Elections sheets clipped to Katie's lap tray. "Give me a pen right now—and wait two seconds while I call my husband and my daughter over from the Route 12 shelter so they can sign too!"

With Dad standing beside our wheelchairs holding the extra clipboards and signing the circulator affidavits in front of a mobile notary at the end of each afternoon, and with me verifying every signer's address and precinct number on my laptop on the spot, we didn't just hit the statutory minimum of one hundred and twenty-five signatures—by the end of February, we had collected four hundred and twelve verified signatures from registered Eighty-Fifth District voters.

When the five-o'clock primary filing deadline closed on the last Thursday of March 2013, not a single Democratic challenger had filed against a popular sitting Kempsville City Councilwoman who had turned in four hundred and twelve bulletproof grassroots signatures. The Virginia State Board of Elections officially certified Katie as the Democratic Nominee for the Eighty-Fifth House District without the expense of a June primary.

Two days later, a pair of sleek political consultants from Richmond rode down I-64 and asked to meet us at a coffee shop near Pembroke Commons. Spreading glossy brochures across the table, they informed Katie that to run a "serious" House of Delegates campaign in Virginia Beach, she needed to pay their firm a forty-thousand-dollar retainer to lease the state party's voter-targeting software, hire an out-of-state direct-mail agency, and host high-dollar donor receptions at Oceanfront hotels.

Katie and I listened politely, took a sip of our ginger ales, and turned their entire forty-thousand-dollar sales pitch down flat.

"My brother and I are professional software engineers," Katie told the stunned consultants before we rolled out to catch our bus home. "Nate already wrote our custom PHP precinct-mapping and voter-turnout engine for my 2010 City Council victory, and it runs faster, cleaner, and with better precinct math than anything on K Street. Every dollar small donors give our campaign is going straight into printing accessible literature and reaching voters in Kempsville—not paying commissions to Richmond consultants."

From April through November 2013, the three of us ran the most disciplined grassroots campaign the Eighty-Fifth District had ever seen.

Every weekday after our software client work and Katie's City Council duties, Katie and I rode CVTA buses across the district and rolled down the residential sidewalks of Kempsville, Bellamy Manor, Brandon, and Larkspur. Whenever a house had two or three brick steps up to the front porch, Dad—who drove down from Alexandria every Friday night through Sunday evening, joined during the weekdays by a dozen volunteer CCHR students and fellow bus riders—walked up the steps to knock on the door while Katie and I parked our SwiftChairs right at the bottom of the front walk so the homeowner could step out onto the porch and talk directly with Katie eye-to-eye.

By mid-October, internal polling showed that Katie had erased Delegate Richard Tatum's twelve-point incumbent cushion.

Panicking during the final ten days of the race, Tatum's Richmond campaign consultants dropped a glossy, full-color attack mailer into every mailbox in Kempsville showing a photograph of the steps of the State Capitol with the headline: *THE GENERAL ASSEMBLY MOVES FAST—CAN KATIE PRAIRIE KEEP UP?* Inside, the mailer sneered that Katie "doesn't even hold a driver's license" and claimed that two twenty-eight-year-old twins who rode the city bus would be "out of their depth in Richmond."

The backlash across Virginia Beach was instantaneous.

Disabled veterans from Naval Air Station Oceana and Little Creek, parents of special-needs children at Morrison Middle School and Northwood High School, senior citizens along Kempsville Road, and hundreds of commuters who rode the evening buses Katie had championed on City Council flooded the local newspaper letters page and local television broadcasts with outrage. Even former At-Large City Councilman and sitting Republican Delegate Ronald Villa over in the neighboring Twenty-First District told a Norfolk newspaper reporter on the record, *"Anyone who thinks Katie and Nate Prairie can't keep up at the State Capitol has never watched those two work a transportation budget."*

On Election Night—Tuesday, November 5, 2013—sitting side by side with Dad at our kitchen table on Lord Nelson Drive tracking the eighteen precincts of the Eighty-Fifth House District on my PHP dashboard, we watched the final returns lock in at 8:15 PM:

*Katie Prairie (D): 54.4% — Richard Tatum (R, Incumbent): 45.6%.*

At twenty-eight years old, my twin sister was officially Delegate-Elect Katie Prairie of the Eighty-Fifth House District.

Over the next eight weeks of November and December 2013, we completed every statutory transition step between Election Night and Opening Day:

First, on the Wednesday and Thursday after the election, the three-member Virginia Beach Electoral Board met at the Municipal Center to conduct the official canvass of precinct returns, transmitting the certified abstract up to the Virginia State Board of Elections in Richmond under Code of Virginia Section 24.2-675 so the Secretary of the Board could mail Katie her official, gold-sealed Certificate of Election.

Second, during the first week of December, we traveled up to Richmond for the one-day House Clerk's administrative intake—receiving Katie's office assignment in Room 518 on the fifth floor of the General Assembly Building—and met via video conference with attorneys at the Division of Legislative Services to draft and pre-file her first three legislative bills on regional transit funding, developmental-disability Medicaid waiver slots, and statewide ADA building enforcement ahead of the December pre-filing deadline.

Third, on the third Tuesday evening of December 2013, Katie rolled up the permanent ramp to the Virginia Beach City Council dais at the Municipal Center one last time. After casting her final municipal votes and thanking the people of Kempsville for three unforgettable years of service on City Council, Katie formally submitted her resignation from the non-partisan Kempsville Council seat effective at 11:59 PM on December 31, 2013—welcoming her newly elected Kempsville Council successor, Councilwoman Amelia Thornton, so she would never hold dual municipal and state legislative office when her House term began.

When Katie and I rolled out of the Municipal Center at nine-thirty that December night and boarded the 9:45 PM CVTA Route 33 bus that she had fought to create, the driver saluted her from behind the wheel.

"Next stop for you two is the State Capitol up in Richmond, Delegate-Elect Prairie!" the driver beamed.

"Only for forty-five and sixty days every winter," Katie smiled, holding my hand across the center aisle between our two wheelchair bays as the bus rolled toward CCHR. "And on Friday, January third, Nate and I are moving into an apartment right on West Broad Street so we can ride the Richmond city bus to the Capitol too."

## Freshman Session on West Broad Street & Riding JRTC Route 6 to the Capitol

### Part 1: The West Broad Street Loft, Unpacking Our Shared Queen Bed, & Weekend Reconnaissance on JRTC Route 6

**Date:** 2014-01-03 at 14:15 America/New_York

#### Friday, January 3, 2014, 2:15 PM — Five Days Ahead of Opening Day at the Broad & Boulevard Lofts

"I still apologize that we only had a one-bedroom accessible apartment left on the ground floor for the legislative session, Delegate Prairie," the property manager of the Broad & Boulevard Lofts said nervously as he held open the wide oak door of Apartment 104 and handed twenty-eight-year-old Katie two brass keys and a pair of electronic lobby fobs. "When the General Assembly convenes every January, the two-bedroom corporate units book up months in advance. If you and your brother need us to roll a folding cot into the living room—"

"No cot needed at all, Mr. Henderson, thank you," Katie said with a warm, reassuring smile from her compact black SwiftChair as she rolled smoothly across the threshold ahead of me and our fifty-five-year-old dad. "A one-bedroom apartment with one queen bed is actually the exact layout my brother and I asked for."

Behind us, pushing a wheeled bellhop cart loaded with our two dark-blue comforter bags from home, our dual laptop stands, and two plastic crates of kitchen staples that he had driven down I-95 from Alexandria in the bed of his pickup truck to meet our morning ARTS train from Norfolk, Dad chuckled softly at the property manager's polite blink of surprise.

Once Mr. Henderson wished us a good winter in Richmond and stepped back out into the wide brick-and-timber hallway, I clicked the deadbolt shut and looked around our new sixty-day winter sanctuary.

It was Friday, January 3, 2014—five full days before the Virginia General Assembly would gavel in at noon on Wednesday, January 8 for Katie's very first session representing the Eighty-Fifth House District back home in Kempsville and Virginia Beach.

When Katie had flipped the Eighty-Fifth District seat two months earlier in November 2013—running on the exact public-transit, disability-access, and working-family platform we had spent the previous eight years championing on Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit—party staffers in Richmond had automatically sent us brochures for upscale high-rise apartments down in Shockoe Slip and luxury suites near the downtown hotels where veteran lawmakers stayed during the winter session.

Katie and I had taken one look at the transit map of Richmond and turned every single one of those downtown hotel brochures down.

Instead, we had chosen Apartment 104 inside the Broad & Boulevard Lofts—a renovated brick warehouse building on West Broad Street right near Boulevard at the edge of Scott's Addition and the Museum District. The building had a flush concrete entrance off the wide West Broad Street sidewalk with zero steps, blue ADA push-button automatic doors, forty-two-inch interior doorways, low-pile commercial carpet, and a roll-in bathroom with steel grab bars. Even better, thirty feet outside the front lobby glass stood a blue-and-white bus stop sign for the James River Transit Company—Richmond's public bus network, universally known across the capital region as the JRTC Transit System.

"Come look at this view out the living room windows, Nate," Katie called out, rolling her SwiftChair across the open floor plan toward the two tall industrial sash windows facing south over West Broad Street.

I rolled up beside her left armrest while Dad carried our bedding bags into the bedroom. Right below our first-floor windowsill, a blue-and-white forty-foot JRTC Stalwart low-floor transit bus with an amber LED destination sign reading *6 BROAD STREET — DOWNTOWN / CAPITOL* glided past the curb.

"Four separate JRTC trunk routes converge right along this stretch of West Broad Street," I said, resting my hand over Katie's fingers on her armrest as we watched a westbound bus pass in the opposite lane thirty seconds later. "Route 6 runs straight down Broad Street past the Science Museum and Central Virginia University to Ninth and Broad at Capitol Square in eleven minutes, and straight west to the shops and grocery store at Oak Lawn in ten minutes."

"And arriving five days early gives us all of Saturday and Sunday to learn the bus routes, test every transfer stop, and get our routine locked down before Monday morning's New Member Orientation even starts," Katie said, letting out a quiet, contented breath as her shoulders relaxed.

Because both of us were autistic, walking cold into an unfamiliar city and a brand-new legislative building on the same morning would have been a recipe for sensory overload. Coming up to Richmond five days ahead of Wednesday's Opening Day gavel wasn't a vacation; it was how Katie and I mapped our perimeter so that by the time the session bell rang, every doorway, curb cut, bus stop, and timetable between our bed and the House Floor felt completely predictable.

We rolled into the bedroom to help Dad finish unpacking.

Although the apartment came furnished with a solid oak queen bedframe, a firm mattress, and two nightstands, Katie and I never slept well under stiff, bleach-scented corporate rental linens. Dad had already stripped off the rental bedspread and folded it onto the top closet shelf, and together the three of us spread out our own familiar cotton sheets, our heavy navy-blue winter comforter, and our four pillows from our shared bedroom back home in Kempsville.

"There," Katie said happily, smoothing the corner of our comforter from her wheelchair while I plugged our dual powerchair chargers into the wall outlet at the foot of the bed. "Now it smells like home."

Out in the front room, Dad helped us push the rectangular oak dining table right up in front of the tall windows overlooking West Broad Street. Within forty minutes, we had our two MagnaBook Pro laptops, our dual twenty-four-inch external monitors, our hardware firewall router, and our portable video-capture encoder cabled neatly across the tabletop.

Before Dad headed back out to his pickup truck at five-thirty to beat the Friday evening traffic north up I-95 to his house on Sunset Drive in Alexandria, he stood by the front door and pulled both of us into a long, tight hug across our wheelchair armrests.

"If anybody at that Capitol gives my daughter or my son a hard time next week, you call me," Dad said fiercely, kissing the top of Katie's blonde head and clapping my shoulder.

"We'll be fine, Dad," Katie smiled, hugging him back as I squeezed his forearm. "We've got each other, we've got our laptops, and we've got a bus stop thirty feet from the front door."

That Friday night, after eating a quiet supper of toasted cheese sandwiches and tomato soup at our window table while watching the headlights and blue-and-white JRTC buses roll down West Broad Street, Katie and I transferred out of our SwiftChairs onto the edge of our shared queen bed. Pulling our familiar Kempsville comforter up to our chins in the dark, we curled up facing each other with Katie's forehead resting against my collarbone and her fingers laced tightly through mine—safe inside our new Richmond perimeter.

***

#### Saturday & Sunday, January 4–5, 2014 — Buying Regular 30-Day JRTC Passes & Mapping the Broad Street Spine

At nine o'clock on Saturday morning, bundled up in our matching winter parkas, knit gloves, and wool scarves with our canvas backpacks hooked behind our wheelchair headrests, Katie and I rolled out the automatic glass doors of the Broad & Boulevard Lofts to the West Broad Street bus stop.

The January air was crisp and clear at thirty-six degrees. Three minutes after we braked our SwiftChairs on the concrete waiting pad alongside a Central Virginia University nursing student and two restaurant cooks heading downtown for the Saturday lunch shift, an eastbound JRTC Route 6 Stalwart low-floor bus pulled smoothly to the curb.

The air brakes hissed, and as the front doors folded open and the front air suspension dropped four inches toward the sidewalk, the bus's external automated voice annunciator spoke through the doorway speaker in its crisp, unmistakable cadence—announcing the route once as the doors opened before switching to the safety warnings as the stainless-steel front flip-ramp folded out onto the concrete:

*"Route 6. Downtown. Welcome aboard JRTC!"*

*"Caution: bus is kneeling! Caution: lift is deploying!"*

"Morning, folks! Watch your roll coming aboard," the JRTC operator called out cheerfully, reaching down to flip up the two bench seats over the front left and right wheelchair securement bays.

"Good morning!" Katie said brightly as she rolled up the gentle slope of the low-floor ramp first and backed her SwiftChair deftly into the curbside wheelchair bay, while I rolled right up behind her into the street-side bay across the narrow aisle.

*"Caution: lift is stowing!"* the automated voice announced as the stainless-steel ramp folded back into the front threshold step with a solid clank.

After the operator clicked the four-point retractable floor straps onto our wheelchair frames and returned to his seat, Katie and I each reached forward to the farebox beside the steering column and fed crisp dollar bills into the bill validator to pay our standard one-dollar-and-fifty-cent base fares like any other rider in Richmond.

"Where are you two headed this cold Saturday morning?" the operator asked as he pulled the forty-foot bus back into the eastbound lane of West Broad Street.

"Ninth and Broad first so we can walk over to the JRTC customer service window and buy our thirty-day unlimited magnetic swipe passes for the winter," Katie replied, holding my hand across the center aisle between our two wheelchair bays. "We're staying right back there at Broad and Boulevard through the middle of March."

"Well, you picked the best bus line in the city of Richmond to live on," the driver said with a proud grin in his wide rearview mirror. "Between the 6, the 1, the 2, and the 3, you won't ever wait more than ten minutes on Broad Street from six in the morning till midnight."

As the bus rolled straight east down the wide six-lane boulevard—passing the domed neoclassical rotunda of the Science Museum of Virginia in the old Broad Street Union Station, the brick dormitories and classrooms of Central Virginia University around Monroe Park, and the historic storefronts of the Arts District—I had my pocket notebook open on my lap tray, logging the exact travel times and stop spacing while the interior automated voice announced every single cross street we passed, whether there was a bus stop on that block or not:

*"Robinson Street."*

*"Mulberry Street."*

*"Allison Street."*

*"Meadow Street."*

*"Lombardy Street."*

*"Belvidere Street."*

"Listen to the onboard GPS annunciator, Katie," I murmured with an appreciative grin as the voice called out *"First Street"*, *"Second Street"*, *"Third Street"* in steady sequence down the straight Broad Street corridor. "It announces every single cross street along the route even when it isn't a bus stop—and whenever a JRTC bus completes a turn, it announces both streets on the corner. For a blind rider or someone visiting Richmond for the first time, you always know down to the exact block where you are."

*"Eighth Street. Ninth Street."*

Eleven minutes after boarding outside our apartment, the bus pulled up to the curb at Ninth and Broad Street—directly across from the tall granite tower of the General Assembly Building and the wrought-iron gates of Capitol Square.

*"Caution: bus is kneeling! Caution: lift is deploying!"*

After thanking the driver and rolling down the front ramp onto the brick sidewalk at Ninth and Broad, Katie and I stopped by the downtown transit ticket counter, paid sixty dollars apiece out of our own checking account for two standard thirty-day unlimited JRTC magnetic swipe passes, and spent the next three hours testing the entire downtown pedestrian and transit grid in our powerchairs.

We rolled down the Ninth Street hill to Ninth and Main to time the bus transfer down to Richmond Main Street Station in Shockoe Bottom—listening at the intersection corner as a turning bus announced externally to the crosswalk, *"Caution! Bus is turning right. Pedestrians, look both ways!"* followed inside by both street names as it straightened out onto Main Street. We tested the exterior brick ramps leading into Capitol Square and located the ground-level accessible entrance on the north terrace of Thomas Jefferson's historic white-columned State Capitol. We checked the elevator lobby inside the General Assembly Building at Ninth and Broad, noting exactly how many minutes it took to roll from the Route 6 bus stop curb into the building, clear the security magnetometer, and ride up to the fifth-floor legislative offices.

Then, at one-thirty in the afternoon, we rolled back out to the westbound bus shelter at Ninth and Broad. When the outbound bus pulled up to the curb and the front doors folded open, the automated voice announced once across the sidewalk:

*"Route 6. Oak Lawn. Welcome aboard JRTC!"*

*"Caution: bus is kneeling! Caution: lift is deploying!"*

Rolling aboard, we swiped our new thirty-day JRTC magnetic passes through the farebox reader with a clean electronic *beep* and rode Route 6 all the way past our apartment building out West Broad Street to the Oak Lawn transfer center.

Rolling down the wide sidewalk of the open-air Oak Lawn shopping plaza, we went straight into the Market Square supermarket. With an insulated canvas grocery carrier clipped to the rear frame of each of our SwiftChairs and two hand baskets resting on our lap trays, Katie and I spent an hour picking out two weeks' worth of breakfast cereal, milk, whole-wheat bread, sliced turkey, pasta, fresh apples, and three twelve-packs of ginger ale.

By four o'clock Saturday afternoon, we had ridden JRTC Route 6 ten blocks back east to our apartment stop at Broad and Boulevard, unloaded our groceries into our kitchen cabinets, and spent Sunday afternoon sitting side by side at our front window table writing a two-thousand-word weekend dispatch for Ideas for Coastal Virginia Transit:

> *What Hampton Roads Can Learn from Richmond's Broad Street Trunk Line: How JRTC Uses a Straight Six-Mile Spine to Deliver Ten-Minute Service*

By Sunday night, we knew every bus stop, every curb cut, and every timetable along Broad Street by heart. We were ready for Monday morning.

### Part 2: Gold License Plates vs. JRTC Route 6, Turning Down the Parking Deck Pass, & The 60-Inch ADA Restroom Showdown

**Date:** 2014-01-06 at 08:15 America/New_York

#### Monday, January 6, 2014, 8:15 AM — The First Time Richmond Politicians See a Delegate Roll Off the City Bus

At eight-fifteen on Monday morning—forty-eight hours before the 2014 General Assembly would formally convene—the intersection of Ninth and Broad Street outside the General Assembly Building looked like a luxury auto dealership convention.

All along the curb leading toward the private legislative parking deck at Seventh and Marshall, gleaming black luxury sedans and massive chrome-trimmed SUVs idled in a bumper-to-bumper line. Nearly every bumper bore a special gold-embossed state license plate reading *VIRGINIA — HOUSE OF DELEGATES* or *VIRGINIA — SENATE*, stamped with the lawmaker's low seniority number. On the wide brick sidewalk flanking the bronze revolving doors of the General Assembly Building, groups of veteran Delegates, State Senators in tailored wool overcoats, and high-priced corporate lobbyists stood clutching leather briefcases and paper coffee cups from the downtown luxury hotels, trading back-slaps and committee gossip in the cold morning sunshine.

None of them paid the slightest attention to the public JRTC bus stop sign planted at the curb thirty feet from the General Assembly Building's accessible side doors—where half a dozen state clerical workers and hospital aides were waiting to step off the morning bus.

With a low rumble of its diesel engine and a crisp hiss of air brakes, our eastbound blue-and-white JRTC Route 6 Broad Street bus pulled up to the Ninth and Broad curb stop after calling out *"Eighth Street"* and *"Ninth Street"* inside the cabin.

As the front doors folded open, the external automated voice rang out across the sidewalk:

*"Route 6. Downtown. Welcome aboard JRTC!"*

*"Caution: bus is kneeling! Caution: lift is deploying!"*

Inside the front vestibule, where Katie and I had just ridden the eleven-minute trip down from Broad and Boulevard after swiping our regular sixty-dollar monthly passes at the farebox, the operator unhooked our floor straps as the stainless-steel wheelchair ramp settled onto the brick sidewalk.

"Have a great first week at the Capitol, Katie and Nate!" the operator called out warmly over his shoulder. "Show 'em how we do things on Route 6!"

"Thank you, Marcus! See you at five-thirty this evening!" Katie called back cheerfully.

Wearing a tailored royal-blue wool blazer over a crisp white blouse—with the small gold seal of the Virginia House of Delegates pinned to her left lapel and her leather legislative portfolio resting on her lap tray—twenty-eight-year-old Freshman Delegate Katie Prairie eased her joystick forward and rolled her compact black SwiftChair briskly down the city bus ramp onto the Ninth and Broad sidewalk.

Three seconds later, wearing my charcoal wool jacket with both of our MagnaBook Pro laptops strapped securely across my lap tray, I rolled my matching SwiftChair down the bus ramp right behind my twin sister.

*"Caution: lift is stowing!"* the automated voice announced behind us as the bus ramp folded back up with a hydraulic *clank* and the blue-and-white JRTC bus pulled away into the Broad Street traffic.

Outside the General Assembly Building doors, the morning chatter stopped dead.

Six veteran Delegates, two senior State Senators who had just stepped out of a town car, and a circle of corporate telecommunications lobbyists standing beside the granite columns all froze in their tracks. Three of the lobbyists literally lowered their coffee cups halfway from their mouths, staring from the retreating tailpipe of the city transit bus to the gold House of Delegates member pin gleaming on the lapel of Katie's blazer as our two power wheelchairs rolled side by side toward the entrance.

One of the veteran lawmakers—a silver-haired, five-term Delegate from Northern Virginia who sat on the House Transportation Committee and still had his gold-plated SUV keys dangling from his gloved fingers—took two steps across the brick sidewalk and blinked down at us in utter bewilderment.

"Excuse me—Delegate-Elect Prairie?" he stammered, glancing back over his shoulder toward the empty bus stop curb as if checking for a hidden camera crew. "From the Eighty-Fifth District in Virginia Beach?"

"Good morning, Delegate!" Katie said brightly, braking her SwiftChair beside mine to shake his hand. "And this is my twin brother and senior legislative aide, Nathaniel Prairie."

"Did—did the two of you just get off the *city bus*?" the veteran Delegate asked, his voice loud enough that a dozen other lawmakers and lobbyists turned their heads to listen. "Don't you have a handicap van or a legislative driver? The Clerk's office reserves oversized accessible spaces on the ground level of the Seventh Street legislative parking deck for members!"

Katie exchanged a quick, amused twin look with me before looking back up at him with complete, unbothered sincerity.

"Why on earth would we drive a car in downtown traffic when JRTC Route 6 picks us up thirty feet outside our apartment on West Broad Street every ten minutes and drops us right here at the front door of the General Assembly Building?" Katie asked smoothly. "A thirty-day unlimited JRTC bus pass costs sixty dollars at the ticket window, the low-floor ramp takes ten seconds to deploy, and my brother and I get to spend our eleven-minute commute talking with the regular working people of Richmond who ride the bus every day. That's how we commute back home on CVTA in Virginia Beach and Norfolk too."

Around the entrance plaza, several lobbyists exchanged stunned glances—realizing at once that the twenty-eight-year-old software developer from Virginia Beach who had just unseated an incumbent by campaigning from her power wheelchair on public buses wasn't doing it as a campaign-season photo op. She actually lived it every single day.

Five minutes later, when we rolled inside the warm marble lobby of the General Assembly Building and checked in at the Clerk's administrative orientation table, the facilities coordinator smiled and slid a thick manila packet across the counter toward Katie's lap tray.

"Welcome to Richmond, Delegate Prairie," the coordinator said, holding up a plastic windshield RFID transponder alongside a DMV application form for special official *HOUSE OF DELEGATES* license plates. "Because both you and your legislative aide use power wheelchairs, we went ahead and blocked off two contiguous van-accessible spaces right next to the basement tunnel elevator in the General Assembly Parking Deck. Here is your garage transponder and your legislative license-plate form."

Katie gently pushed the plastic garage transponder and the *HOUSE OF DELEGATES* license-plate application right back across the counter.

"Thank you so much for thinking of us," Katie said kindly, "but neither Nate nor I can drive due to our medical restrictions, and neither of us owns a car—so there isn't any vehicle to put House of Delegates license plates on. Whenever anyone does drive us in a private vehicle, the only person we want behind the wheel is our dad in his own truck, and as a State Delegate I'm thankfully not the President of the United States being forced to ride around in an armored Secret Service limousine. We bought our thirty-day JRTC bus passes on Saturday and we're riding Route 6 down Broad Street every morning and evening. Please reassign those two ground-floor parking spaces to Capitol custodians or building maintenance staff who have to drive in before dawn—they deserve a close parking spot a whole lot more than we do."

The orientation clerk stared at the returned garage transponder on the table as if nobody in the four-hundred-year history of the Virginia General Assembly had ever voluntarily given back a reserved downtown parking pass.

***

#### Monday Afternoon & Tuesday, January 6–7, 2014 — The Steel Tape Measure & The 60-Inch ADA Restroom Showdown

While Katie didn't care one bit about a VIP parking space or a gold license plate, we discovered at one-thirty that Monday afternoon what she *was* willing to go to war over before her first session even gaveled in.

After spending the morning getting our legislative network credentials, setting up Katie's new fifth-floor office in Room 518 of the General Assembly Building, and eating a quick lunch in the basement cafeteria, Katie and I rode the elevator down to the underground concourse tunnel and rolled up into the first floor of the historic State Capitol to inspect the accessible desk that the Clerk's staff had modified for her at the end of the minority aisle on the House Floor.

The desk itself on the green carpet of the House Chamber was fine—they had removed the standard mahogany swivel chair so Katie's compact SwiftChair could roll straight under the writing surface within easy reach of the gooseneck microphone and the electronic voting buttons.

The problem came ten minutes later, when we rolled out into the first-floor marble corridor near the House cloakroom and Katie went to use the nearest ladies' restroom while I rolled toward the men's room next door.

Less than thirty seconds after Katie pushed her way past the heavy, spring-loaded solid-oak outer door of the women's restroom—which didn't even have a blue ADA automatic push-button opener on the wall—she backed right back out into the corridor with her jaw set and a familiar flash of storm-cloud steel in her blue eyes.

At the exact same moment, I backed my SwiftChair out of the men's restroom entrance twenty feet down the hall.

"How bad is the women's side?" I asked, rolling quickly up beside her.

"Go look inside the doorway while it's empty, Nate," Katie said tightly, reaching into the side pocket of my backpack and pulling out the twenty-five-foot retractable steel tape measure we always carried for site audits. "It's the exact same fake 'retrofitted' stall they have up on the fifth floor near our office."

I checked that the restroom was unoccupied and looked inside the entrance as Katie pulled out the yellow steel tape blade and measured the end stall.

Whoever had signed off on the restroom renovation twenty years earlier had clearly never sat in a wheelchair a day in their life. They had simply bolted two chrome grab bars onto the tile wall of a forty-two-inch-wide stall at the end of a narrow forty-inch aisle and hung an *ACCESSIBLE* plaque on a stall door that swung *inward* toward the porcelain toilet bowl.

When Katie rolled her compact SwiftChair inside the stall frame, her front footrests stopped two inches short of the toilet bowl while the rear battery casing of her wheelchair still stuck eight inches out past the door jamb—making it physically impossible to pull the inward-swinging stall door closed behind her. To use the restroom at all, any wheelchair user would either have to leave the stall door wide open to the entire room or take two separate elevators down through the underground tunnel to a single unisex medical restroom in the far basement corner of the complex—a twenty-minute round trip that would cause a sitting Delegate to miss roll-call floor votes.

"Forty-two inches wide by fifty-four inches deep, with an inward-swinging door and zero sixty-inch turning circle," I said, writing the exact measurements into my notebook while Katie snapped the steel tape measure back into its casing. "That fails federal ADA Accessibility Guidelines Section 4.17.3 on three separate counts."

"And there are plenty of extra square feet at the end of both restroom rows if they simply shift the outer laminate partition twelve inches toward the dead vanity corner and reverse the hinges so the stall door swings outward," Katie said, her voice calm, quiet, and utterly immovable. "Come on, Nate. Let's go pay a visit to the Department of General Services Facilities Director before two-thirty."

Ten minutes later, Katie and I rolled our two SwiftChairs side by side into the Capitol Facilities Management suite on the lower level.

When the Deputy Director for Capitol Facilities came out to the reception counter in his gray suit, he offered Katie the indulgent, patronizing smile of a career bureaucrat who assumed a twenty-eight-year-old freshman in the minority party could be brushed off with paperwork jargon.

"Delegate-Elect Prairie, we appreciate your feedback," the Deputy Director said smoothly, folding his hands across his stomach, "and I assure you that accessibility upgrades are part of our long-term Master Capitol Complex Modernization Plan. However, modifying restroom partitions and installing motorized door actuators in the General Assembly Building and the Capitol requires an architectural engineering review, historical preservation sign-off, and a capital-outlay work order. Realistically, we can put that in the queue for the summer interim after the session adjourns in March."

Katie didn't raise her voice by a single decibel. Instead, she set our steel tape measure down on the counter with a crisp *clack* and looked straight up into his eyes from her SwiftChair.

"Let me explain what is actually going to happen between now and Wednesday at twelve o'clock noon," Katie said evenly. "The partitions on the fifth floor of the General Assembly Building and in the first-floor corridor restrooms outside the House Chamber aren't Thomas Jefferson's historic plaster—they're standard bolt-together powder-coated steel commercial bathroom dividers installed in the nineteen-nineties. Under federal ADA Accessibility Guidelines Section 4.17.3, a standard wheelchair toilet stall is required by federal law to provide a minimum sixty-inch clear width and a stall door that clears the turning envelope. Right now, a sitting member of the Virginia House of Delegates cannot close the bathroom stall door in her own workplace."

The Deputy Director shifted his weight uncomfortably. "Delegate Prairie, our carpenter and electrical shops are completely booked with pre-session podium setups—"

"At twelve o'clock noon on Wednesday, every television network camera in the Commonwealth of Virginia is going to be rolling live in the House Chamber for Opening Day," Katie continued without blinking, her left hand resting on my wrist across our wheelchair armrests. "If the fifth-floor restrooms and the first-floor House corridor restrooms do not have outward-swinging stall doors, a full sixty-inch turning clearance, and working ADA push-button outer door openers by five o'clock tomorrow afternoon, my very first act as a sworn Delegate on Wednesday will be rising on a point of personal privilege on live statewide television—and inviting the Speaker of the House and the entire Capitol press corps to follow me out into the hallway with their cameras so the voters of Virginia can watch a female Delegate in a power wheelchair get wedged halfway out of an illegal forty-two-inch bathroom stall door because Facilities told her to wait until March."

The silence in the Facilities office was absolute.

Behind the reception glass, two maintenance supervisors who had been listening over their clipboards traded a wide-eyed look. The Deputy Director's face went pale as he pictured the six-o'clock Wednesday evening news showing a newly sworn twenty-eight-year-old Delegate in a wheelchair stuck in an open bathroom stall door while trying to cast her first floor votes.

"I—let me call the carpentry foreman and the electrical shop chief right now," the Deputy Director said quickly, reaching for his desk telephone. "We have heavy-duty commercial partition brackets and two power-door actuator kits in the maintenance warehouse off Fourteenth Street."

"Thank you," Katie said politely, picking our steel tape measure back up off the counter. "Nate and I will be working in Room 518 until five o'clock today and all day tomorrow. Feel free to ask us to test the turning radius with our chairs before your crew bolts the brackets down."

By four o'clock on Tuesday afternoon—twenty hours before the Opening Day gavel—the carpentry and electrical crews had shifted the end partitions fourteen inches into the dead alcove space, reversed the heavy chrome hinges so the stall doors swung cleanly outward with interior loop pulls, and mounted blue wireless ADA push-button door openers outside both the women's and men's restrooms on the fifth floor and outside the House Chamber.

When the head carpenter asked Katie and me to test the finished stalls at four-fifteen on Tuesday, Katie rolled her SwiftChair effortlessly inside, spun a full three-hundred-and-sixty-degree circle without her footrests touching a single wall, pulled the outward-swinging door shut with the interior D-ring handle, and rolled back out to shake every carpenter and electrician's hand in person.

"Nobody should ever have to beg for the right to close a bathroom door in privacy," Katie told me quietly as we rolled out the front entrance of the General Assembly Building at five o'clock that Tuesday evening to catch our westbound JRTC Route 6 bus back to Broad and Boulevard.

On Wednesday morning, January 8, 2014, when the blue-and-white JRTC Route 6 bus kneeled at the curb of Ninth and Broad at ten-thirty ahead of the noon Opening Day swearing-in ceremony—with Dad waiting proudly for us on the brick sidewalk outside the Capitol gate in his best navy suit—none of the veteran politicians or lobbyists by the entrance looked surprised anymore when the front wheelchair ramp folded down.

Inside the House of Delegates Chamber at twelve o'clock noon, while Dad and I stood beside Katie's modified desk on the first-floor chamber carpet, the Clerk of the House called the freshman class forward to take the constitutional oath of office under Article II, Section 7 of the Constitution of Virginia.

Just as she had done when she was sworn onto the Virginia Beach City Council three years earlier, Katie didn't reach for a Bible. Instead, as I held our bound leather copy of the Constitution of the Commonwealth of Virginia steady across my lap tray between our two wheelchairs, Katie placed her left palm squarely on the gold-stamped cover of the Virginia Constitution, raised her right hand toward the Speaker's rostrum, and swore her oath in a clear, ringing voice:

"I do solemnly swear that I will support the Constitution of the United States, and the Constitution of the Commonwealth of Virginia, and that I will faithfully and impartially discharge all the duties incumbent upon me as a member of the House of Delegates, according to the best of my ability, so help me, God!"

Across the center aisle, three senior opposition delegates immediately craned their necks, whispering indignantly to a political blogger in the front press row that the new twenty-eight-year-old Democrat from Virginia Beach had placed her hand on a copy of the state Constitution instead of a Bible. By five o'clock that evening, conservative talk-radio hosts in Richmond and Tidewater were already having a field day over it—only for Katie to tell a Capitol corridor reporter with a calm, unbothered smile as we rolled out to catch our evening bus:

"The oath in Article II, Section 7 requires a lawmaker to swear before God to support and defend the Constitution of the United States and the Constitution of the Commonwealth of Virginia. My personal faith answers to God, and my left hand belongs on the exact Constitution I just promised God and the people of Virginia Beach I would defend."

Delegate Katie Prairie of Virginia Beach and her twin brother Nate had arrived in Richmond on the city bus, paid their regular fare, sworn their oath on the Constitution of Virginia, and made it crystal clear from Day One that the people's House belonged to everyone.

## House Bill 663

### Part 1: Line 18, Seated by Delegate Ronald Villa on the Floor, & A Question Across the Chamber

**Date:** 2016-02-04 at 11:35 America/New_York

#### Thursday, February 4, 2016, 11:35 AM — The Capitol Tunnel & Splitting Up at the Chamber Doors

"Look at Line 18 on Page 1 again, Nate," my twin sister Katie said, tapping her yellow highlighter against the two-page bill printout resting on her lap tray as our compact SwiftChairs rolled side by side down the long underground concourse tunnel connecting the General Assembly Building to the historic Virginia State Capitol. "'Anatomical sex means the physical condition of being male or female, which is determined by a person's anatomy.' Not a birth certificate, not a driver's license, and not how someone lives their daily life—strictly their physical anatomy. And then look at Line 54 on Page 2: 'Any person who willfully and knowingly violates this section shall be liable for a civil penalty of not more than fifty dollars,' enforced by a law enforcement officer's summons."

"I read it three times while I was setting up the daily calendar script this morning," I replied, steering my wheelchair smoothly beside hers past the subterranean security desk as the fifteen-minute warning bell for the noon floor session chimed from the bronze wall speakers overhead. "Delegate Cobb and Delegate Marsh put it straight onto today's Second Reading floor calendar. They think everybody on the Floor is going to treat it like a routine committee bill and wave it through before lunch."

It was thirty-year-old Katie's second two-year term representing the Eighty-Fifth House District back home in Kempsville and Virginia Beach, and she sat in a thirty-four-seat Democratic minority facing a sixty-six-seat Republican supermajority. Every day the General Assembly was in session during January and February, the two of us operated by the exact same synchronized routine we had followed since her freshman session in 2014: we woke up in our accessible one-bedroom winter-session apartment at the Broad & Boulevard Lofts on West Broad Street, went over every bill on the daily House calendar line by statutory line the exact same way we audited PHP code or Coastal Virginia Transit Authority bus schedules, and rode the eastbound JRTC Route 6 bus straight down Broad Street to Ninth and Broad before the noon gavel.

We rode the subterranean elevator up into the historic Capitol and rolled out onto the first-floor marble corridor just outside the tall mahogany double doors of the House Chamber. Lobbyists, school groups, and legislative aides were hurrying past the white marble columns as the ten-minute bell sounded.

At the velvet brass rope flanking the Chamber entrance—where the Sergeant-at-Arms staff checked member pins—Katie braked her SwiftChair and turned toward me.

Ever since our very first visit to the State Capitol back in February 2011—when Capitol Police had stopped us outside the third-floor public viewing gallery because the small historic elevator locked out during fire alarms and there was no safe way to evacuate a wheelchair down three flights of eighteenth-century stairs—House Leadership and the Sergeant-at-Arms had maintained a standing ADA floor pass for me (and any visiting constituent in a wheelchair plus one companion) to watch the daily session from a level wheelchair bay inside the first-floor House Chamber itself, right along the outer right aisle beside our fellow Virginia Beach lawmaker, Delegate Ronald Villa of the Twenty-First District.

"Are you set to plug into the floor feed over by Ron's desk?" Katie asked, reaching across our armrests to give my right hand our familiar twin squeeze.

"All set," I said, patting the padded laptop bag strapped across my lap tray where my MagnaBook Pro and my digital stream-capture rig were tucked alongside our daily legislative binder. "I'll have the chamber stream hooked into my laptop before the Speaker bangs the gavel so I can record and caption your remarks for the constituent archive."

"Wait until Delegate Cobb finishes his floor pitch on House Bill 663," Katie said, her blue eyes steady and focused as she tucked the highlighted two-page printout under her left wrist on the lap tray. "I'm going to ask the Speaker to let me put one direct operational question to him on the record."

"Give him hell, Delegate Prairie," I whispered with a small smile.

"See you at the center doors when we recess," Katie said warmly.

Releasing my hand, my twin sister eased her joystick forward, nodded to the doorkeeper holding open the left-hand mahogany door, and rolled her compact black power wheelchair across the green carpet toward her modified accessible desk at the end of the far left minority aisle.

I flashed my gold-stamped ADA floor pass to the Sergeant-at-Arms assistant at the right-hand doorway and rolled quietly inside to the designated wheelchair bay along the outer right aisle, twenty feet from the ground-level emergency exit.

Right beside my bay, seated at his mahogany desk bearing the brass nameplate *MR. VILLA — VIRGINIA BEACH (21ST DISTRICT)*, Delegate Ronald Villa looked up from his leather binder and gave me a warm, familiar nod.

"Morning, Nate," Delegate Villa whispered, reaching over to shake my hand just as he had done the very first day the Sergeant-at-Arms had brought Katie and me onto the Floor back in 2011. "How's the Route 6 bus running down Broad Street this morning?"

"Right on the ten-minute headway, Delegate Villa," I whispered back with a smile as I flipped down my lap tray, opened my laptop, plugged my headphones into the assistive-listening audio jack at the side rail, and launched my digital capture software hooked into the chamber's fixed legislative webcast camera.

Looking across the wide expanse of green carpet under the tall cream-and-gold coffered ceiling and crystal chandeliers of the House Chamber, I had a clear, floor-level view of the Speaker's high marble rostrum at the front of the room, the Clerk's well, the red-and-green electronic voting boards flanking the front wall, and Katie sitting upright in her compact SwiftChair at her accessible desk across the chamber on the minority aisle, her yellow-highlighted copy of House Bill 663 resting squarely in front of her desk microphone.

***

#### Thursday, February 4, 2016, 12:18 PM — Watching Across the House Floor as Katie Challenges the Patron

At twelve o'clock noon on the dot, the Speaker of the House rapped his wooden gavel against the rostrum block.

Up in the third-floor public gallery overhanging the rear wall—where two dozen college students from Virginia Dominion University and Commonwealth Polytechnic Institute, a handful of off-duty teachers, and several parents sat along the wooden benches—and down across the hundred mahogany desks on the Floor, everyone fell silent for the opening prayer, the Pledge of Allegiance, and the routine morning leaves of absence.

By 12:18 PM, the House had moved into the Second Reading calendar of bills reported out of committee. Half the Delegates on the floor were leaning back in their leather swivel chairs chatting with deskmates or flipping through binders while the Clerk read bill titles in a rapid, monotone cadence.

"House Bill 663, a bill to amend the Code of Virginia relating to single-gender public restrooms and facilities in buildings owned or leased by the Commonwealth, public institutions of higher education, and local school divisions; civil penalty. Patron, Delegate Cobb."

From my wheelchair bay beside the outer right aisle, I clicked the red Record button on my laptop capture window and checked my audio level meters. Right in front of me, I saw Delegate Ronald Villa—a pragmatic coastal Virginia Beach Republican who cared about bus shelters, military families, and Oceanfront tourism, not culture-war stunts—let out a quiet sigh and rub his forehead as the bill was called.

Three rows ahead on the majority side of the House Floor, Delegate Martin Cobb—with his chief co-patron, Delegate Robert Marsh, seated two desks over—stood up behind his mahogany desk, buttoned his navy suit jacket, and leaned into his silver gooseneck microphone.

"Mr. Speaker and members of the House," Delegate Cobb's voice echoed across the chamber and came cleanly through my floor headphones, "House Bill 663 is a simple, common-sense measure to protect personal privacy and public safety across the Commonwealth of Virginia. This legislation directs the Department of General Services, every public institution of higher education, and every local school board to ensure that every single-gender public restroom, locker room, and shower facility in any public building or school is used exclusively by individuals whose anatomical sex matches the designated gender on the door—backed by a fifty-dollar civil penalty enforceable by law enforcement summons. I hope it will be the pleasure of the House to engross the bill and advance it to its third reading."

Across the chamber at the end of the minority aisle, I saw Katie press the white request-to-speak button on her desk console and raise her right hand into the air.

Up on the high marble rostrum, the Speaker glanced down toward the left aisle.

"For what purpose does the gentlewoman from Virginia Beach, Delegate Prairie, rise?" the Speaker asked into his microphone.

Across the green carpet, Katie flipped the toggle switch at the base of her desk microphone so the green indicator ring illuminated, and lifted the two printed pages of House Bill 663 in her right hand.

"Mr. Speaker, to see if the gentleman from Spotsylvania will yield for a question on the bill," Katie's voice rang out crisp and clear across the chamber.

"Does the gentleman from Spotsylvania yield to the gentlewoman from Virginia Beach?" the Speaker asked.

Standing at his desk across the center aisle, Delegate Cobb gave a brief, stiff nod. "I yield for a question, Mr. Speaker."

"The gentleman yields," the Speaker said.

From my wheelchair on the far side of the floor, I watched my twin sister look straight across the House Chamber at the bill's patron.

"Thank you, Mr. Speaker," Katie said. "Look with me for a moment at the exact text printed in House Bill 663, Delegate Cobb. My brother and I write computer software for a living, and when you write code—or when you write a state law—definitions determine how an instruction actually executes in the real world. Unlike bills that reference a birth certificate or a state identification card, your Line 18 on Page 1 explicitly defines anatomical sex as 'the physical condition of being male or female, which is determined by a person's anatomy.' Not their driver's license. Not how they are dressed. Their physical anatomy. And then on Page 2, Line 54, you mandate that a law enforcement officer issue a fifty-dollar civil summons at the restroom door based on that physical anatomy."

Across the floor around me, the background murmur of side conversations suddenly died down. Delegates who had been thumbing through their afternoon calendars looked up from their desks.

"The language of the bill speaks for itself, Mr. Speaker," Delegate Cobb replied stiffly into his desk microphone. "Restrooms in state buildings, public colleges, and public schools will be restricted strictly by anatomical sex, and law enforcement officers are authorized to issue a fifty-dollar civil summons to anyone who enters a restroom that does not match their anatomical sex."

Over at her accessible desk, Katie released her joystick brake half an inch, rolling her compact SwiftChair flush against the mahogany desk edge as she held the two printed pages of House Bill 663 high in the air. Across the chamber, I could hear the raw, unmistakable fire rising in my sister's voice.

"Mr. Speaker, when the people of Kempsville and Virginia Beach first elected me to serve in this House of Delegates two years ago," Katie declared, her voice echoing off the marble walls of the Capitol chamber, "I had to fight the facilities bureaucracy in this very Capitol complex just to get an ADA-compliant restroom stall wide enough for my power wheelchair to turn around in and close the door in privacy! Every single day of my life since I was sixteen years old, I have known what it feels like to have strangers stare at my body and treat a basic human necessity like using a public restroom as a public spectacle. And now the gentleman comes onto the Floor of this House with House Bill 663 and tells the women and girls of Virginia that the state government is going to police 'anatomical sex' at the door of every ladies' room from Virginia Beach to Blacksburg for a fifty-dollar fine!"

Up in the third-floor public gallery above us, the college students and parents leaned forward over the brass balcony rail, holding their breath. Down on the House Floor, you could have heard a pin drop.

"So I ask the gentleman from Spotsylvania—right here on the Floor of the Virginia House of Delegates!" Katie demanded, shaking the printed pages of House Bill 663 across the aisle. "How, exactly, is a police officer or a state building inspector standing outside a restroom door at the Community College of Hampton Roads, or Virginia Dominion University, or right outside in this State Capitol corridor supposed to verify a citizen's 'physical anatomy' before writing your fifty-dollar ticket? Why on earth should I—or any woman or girl in the Commonwealth of Virginia—have to pull my pants down for a government official or a police officer to prove I have female parts just to use the ladies' room?!"

An audible gasp swept across the chamber, followed half a second later by a sudden burst of applause from the visitors up in the third-floor gallery benches and several sharp "Hear, hear!" calls from the minority desks down on the Floor. Right beside my wheelchair on the Republican side of the floor, Delegate Ronald Villa winced, shook his head at Cobb's bill, and exchanged an unmistakable *she's got him dead to rights* look with his seatmate.

*Bam! Bam!*

"The House will be in order!" the Speaker barked from the rostrum, pounding his wooden gavel twice as he looked up toward the third-floor balcony. "The guests in the public gallery are reminded that demonstrations of approval or disapproval are strictly prohibited by the rules of the House!"

Out on the majority aisle of the House Floor, Delegate Martin Cobb stood completely frozen behind his desk microphone for four full seconds, his face turning beet-red under the chamber chandeliers as he looked from Katie's power wheelchair to the uneasy, downward stares of his own party colleagues around him.

"I—Mr. Speaker, the bill does not—nobody is suggesting—" Delegate Cobb stammered into his microphone, shuffling his folder on his desk without answering the question.

"Mr. Speaker, that is literally what the words printed on Page 1 and Page 2 of House Bill 663 require!" Katie replied from her SwiftChair, her voice steady, clear, and unflinching across the silent chamber. "Transgender women are women, transgender men are men, and every citizen in this Commonwealth deserves basic human dignity and privacy without politicians stationing bathroom police at the door to inspect people's anatomy. No woman or girl in Virginia should ever have to pull her pants down to prove she has female parts just to use a public restroom!"

At the front row of the center aisle, the House Majority Leader quickly stood up at his desk, exchanging a tense look with the Speaker before glancing back at a flustered Delegate Cobb.

"Mr. Speaker," the Majority Leader said briskly into his microphone, "I move that House Bill 663 be passed by for the day."

"Without objection, House Bill 663 is passed by for the day," the Speaker announced immediately, rapping his gavel once and hurrying straight ahead. "The Clerk will read the next bill on the calendar."

***

#### Thursday, February 4, 2016, 12:35 PM — Archiving the 95-Second Clip & Rolling Back Through the Tunnel

In my wheelchair bay beside Delegate Villa's desk, I clicked the Stop button on my capture window and exhaled a quiet breath.

Delegate Villa leaned slightly toward my armrest and whispered out of the corner of his mouth, "Your sister just drove a freight truck straight through the middle of that bill, Nate."

I gave him a quiet grin and looked down at the timeline bar on my laptop screen while the Clerk moved on to a routine highway-naming bill. Checking the timestamp on the video file I had just recorded from the chamber feed, I saw that the entire floor exchange between Katie and Delegate Cobb ran ninety-five seconds.

Just like I did whenever Katie spoke on a bill on the House Floor, I opened Quantum Code right there on my lap tray, typed out a verbatim, frame-accurate WebVTT closed-caption file for the ninety-five-second video, burned high-contrast open captions along the bottom of the frame so constituents watching on mute could read every word, and uploaded the clip to Katie's legislative website archive, StreamTube, and The Grid at 12:35 PM with a standard archival heading:

> Delegate Katie Prairie (D-Virginia Beach) Questions HB 663 Patron on the House Floor Regarding "Anatomical Sex" Restroom Enforcement (Feb. 4, 2016)

Twenty-five minutes later, at one o'clock, the House adjourned its Thursday floor session so members could grab lunch before afternoon committee work.

I unplugged my audio cable from the side rail, packed my laptop and capture rig back into my bag, shook Delegate Villa's hand, and rolled out the right-hand chamber doorway into the first-floor marble corridor just as Katie came rolling out the left-hand doorway in her SwiftChair.

"Hey," I said, rolling right up beside her and reaching out to take her left hand. "The ninety-five-second floor clip is captioned and archived on your constituent page. Delegate Cobb didn't have a single word to say when you asked him how a police officer is supposed to verify Line 18 at the door—and even Ron Villa over on my aisle said you drove a truck straight through the middle of that bill."

"Good," Katie sighed, squeezing my fingers as we turned our wheelchairs side by side toward the elevator down to the underground Capitol tunnel. "Even if the majority brings it right back up on Monday and tries to ram it through on a party-line vote, at least we put the truth about that bill squarely on the permanent House record. Come on—let's go grab a sandwich in the basement cafeteria before our two o'clock constituent meetings."

### Part 2: Thursday Evening at Six, Friday on Capitol Square, & Sunday Morning on Meet the Nation

**Date:** 2016-02-04 at 18:00 America/New_York

#### Thursday, February 4, 2016, 6:00 PM — The Evening News in Richmond and Norfolk

"Hand me the pepper shaker, Nate," Katie said at six o'clock that Thursday evening, setting two warm plates of baked chicken and rice down on the kitchen table of our accessible one-bedroom winter-session apartment at the Broad & Boulevard Lofts on West Broad Street after we had ridden the 5:35 PM westbound JRTC Route 6 bus home from Ninth and Broad. "And turn on the television so we can see the weather forecast for the weekend."

We had spent the entire afternoon from two until five-thirty back in Katie's fifth-floor office in the General Assembly Building, meeting with special-education parents from Virginia Beach and reviewing coastal flooding amendments with our legislative aide, with both of our laptops closed during the meetings.

Picking up the remote control from the corner of the table, I clicked on the wall-mounted television—tuned first to the Richmond local six-o'clock news broadcast, with my laptop open beside my water glass streaming our hometown six-o'clock broadcast from Norfolk so we could keep up with Hampton Roads news while we were away in Richmond.

I barely had time to set the remote down before both my sister and I froze with our forks halfway to our mouths.

On the television screen on the wall—and simultaneously on the Norfolk local news stream playing on my laptop—was the exact ninety-five-second video clip I had captured from my wheelchair bay on the House Floor at lunchtime.

*"—and our top story from the State Capitol tonight,"* the Richmond evening anchor announced over the footage of Katie sitting upright in her compact SwiftChair on the House Floor holding up the two printed pages of House Bill 663, *"a fiery floor exchange in the House of Delegates this afternoon over House Bill 663, the controversial bathroom bill restricting public restrooms by 'anatomical sex,' after Virginia Beach Delegate Katie Prairie brought the House Chamber to a standstill with a single question for the bill's patron."*

Before our eyes, both the Richmond station and the Norfolk station aired the floor clip in full—every word of Katie asking Delegate Martin Cobb why she, or any woman or girl in the Commonwealth of Virginia, should have to pull her pants down for a government official or a police officer to prove she had female parts just to use the ladies' room, followed by the four seconds of Delegate Cobb standing frozen and beet-red behind his desk microphone before the Majority Leader hurriedly passed the bill by for the day.

Katie stared at the television screen, her blue eyes wide. "Nate... they're playing the whole floor clip on the six-o'clock news. In Richmond and back home in Norfolk."

I reached over and woke my laptop browser window, refreshing the server dashboard for Katie's legislative archive and The Grid.

My jaw dropped.

"Katie, look at the traffic logs," I said, turning the laptop screen toward her wheelchair. "The two Capitol beat reporters from the Richmond daily paper and the Norfolk paper embedded your captioned clip on their blogs at two-thirty this afternoon while we were in constituent meetings. Because the open captions were burned right into the video frame, thousands of office workers and college students at Virginia Dominion University, CCHR, and Commonwealth Polytechnic Institute watched it on their phones during the afternoon and shared it across the state. That's how the assignment desks at the Richmond and Norfolk TV stations grabbed it for the five-o'clock and six-o'clock broadcasts!"

Before Katie could even answer, both of our cell phones on the kitchen counter started vibrating nonstop, one call and email notification after another chiming across the room.

I rolled over to the counter, picked up both phones, and looked at the caller IDs and the shared press inbox.

"It's the Capitol news producers for all three local stations in Richmond and the Hampton Roads bureaus in Norfolk," I told Katie, reading the timestamps scrolling down the screen. "Every single one of them is asking if you'll do a live on-camera interview outside the Capitol tomorrow morning."

***

#### Friday, February 5, 2016, 11:00 AM to Saturday Afternoon — Friday's Local News Interviews & The Call from Washington

On Friday morning, February 5, a row of local television microwave trucks from Richmond and Norfolk was parked bumper-to-bumper along the brick curb of Capitol Square outside the General Assembly Building.

Rolling out onto the sunlit brick walkway beside me in the crisp winter air at eleven o'clock, thirty-year-old Katie parked her compact SwiftChair in front of the cluster of local station microphones while I stood by two feet to her left with our legislative binder on my lap tray.

One after another, Katie gave back-to-back live interviews to the Richmond and Norfolk television crews. She didn't use canned partisan talking points or sound like a rehearsed career politician; she held up the exact same yellow-highlighted two-page printout of House Bill 663 she had carried onto the House Floor on Thursday and explained in plain, steady language why policing "anatomical sex" at the door of public restrooms for a fifty-dollar fine was unworkable, invasive, and an insult to every woman, every disabled citizen, and every transgender Virginian in the Commonwealth.

"When you write a law that carries a fifty-dollar police summons based on 'a person's physical anatomy,' you have to answer honestly how that law gets enforced at a public restroom door," Katie told the Norfolk anchor live on the Friday noon broadcast. "Nobody in Virginia sent us to Richmond to turn police officers and school principals into bathroom inspectors."

That Friday afternoon was when the story crossed the Potomac River and exploded coast-to-coast.

The national network news desks at GBS, Global News Network, and Beacon News pulled the Friday lunchtime broadcast packages directly from their Richmond and Norfolk local affiliates—pairing Thursday's ninety-five-second House Floor clip with Katie's poised Friday interviews on the Capitol lawn. By Friday evening at 6:30 PM, the national network evening broadcasts were airing the clip from New York to Los Angeles; by 11:35 PM Friday night, all three national late-night talk-show hosts were roasting Virginia's fifty-dollar "anatomical inspection" summons in their opening monologues; and by Saturday morning, my server analytics counter showed the captioned floor clip passing three million views as the Virginia Chamber of Commerce and the Virginia Hospitality and Travel Association began calling House leadership in a panic that House Bill 663 was threatening conventions and tourism jobs across the state.

Then, at 2:15 PM on Saturday afternoon, while Katie and I were sitting side by side at our apartment dining table answering constituent emails from Virginia Beach, my cell phone rang with a Washington, D.C. area code.

"Hello, Delegate Katie Prairie's office, this is Nathaniel Prairie speaking," I answered.

*"Hi, Nathaniel—this is the senior booking producer for Meet the Nation at GBS News in Washington,"* the voice on the line said urgently. *"Every political desk in the country is talking about your sister's floor speech on House Bill 663. We want to book Delegate Prairie live from our Richmond affiliate studio tomorrow morning at nine o'clock as our lead guest at the top of the broadcast. Can the two of you be at the studio by 8:35 AM?"*

I muted the phone for two seconds and looked across the table at my twin sister.

"Katie," I whispered, "it's GBS News in Washington. They want you live on Meet the Nation tomorrow morning."

***

#### Sunday, February 7, 2016, 9:00 AM — "If It's Sunday, It's Meet the Nation" & Laying the Bill on the Table

At 8:35 AM on Sunday morning, Katie and I rolled our compact SwiftChairs up the studio ramp of the local GBS television affiliate in Richmond.

Inside the quiet, darkened broadcast studio, the floor crew had cleared away the standard swivel anchor chair from the center of the remote-interview set so Katie could roll her own SwiftChair right up to the tape mark in front of the illuminated Virginia State Capitol backdrop. When a well-meaning stage manager asked if the camera operator should crop the shot tight around Katie's shoulders so national viewers wouldn't see her wheelchair controls, Katie shook her head politely and rested her left hand squarely on her joystick armrest.

"Keep the frame wide enough to show my chair, please," Katie told him warmly. "A lot of the people watching across the country this morning have never seen a legislator in a power wheelchair on a Sunday morning show, and I don't hide who I am."

Parked four feet to the left of Camera One beside the studio floor monitor with my laptop resting on my lap tray, I watched the red digital countdown clock tick down to 9:00 AM Eastern as the iconic brass orchestra fanfare of Meet the Nation echoed through the studio earpieces.

On the monitor, the veteran Washington moderator looked straight into the main camera from the network desk in D.C.

"Good morning," the moderator announced across millions of living rooms from coast to coast. "If it's Sunday, it's Meet the Nation. Later this hour, we will hear from the presidential campaigns ahead of Tuesday's primary—but we begin this Sunday morning with a ninety-five-second moment from the floor of the Virginia House of Delegates in Richmond that has been watched more than four million times across the country in the last seventy-two hours, igniting a national debate over so-called 'bathroom bills,' personal privacy, and civil rights."

On the national network broadcast, Meet the Nation rolled the exact ninety-five-second video clip I had recorded and captioned from my wheelchair bay on the House Floor on Thursday afternoon—showing Katie down on the House Floor in her power wheelchair holding up the printed pages of House Bill 663 and demanding of Delegate Martin Cobb why she, or any woman or girl in the Commonwealth of Virginia, should have to pull her pants down for a government official or a police officer to prove she had female parts just to use the ladies' room.

When the clip cut back to the live split-screen studio feed, the lower-third graphic flashed across television screens nationwide:

> LIVE FROM RICHMOND, VA<br>
> DELEGATE KATIE PRAIRIE (D-VIRGINIA BEACH)<br>
> VIRGINIA HOUSE OF DELEGATES — 85TH DISTRICT

"And joining us live this morning from Richmond is the lawmaker in that video—State Delegate Katie Prairie, Democrat of Virginia Beach," the moderator said. "Delegate Prairie, welcome to Meet the Nation."

"Good morning, and thank you for having me," Katie said, her posture upright, calm, and completely natural in her SwiftChair under the studio lights.

"Delegate Prairie, on Thursday afternoon you rose at your desk on the floor of the Virginia House of Delegates to ask a single question about House Bill 663, and by Friday evening your remarks had made national news," the moderator said. "The sponsors of the bill argue they are simply trying to protect privacy in public restrooms and schools. Why did you zero in so forcefully on Line 18's definition of 'anatomical sex'?"

"Because when you write laws that govern eight million people and carry a fifty-dollar law-enforcement summons, words matter," Katie answered smoothly, looking straight into the camera lens without a single note in front of her. "My twin brother Nate and I are software developers by trade, and when you write code—or when you write a state statute—you have to trace how an instruction actually executes in the real world. House Bill 663 defines 'anatomical sex' on Page 1 not by an identification card or how someone lives their daily life, but strictly as 'the physical condition of being male or female, which is determined by a person's anatomy,' enforced by a fifty-dollar police summons at the door of every state building, public college, and public school restroom in Virginia."

Katie leaned slightly forward in her chair, her voice ringing with the exact same moral clarity that had hushed the House Chamber on Thursday afternoon.

"As someone who has used a power wheelchair full-time since I was sixteen years old—and who had to fight just to get an ADA-accessible bathroom stall inside our own State Capitol complex—I know firsthand that the last thing any citizen needs at a restroom door is government surveillance," Katie continued on live national television.
